## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
* Accreditations are subject to review and may be withdrawn if the content provided in the funding proposal does not reflect the approved project or programme.
* The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 50. Pages exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual approval time frame.
* The recommended font is Arial, size 11.
* Upon submission of the Funding Proposal, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to the confidentiality requirements set out in the IDP. A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Climate vulnerability context: Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) is one of the most vulnerable regions to the impacts of climate variability and change. This is due to its socio-economic context* and not already high exposed population to natural hazards such as hurricanes, earthquakes, tsunamis, droughts, floods, among others. According to a recent United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNISDR) report, LAC countries are the most vulnerable in terms of disaster risk exposure (high vulnerability index). Climate change medium-term impacts will include desertification, rapid glacier retreat, longer periods of hotter weather and more frequent heatwaves, increased frequency of extreme precipitation events, and an increase in the intensity and frequency of tropical cyclones. Between 1996 and 2017 indicate that 53% of global economic losses from natural disasters were due to climate-related hazards. In this context, it is clear that for any region, disaster risk linked to climate change will be an important element to be considered in the planning and design of development projects.

*As of March 21st 2022, only Country programmes of Uruguay and Jamaica are available on GCF website. Both include reference to this FP.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

## Greenhouse gases (GHG) emissions context: Transport contributes to almost one-quarter of the current global energy-related GHG emissions and is growing faster than any other energy end-use sector. The transport sector is also responsible for today’s largest share of CO2 emissions, projected to rise by 50% by the year 2050 unless major action is undertaken. Between 2000 and 2018 the total GHG emissions of the participating countries grew or remained stable in all but one country.

### The average carbon intensity of electricity generated by Program Countries is 2,433 kgCO2e/kWh which allows electric vehicles (EVs) to reduce GHG emissions on a well-to-wheel (WTW) basis by around 80% compared to fossil fuels. However, the benefits are not evenly distributed across all regions and countries within the region, with some being larger.

### Oceans are suffocated by salt and acid. Air pollution is a major problem in most Latin American cities with levels affecting human health. The population of major cities of participating countries is exposed to levels of air pollutants that significantly surpass WHO guideline levels. The World Bank estimates that annually 4.2 million deaths result due to excessive exposure to PM10, PM2.5 and NOx emissions, which are the main components of particulate matter and a major source of air pollution.

### Barriers: Fossil vehicles will continue to dominate the landscape as electric (EVs) remain even with decreasing prices, financially and from the convenience point of view not yet sufficiently attractive to be commercially viable. Public Transport (PT) modes should also be expected to continue to decline. The result of this Business as Usual (BAU) scenario is that CO2 emissions will continue to rise in the region, with the transport sector remaining at very high levels as the impact of new vehicle emission standards is offset by increased vehicle numbers and longer vehicle lifetimes.

### Transformational change is required: Limiting the global temperature increase to below 2 degrees Celsius requires changing this transport emissions trajectory, which includes core components the development of an integrated electric mobility system (EMS) that will be a key enabler for sustainable urban development. The EMS fosters public and non-motorized transport (NMT). The Paris Declaration on Electric-Mobility calls for the deployment of 10 million EVs by 2025, which is equivalent to 3% of global sales in 2020. The International Energy Agency (IEA) modeling indicates that EVs need to represent 35% of global sales in 2030; EV deployment will be a key enabler for sustainable urban development and the achievement of SDGs. However, achieving this target requires policy changes and corrective market interventions. Additionally, and in connection to the climate change mitigation goals, it is important that the transport sector also contributes to the creation of green jobs which appear to effectively manage sustainable urban development. This will be further aggravated by the effects of forced migration due to climate change. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) can play a key role in supporting LAC countries in LAC to build sustainable climate-resilient and carbon-neutral development pathways; infrastructure for electrified urban transport systems, including charging stations, is one such example.

### The main motivating factors to foster e-mobility are: (i) to reduce GHG emissions; (ii) improve air quality and reduce health problems associated with air pollution; (iii) reduce dependency on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks; (iv) increase the energy security of countries by reducing reliance on imports for electricity generation creation; (v) increase climate resilience of urban and grid infrastructure; and (vi) assess option of the hydrogen economy for future transport. The GCF can play a key role in supporting LAC countries in achieving these objectives as an important measure to achieve climate targets. Many countries have also initiated policies to foster the deployment of EVs.

### Barriers to massive EV deployment are primarily: (i) limited commercial viability of EV investments related to high purchase and operating costs; (ii) lack of charging infrastructure; (iii) low consumer awareness and acceptance at the equal level as fossil units; (iv) business models and policies which are not conducive to a mass EV deployment; (v) lack of evidence on how climate change will impact the quantity and frequency of power outages and extreme weather-related events that can happen in the future and impact the IEUITS, and limited experience with cost-effective measures

The E-Mobility Program links (a) EV deployment with sustainable low-carbon urban development and increased climate resilience, (b) EV deployment to support the transition of transport systems towards a low-carbon economy, (c) Sustainable urban development includes measures to strengthen and improve the public transport (PT) sector, NMT, pedestrian and cycling infrastructure, as well as other aspects such as land use planning, building design, etc., responding to observed and anticipated climate-related risks associated to more intense precipitation and flooding, heat waves, extreme cold, sea level rise, hurricanes and strong winds. The results of the screening will determine the need for prioritization and design of specific adaptation measures to reduce climate vulnerability and risk on the IEUDTs to these hazards. These measures may include: (i) EV deployment in urban transport infrastructure, including public use (preferable trucks, vessels or mobile machinery) and include piloting the usage of hydrogen for energy storage and/or power generation; (ii) integration of renewable energy sources into the grid and the development of linking EV deployment with renewable energy deployment whilst increasing the grid resilience with a focus on Small Business Enterprises (SBEs); (iii) capacity building activities aimed at improving existing planning, design and decision-making processes to integrate climate change adaptation measures in urban transport infrastructure; (iv) institutional capacity-building activities to sustainably manage climate-related risks associated to intense precipitation and flooding, extreme heat and more intense and frequent tropical cyclones and strong winds (See Annex 2 for detailed details). Accelerated EV deployment is achieved by having a long-term outcome on an EV conducive ecosystem which results in investors pursuing EVs in a commercial base. The Program fosters a structural shift towards low-carbon, climate-resilient urban transport infrastructure through the following: (i) institutional capacity-building activities towards EV deployment can be successfully initiated or reduced through Program interventions. The Program provides support to the IEUDTs to develop and implement an integrated approach for sustainable urban development and NMT, including land use planning, building design, etc., responding to observed and anticipated climate-related risks associated with more intense precipitation and flooding, extreme heat and more intense and frequent tropical cyclones and strong winds (See Annex 2 for detailed details). A

investments in places due to an e-mobility conducive policy environment, innovative business models, lower performance and income risks and the entrance of new market players. The Program acts basically as a market stimulator for the adoption of electric vehicles and other low-emission technologies at a much larger scale than under a BAU scenario. The Program creates added value through knowledge management, interchange of experience, development of the skills and competences needed in the workforce and through creating a critical mass of actors in the e-mobility sector.

The expected GCF fund-level impacts are (i) a total investment of 450 MUSD of which the GCF 200 MUSD (leverage ratio: 1.75), (ii) GHG emission reductions with a GCF cost of intervention of 36 USD/CO2eq, (iii) 548 IPM and 5,211 INQs reduced economic losses due to reduced emissions valued at 450 MUSD of which 74 MUSD due to reduced local air pollution plus an additional 1.5 million USD in avoided health costs, (iv) the creation of jobs for more than 30% of the entire population of Program countries.

The initial Sub-Project pipeline of the Program for implementation within the first 2-3 years includes individual projects from Barbados, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay.

# B. PROJECT PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Project Title (Max: 100 words, approximately 8 pages)

### CLIMATE CHANGE PROBLEM AND THE RELEVANCE OF ELECTRIC MOBILITY

Transport contributes almost one-quarter of the current global energy-related GHG emissions and is growing faster than any other energy end-use sector. Latin America already accounts for 10% of global GHG emissions as of today. Transport in this region is dominated by road transport, which accounts for approximately 95% of total transport emissions.

Regarding urban development, LAC is among the most urbanized regions in the world with over 80% of its population living in cities. It has coastline extends over 27,000 km and it is where many of the largest cities are located. More than 150 million people live within 30 meters of sea level, making them vulnerable to coastal flooding. A large number of these areas are being affected by coastal hazards among them, the low-lying Caribbean states, which are especially vulnerable (World Bank, 2018). The region is also highly exposed to extreme weather events such as hurricanes and cyclones.

Infrastructure is expected to be an important element of their vitality and economic growth. For that reason, as climate change-related risks increase, it becomes increasingly important for infrastructure planning, design, construction, and operation so that climate-related risks are managed. According to UNDRR, risk in concentrating in the region's fastest-growing urban areas, where 80% of its population lives. The region is also highly exposed to extreme weather events such as hurricanes and cyclones.

Regarding urban development, LAC is among the most urbanized regions in the world with over 80% of its population living in cities. It has coastline extends over 27,000 km and it is where many of the largest cities are located. More than 150 million people live within 30 meters of sea level, making them vulnerable to coastal flooding. A large number of these areas are being affected by coastal hazards among them, the low-lying Caribbean states, which are especially vulnerable (World Bank, 2018). The region is also highly exposed to extreme weather events such as hurricanes and cyclones.

According to UNDRR, risk in concentrating in the region's fastest-growing urban areas, where 80% of its population lives.

The region is also highly exposed to extreme weather events such as hurricanes and cyclones. The region has experienced a large number of high-intensity hydrometeorological events that occurred during 2020 and that were associated with both climate change and natural disasters. In the Caribbean, climate-related infrastructure has been identified as a priority in almost every single NDC within the LAC region.

Hydrometeorological events such as floods, storms, droughts and heatwaves account for 95% of all disasters that have affected Latin America since 1970 (UNDRR, 2018). The region is highly exposed to extreme weather events. For the LAC region to be exacerbated by climate change include: (i) intense precipitation and flooding; at the end of the rainy season, heavy rainfall can cause flash floods in urban areas; (ii) sea level rise and coastal erosion in Central and South America; (iii) temperature increase, drought and extreme heat; major heatwaves affected the region, especially in 2019. In addition to these climate change-related risks, the region is also highly exposed to hurricanes in the Caribbean, rising at a slightly higher rate than the global average (3.3mm/year) and (iv) more destructive hurricanes: for example category 4 hurricanes Eta and Iota affected over 8 million people in Central America in 2020.

# NEED TO ACT ON E-MOBILITY SOLUTIONS TO CLIMATE CHANGE ASSOCIATED RISKS

Many cities in LAC are betting on electrification and expect it to be an important element of their climate-resilient carbon-neutral development pathways. Although the region contributes only to 0.4% of global CO2 emissions, its cities are among those most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The region has been particularly affected by climate-related disasters between 2000 and 2019. Furthermore, 56% of losses suffered by firms in LAC after natural disasters were related to power outages (World Bank Group, 2018). In addition, the World Bank (WB, 2019). Therefore, cities in the region need to act and prepare future electrified urban transport for the challenges ahead. The electrification of urban transport is a key element of climate-resilient development pathways, with benefits such as improved air quality, reduced greenhouse gas emissions, and increased energy access, which opens up from improved accessibility and stronger economic growth to social inclusion.

In connection to the above, electrifiable assets such as V2G for example, may also contribute to strengthening the resilience of electricity systems in LAC. This is particularly true in countries like Mexico, Peru, and Chile. Such is the case of Costa Rica where Central America and the Caribbean which have seen an increase in the number and intensity of tropical cyclones in recent years.

The IIE has led research on the vulnerability of hydropower to climate change in Latin America, specifically in Central America1 and the Andean region2 being hydropower the main source of electricity in the region with almost 50% contribution. The impact of climate change is expected to vary significantly across countries within the region depending on the country analyzed, with average electricity supply costs increasing by up to 7% in some cases. In the case of Central America, the study found that the vulnerability of hydropower systems to climate change will be significantly affecting future hydroelectric production; while an increase in average precipitation and seasonality is expected for most countries, this will not necessarily translate into increased electricity generation capacity due to future impacts. It is foreseen that power grids in countries with high shares of hydropower will become less flexible to cope with the variability of water resources. In addition, the study found that natural gas plants may become the preferred choice when it comes to capacity expansion for electricity generation in Latin America due to their flexibility and ability to respond quickly to changes in demand. However, this would require significant network operators to maintain system reliability and adequacy. Energy storage possibilities and auxiliary services provided by V2G could help to mitigate some of these climate change induced impacts on the power grid and can be considered as a key element for future electrified urban transport systems. These findings will be used in deemed necessary under DBS Climate and Risk Assessment Methodology (DCRA).

In order to address the challenges that electrification investments face in the region in light of climate change, the proposed actions are divided into two main categories: the first is related to strengthening the resilience of the associated risks of the programs of financed infrastructure (included in Components 1 to 3). For this purpose, IIE’s DCRA methodology will be used to identify and assess the potential impacts of climate change on electrification measures. The second aspect is related to building/strengthening the adaptive capacity of stakeholders to better manage the risks associated with climate change. This includes the development of adaptation strategies to mitigate created by climate threats (this includes also creating awareness of climate hazards and risk-reduction processes) and the implementation of these strategies in the context of electrification projects. These include the system’s operation, maintenance and the improvement of planning and design methodologies and processes to duly incorporate climate change considerations into infrastructure development.

The IIE will work with the relevant stakeholders to identify and assess the potential impacts of climate change on electrification measures, synthesize, and apply new knowledge on climate risk management in the design of sectoral regulations/norms for the implementation of electrification projects. This will be done through the application of the DCRA methodology in the context of the IIE’s Sustainable Urban Transport (SUT) Mobility Program and is aimed at building climate-resilience of the SUTS which encompasses e-mobility infrastructure, urban infrastructure (green and grey), and grid infrastructure (see Figure 1).

### Context

Latin America has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean transport and e-mobility. The region has one of the highest shares of renewable energy sources in the world (high hydroelectric production and growing deployment of wind and solar power plants). Therefore, the adoption of an e-mobility strategy would allow the sector to move towards a decarbonization pathway that is consistent with the carbon intensity of electricity generated by Program countries. With these grid factors, EVs can reduce GHG emissions on average by 70-80% compared to fossil fuels.

### Proposals that currently have above-average carbon grid factors have all adopted ambitious renewable energy (RE) targets for the near future with the goal of reducing dramatically GHG emissions from electricity production and increasing RE penetration in their power systems.

* Barbados is committed to reduce 22% of electricity consumption compared to BAU by 2029, and has been 20% and 29% of its fuel supply replaced by renewables since 2015.
* Chile has a target of 30% for solar, wind, and biomass energy sources, as well as biofuels for the transportation sector and natural gas as a bridge fuel to reduce the use of heavy fuels. In the past two years a government decree was issued that requires all new buildings to be equipped with photovoltaic panels.
* The IADB Energy Fund has been supporting projects in renewable energy and energy efficiency through the IDB led Energy Smart Fund has allowed annual savings of approximately 10% of electricity consumption, while also reducing CO2 emissions by 35%. In addition to the iADB loan operation, the Public Sector Smart Energy Program (BAU:1.1025) has installed approximately 4.3 MW of solar capacity and 6.7 MW of wind capacity in public buildings.
* The Chilean government is also supporting a project under the IDB’s Climate Change Fund that aims to increase RE penetration by 8% through the installation of solar panels on public buildings, as well as the development of an energy efficiency program for public buildings.

### Chile has a target of 60% RE generation by 2030** compared to 45% in 2019**.

* The government has set a target of 25% of BAU by 2028** compared to 10% in 2019** and 30% by 2030 as part of the RELAC initiative.
* Chile has seen a larger shift in 2016 and 60% by 2017, compared to 11% in 2015**. Juxtaposing this rapid progress in RE penetration is the fact that Chile has an overall increase of 34% for oil for electricity generation towards LNG and increasing the penetration of variable renewable sources, namely wind and solar PV.
* On the demand side, there are several factors driving the growth of RE in Chile: a large number of electric vehicles (EVs) on the road, as well as one of the highest electricity tariffs, which together represent an important opportunity for intervention with a combination of policy measures and financial instruments. The government has recently launched a new energy law, underfunded and led by the IDB, is a demonstrative step in this direction for the public sector, aimed at reducing costs and increasing RE penetration.
* Chile’s National Energy Plan (NEP) 2017-2035 includes a target of 48% RE generation by 2035. The most recent draft Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) document indicates an increasing RE penetration to 35% of generated electricity in the country.

### Latin America is also well known for innovative bus business models in Colombia and Chile (see Inbox).

#### Inbox: Innovative Business Models for e-Bus Promotion

The case of Chile is especially interesting due to the introduction of new business models separating bus ownership from operations. The government has been encouraging private companies to invest in electric buses to operate, in order to boost their core business (energy sales) and the installation of charging infrastructure.
* Electric bus operators are responsible for purchasing and maintaining the vehicles as well as for reducing fuel costs in vehicles and also ensuring payments from public operators. In subsequent tenders, the government has been encouraging private companies to invest in electric buses and charging stations, and others being 20% of bus operators with investment firms. Electric utilities are currently no longer engaged in new investments in electric buses.
* The government is now considering a new model where electric buses would be a part of their core business (e.g., AMP Capital and Nitol Capital have entered as investors). The government could

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 10

## Colombia

Large Colombian cities already run successfully since many BRT systems with large private companies working as operators and a public system manager paying companies based on distance driven cum service delivery target (e.g., Bogota). The first concession contract for the operation of a BRT system in Colombia was awarded to buses in Bogota (2018) was based on a tender which only allowed for the provision of e-buses. A subsequent second tender was launched in 2020, and this time, the operator was allowed to use conventional diesel buses as well as e-buses are not yet commercially competitive (end 2019). The third tender (2020) included significant additional points for the provision of electric buses. As a result, the city has now committed to procuring only electric buses and also resulted in 16th per km higher cost of buses than which is being paid by the municipality. The experience of Bogota shows that the transition from conventional diesel buses to e-buses can be challenging for both the operator and concessionaires as sufficient charging infrastructure needs to be put in place, and the operator if concession contracts are sufficiently attractive and if e-buses receive benefits compared to fossil units; until this happens, it is unlikely that cities will make a significant investment in electric buses. In the case of Bogota, they are willing to pay for these incremental costs due to e-buses improving air quality and reducing GHG emissions.

The e-Mobility Program will work frequently in smaller and medium sized cities where such business models might be less applicable due to lower levels of planning, and regulation as well as much smaller volumes and sizes of transport infrastructure. However, the experience from Bogota shows that the transition towards electric buses is possible and will therefore develop business models apt to their size, structure and conditions.

The COVID-19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, from the health and economic perspectives, but it also is opening new opportunities for an accelerated sustainable transformation in the region. The World Bank (2020) estimates that the pandemic will reduce GDP growth by 3% to -5%. A study shows that fostering of electric mobility can have a significant positive job impact primarily due to the induced impact of electric vehicles on the manufacturing sector, which is expected to be more resilient than other sectors. The income elasticity which tend to be labor intensive services-good (LTS-G). Equally a recent study at the IDB and the International Energy Agency (IEA) shows that Latin America has the potential to become one of the largest EV regions by 2030 (IDB, 2020). Thus, accelerated transformation in energy and transport, among other sectors, make electric vehicles an important part of the region’s response to the pandemic. The region is rich in renewable natural resource resources. Some countries in LAC are already responding to these challenges and opportunities by adopting policies that promote the use of electric vehicles. This is a very positive development as it offers great potential for the creation of new jobs, it will also negatively affect some high-emitting sectors and also may exist occasions in terms of task composition and skills requirements. In order to ensure Just Transition for all stakeholders, it is important to consider the social dimension of the industrial creation in new green activities and in addressing the social challenges in shifting to more sustainable models.

## Chile

| Area | Population | GDP/capita | Vehicle Fleet | Diesel price | Electricity price |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 756,950 km² | 18.4 million | $13,900 USD | 2.9 million | 0.96 USD/L | 0.19 USD/kWh |

At the end of 2017 Chile published its National Electrification Strategy, a document realized jointly by the Ministry of Energy, the Ministry of Transport and Telecommunications and the Ministry of the Environment (MINET, MITT, MMA). The strategy is based on the National Decarbonization Plan (NDC) for the energy sector and the Climate Change Strategy (MINEA). The updated NDC Chile has as target that the country will be carbon neutral by 2050; and in order to achieve this goal, it aims to reduce GHG emissions by: 10% of its total emissions by 2030. National public transport buses by 2040 (60% of light vehicles in stock), national fleet by 2030 (95% electric vehicles), and all new cars sold by 2030 (80% of them electric). Chile has thus policies and incentives to promote different categories of electric vehicles including buses and taxis and has as of end-2017 more than 600+ a buses operating primarily in Santiago de Chile.

Colombia has an automotive industry dedicated mainly to vehicle assembly, auto parts production and motorcycle assembly. The NDC contemplates for the transportation sector amongst others to achieve 600,000 registered electric light vehicles by 2030 (5% of total stock), 100,000 registered electric heavy-duty vehicles by 2030 (5% of total stock) and 400,000 registered electric buses by 2030 (60% of the national fleet). Colombia has also launched a National Strategy for Electric Mobility, which aims to promote the electrification of the transportation sector. Bogotá has put in place several policies and incentives to promote the use of electric vehicles including the creation of the National Commission for Electric Mobility, the establishment of public charging infrastructure as well as important tax incentives for private EVs.

Jamaica's geographical location and biophysical landscape make it vulnerable to climate change impacts, especially for the energy sector. The country has a low level of electrification (34%) compared with other countries in the Caribbean. Jamaica is lagging behind in the uptake of EVs when compared to other countries in the Caribbean. The adoption of EVs will help reduce greenhouse gas emissions and improve air quality by reducing the reliance on fossil fuels as sources of energy, and minimizing the environmental impacts of energy production and utilization. Jamaica Public Service is developing a charging infrastructure for public transport (buses) and private vehicles, including the installation of public charging infrastructure (e.g., LPS), until 2021 when the Ministry of Science, Energy and Technology proposes a target of at least 10% of the transport mix to be electric (around 50,000 vehicles).

Panama's national government aims to reduce GHG emissions from the transport sector, hence different departments presented proposals for a National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) in 2018. The NCCS was approved by Congress and approved in 2019, promoting four objectives until 2030: 15-20% of the total fleet of private vehicles shall be electric; 24-30% of public transport vehicles will be electric; 70-80% of the total fleet of taxis will be electric; and 25-50% of the public fleets shall be made up of electric vehicles. Panama's updated NCC focuses on reducing GHG emissions from the transport sector, which accounts for about half of the sector’s emissions and represents the biggest challenge.

CURRENT GREEN CLIMATE FUND

Between 2018 and 2030 the total GHG emissions excluding Land Use Change and Forestry (LUCF) of the participating countries grew on average annually 1.5%, whilst transport emissions in the same period grew annually by 2.5%. Transport is the largest emitting sector, accounting for about one-third of global greenhouse gas emissions.

Current transport demand per capita in developing and emerging economies is far lower than in Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) countries but is expected to increase at a much faster rate in the near future. The IEA projects that by 2030, the number of cars on roads worldwide will have increased from about 1 billion today to over 1.5 billion.

Electric mobility is still in its infant stage. End 2020 nearly 7 million Battery Electric Vehicles (BEVs) were in circulation worldwide (IEA, 2021). This represents less than 1% of the total vehicle stock, countries (PR China, USA, Germany, France and Japan account for more than half of all BEV sales globally. The share of BEVs is highest in Norway where they represent about 58% of new car registrations. In Sweden, BEVs are around 20% of new car registrations.

The BEV sales share is worldwide around 3% as of 2020. Only the country in Latin America with more than one million BEVs on its roads is Chile (IEA, 2021). The number of BEVs in Brazil and Argentina are below 50,000 each.

In Europe, the share of BEVs in all other countries in Latin America have BEV sales share below 0.0%. EVs as percentage of total car stock rises much slower due to vehicle replacement rates. In Norway as leading country worldwide the EV’s car stock was around 35% at end-2020.

The IEA projects that by 2030, the number of BEVs on roads will have increased from about 7 million today to over 18 million in 2030. To achieve this goal modelling of the IEA indicates that NEV need to represent 35% of all sales in 2030.

Electric mobility is still in its infant stage. End 2020 nearly 7 million Battery Electric Vehicles (BEVs) were in circulation worldwide (IEA, 2021). This represents less than 1% of the total vehicle stock, countries (PR China, USA, Germany, France and Japan account for more than half of all BEV sales globally. The share of BEVs is highest in Norway where they represent about 58% of new car registrations. In Sweden, BEVs are around 20% of new car registrations.

The BEV sales share is worldwide around 3% as of 2020. Only the country in Latin America with more than one million BEVs on its roads is Chile (IEA, 2021). The number of BEVs in Brazil and Argentina are below 50,000 each.

In Europe, the share of BEVs in all other countries in Latin America have BEV sales share below 0.0%. EVs as percentage of total car stock rises much slower due to vehicle replacement rates. In Norway as leading country worldwide the EV’s car stock was around 35% at end-2020.

The IEA projects that by 2030, the number of BEVs on roads will have increased from about 7 million today to over 18 million in 2030. To achieve this goal modelling of the IEA indicates that NEV need to represent 35% of all sales in 2030.

Fuel cells with hydrogen reformers are still at a demonstration stage. If H2 production is based on water electrolysis, it can be considered carbon neutral. It can however offer a way to decarbonize the transportation sector, especially long-haul and heavy transport. First examples of developing entire H2 value chains are currently being realized in countries such as Germany, Japan, South Korea, China, India, and the United States.

IDB’s public and private worlds are currently supporting various countries with national hydrogen strategies and initiatives on hydrogen production, training and education, and infrastructure, including the creation of fuel cell inclusive “hydrogen ecosystems”.

Private cars are becoming the dominant mode of transport. The mode share of PTV is going down in Program Countries (PC) while it is increasing by private means of transport indicating a growing share of public transport services. This results in increasing emissions of the transport sector as well as increasing economic costs of transport.

Latin American cities are suffocated by bad air. La Paz is the most urbanized region on the planet with an urban population share of 81% (2019 World Bank). According to the latest urban air quality database, 93% of cities in low- and middle-income countries have air pollution levels that exceed WHO’s air quality guidelines. The WHO estimates that annually 4.6 million deaths result due to excessive exposure to fine particulate matter (PM2.5) in cities.

The transport sector is the largest source of PM2.5 emissions, followed by energy production and industry. The most vulnerable population is disproportionately affected by air pollution, as they tend to live and work close to its sources (Nel et al., 2018). Children and the elderly are particularly vulnerable. Recent studies show that women are more


---

affected by gender inequality than men (Clougherty, 2010) due to biological differences, socioeconomic disparities and unequal gender norms. (Mehra, 2021). Gender and transportation: women and men have differentiated needs in terms of transportations given the social normed gendered distribution of care and householded responsibilities, which affect mobility patterns and access to transport.

## BASELINE TRENDS

* By 2030 transport emissions will increase in absolute terms and will represent a much higher share of total GHG emissions.
* Fossil vehicles will continue to dominate the landscape as EVs remain, even with decreasing prices, financial subsidies and infrastructure development.
* PT mode share is likely to decrease with private vehicles being the dominant mode of transport.
* At the same time, urbanization and population growth in cities will lead to fewer vehicle-kilometers for a decrease of emissions per vehicle-kilometer, which will however be (partially) offset through increased vehicle ownership and more parking locations.

## RELATED PROJECTS AND INTERVENTIONS

Multiple actors are engaged in promotion of e-mobility. Next to IDB, the ATD group, CAREL, CABE, the GEF, GIZ, KfW, NAMA Facility, the World Bank and UN Environment Programme are all active in this area.

UN Environment Programme is implementing the GEF Readiness Program: Advancing a regional approach to modernizing public transport systems in Latin America and the Caribbean (Argentina, Bolivia, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Paraguay, Peru, Uruguay). The program will support countries in developing policies and business models, identification of strategies to make the shift to electric mobility in the region and will be implemented through a regional approach.

The Global Environment Facility (GEF) is implementing UNDP’s Global E-Mobility Program including as Program countries Chile, Costa Rica and Jamaica. Technical Assistance (TA) of this program relates to awareness raising and capacity building for policy makers and other stakeholders on the benefits and challenges of electric mobility. It furthermore includes laying the ground for large-scale market introduction of electric mobility through (i) the development of a business case for electric vehicles in the respective countries, including the business models and finance mechanisms; and (ii) the development of plans and studies to ensure environmental and social sustainability of electric vehicle deployment. The TA will also include the identification of pilot projects that can be used for electric mobility demonstration projects to allow for the creation of local experience to de-risk the technology and business case.

ATD together with GIZ and KfW have put forward an e-mobility Program to the GEF which includes Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Panama and Uruguay. AFD only includes mitigation and focuses on commercial electric vehicles (EVs) for urban transport in cities of 100,000 inhabitants or more. The program will focus on urban mobility including sustainable urban development with a gender perspective and micro mobility as well as all other aspects related to the deployment of electric vehicles in urban areas (e.g., charging infrastructure in transport), and (b) it is also including electric vessels for commercial use. A Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) between GIZ and ATD has been signed to strengthen collaboration and synergies on E-mobility.

Countries are increasingly recognizing the importance that e-mobility can play in achieving their NDCs. Many ongoing initiatives are focused on technical assistance and pilot projects. This Program shall complement these efforts by strengthening financial resources for the deployment of electric vehicles, including charging infrastructure, as a complementary manner to other assistance already offered with a focus on countries where TA of other organizations is not available or limited and/or including the aspects not covered by other projects or programs such as green hydrogen usage for transportation or integration of e-mobility with improved urban resilience. Additionally, the green

## B (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

**IF green hydrogen and V2G projects are IMPLEMENTED THEN the population will have access to climate resilient transport infrastructure that is more sustainable than fossil fuel-based transport systems.**

The shift from fossil fuels to electric mobility will reduce greenhouse gas emissions and energy use of transportation by 17% in 2050 compared with business as usual, while also reducing reliance on imported fossil fuels and increasing resources for EVs projects which helps the involved countries to meet their GHG reduction targets. BECAUSE transport is a major source of greenhouse gases and air pollutants, and because electric vehicles are cleaner than fossil fuel cars and men in transportation will have a more comprehensive mobility offer using low carbon electric vehicles compared with fossil fuel-based vehicles.

The potential paradigm shift of the Program in terms of scale is a shift from the current reliance on fossil-fuel-powered transport with high vulnerability of urban transport infrastructure against impacts of climate change towards electrification of transport systems and the development of resilient urban transport infrastructure that meets the needs of people for cycling, walking and using electric micro-mobility combined with gender-sensitive design of urban transport infrastructure. The Program will also contribute to a paradigm shift in terms of scale by increasing use of public and NMT, especially women’s satisfaction and safety in public transportation. The programs target countries are expected to see an increase in the number of EVs on roads as well as an increase in the adoption of electric mobility and in fostering comprehensive urban mobility interventions centered around public and NMT and the electrification of transport systems. This will be achieved by increasing the availability of charging infrastructure, reducing the barrier of high upfront investments puts in other investors and allowing for massification at scale in a commercial manner. Innovative business models include such as separate asset ownership and asset operations, bulk purchase or leasing of EVs, public-private partnerships (PPPs), and blended finance.

The Program will also contribute to a paradigm shift by increasing the resilience of urban transport infrastructure in a cost-effective manner that has the potential for a shift towards more resilient urban transport systems. The Program will also contribute to a paradigm shift by increasing the share of renewables. In terms of replication, the Program’s investments in IBEUTS could be replicated at scale and across cities in countries with similar characteristics as those targeted by the Program cities in Program countries. This is enabled through knowledge management and outreach instruments and the implementation of replicable urban mobility interventions that are tailored to the specific needs of cities in V2G which reduce the decay risk of milk levels for follow-up investors. The paradigm shift is sustainable due to the deployment of electric vehicles, the electrification of transport systems, and the promotion of policies that make EVs achieve price parity with fossil units within this decade. Business models which favor the development of EVs and the electrification of transport systems are also more likely to be sustainable because they are commercially viable and sustainable.

The interventions make cities more livable resulting in a also a behavioral change towards public and non- motorized transport, as well as a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions and energy use. The promotion of policies which enable a commercial mass-uptake of e-mobility, development of human capital in the transition to e-mobility and the interaction with the private sector in the implementation of projects are critical for the success of the Program.

The paradigm shift is also expected to be more rapid than BAU because the transition towards e-mobility happens faster and at a much larger scale than under a BAU scenario.

This means that the transport system will be less vulnerable to extreme climate events, including floods, heatwaves and landslides, by implementing climate-proof urban transport infrastructure which is expected to reduce the vulnerability of cities and their transport system to climate hazards. (2) an electric vehicle fleet with a higher share of EVs than fossil-fuel vehicles will reduce greenhouse gas emissions and energy storage providing electricity during climate hazards. (3) reduced GHG emissions from the commercial vehicles (public and private) will also contribute to reducing air pollution in urban areas. (4) the transition towards e-mobility from fossil to electric means of transportation resulting in 7.85 MTCO2, decreased GHG emissions and energy savings of 130 TWh compared with business as usual. (5) the electrification of transport systems will also have a positive climate benefits reflected in reduced fuel imports, and less foreign dependency and environmental benefits of reduced resource use and waste generation.

The Program is expected to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals by creating favorable enabling policy framework for low carbon and climate resilient transport in the domain of electric mobility.

## Expected specific outcomes are (i) an improved air quality resulting in environmental co-benefits of 549 tons less PM2.5 and 5,221 tons lower NOx emissions which again result in economic health cost savings estimated at $74 MUSD; (ii) a reduction in the number of road traffic fatalities by 30% due to improved road safety measures and infrastructure; (iii) an increase in women's empowerment and participation in public life due to implementing measures which favor a safe, available and affordable usage of public and MTM for women by incorporating a gender perspective in the system's design.

### Objectives

The project aims at reducing urban transport damage resulting from climate shocks because of higher investments in vulnerability reduction: (1.2) Fewer days to restore the USTC’s public transport service after climate change events; (3.4) Increased resilience and capacity of USTC’s public transport system to withstand extreme weather events; (3.5) Reduced number of road traffic fatalities due to improved road safety measures and infrastructure; (3.6) Improved access for women to public and MTM services, which are deployed with innovative business models and supported by regulatory policies to enable mass application of EVs; (4.2) Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and decision-making.

Activities related to the integration of urban infrastructure with climate resilient measures are (1.1.3) identification and implementation of measures to increase the climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure. Activities related to the adaptation of urban transport systems to extreme weather events are (2.1.1) at least 2 H2 projects can produce electricity for insertion into the grid during critical periods; (2.1.2) At least 3 EV charging stations are installed in public spaces and/or along major roads; (2.1.3) At least one project is tested on the feasibility of H2 storage and VGC for increasing grid resilience and tested and assessed. Activities related to the integration of urban transport systems with climate resilient measures are (3.1.2) implementation of urban mobility measures; (3.1.2) implementation of urban mobility measures such as improved pedestrianization, bus lanes, cycle lanes, etc., which are deployed with innovative business models.

Activities related to the deployment of EV Hubs linked with innovative business models and supportive policies are (3.2.2) deployment on around 476 electric buses; (3.2.2) deployment of 50 H2 cars linked with an urban fast-charging station; (3.2.3) at least one project is tested on the feasibility of H2 storage and VGC for increasing grid resilience and tested and assessed. Activities related to the integration of urban transport systems with climate resilient measures are (4.1.1) Urban interventions realized with a climate-resilient mobility ecosystem integrated into urban transport include: (4.1.3) Design and promotion of EV conductive policies; (4.1.3) Design and promotion of EV conductive policies. Activities related to the strengthening of institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and decision-making are (5.2.1) At least 60% of the project’s identified investments for urban transport are allocated to low-carbon mobility solutions; (5.2.3) Capacity Building (CB) and knowledge generation on H2 and VGC for increased grid resilience for investments under output 2; (5.2.4) Capacity Building (CB) and knowledge generation on H2 and VGC.

Based on the above, the project is expected to have a positive financial impact in terms of the viability of EV investments related to high upfront costs or low or negative profitability, long payback times and possible loss of income if a technology fails. # PROGRAM DESCRIPTION

The Program links EV deployment with sustainable urban mobility solutions through bus lanes, bus system upgrades and restructurings, smart fare systems, and urban interventions in public spaces surrounding BRT stations and terminals to facilitate and foster the use of PT, NATM and electric micro-mobility. This shall result in low carbon emissions from urban transport and contribute towards building resilience that can be triggered to modernize and upgrade the PT sector and improve its acceptance and image among the public.

The Program links mitigation with adaptation efforts and includes activities to increase the climate resilience of urban transport systems, including: (i) the development of a comprehensive approach for electric mobility infrastructure; (ii) adapting the design of EVs to potential climate associated risks; (iii) structural and non-structural measures to reduce emissions from road traffic in urban areas; (iv) testing of hydrogen refueling stations and V2G in SIDS and infrastructure for testing of peak shaving and as storage option to increase with a potential demand decrease; (v) the development of an integrated approach for electric mobility in public transport systems. Hydrogen as energy carrier is attractive because it can be stored and transported in a stable way and can add to a more resilient urban transport system, especially when combined with battery technology or used at specific times. This can increase the grid resilience and can facilitate the achievement of a 100% renewable energy system. Additionally, the Program includes strengthening awareness of climate risks and risk reduction processes of stakeholders to better manage climate change impacts on urban transport systems as well as addressing operational challenges created by climate threats.

The Program will develop an approach for electric mobility including the vehicles themselves, the identification of the most appropriate charging infrastructure, depot infrastructure upgrades and upgrades to the grid. It will also include the development of a comprehensive hydrogen refueling station and hydrogen storage logistics/distribution, filling stations to Fuel Cell Electric Vehicles (FCEVs) and other hydrogen applications such as hydrogen forklifts, vessels and mobile machinery e.g. forklifts or cranes. The Program however only includes technical aspects but also matches electric mobility projects with the most appropriate business model and financing.

The Program shall contribute to overcome the barriers to mass deployment of EVs. The Program has a comprehensive approach and provides long-term advice on vehicle acquisition, charging infrastructure, and other related issues that are critical for the success of large-scale EV fleets works as well as providing guidance on how to address the challenges associated with large scale EV fleets. The development of large-scale EV fleet works without further concessional finance. The Program will also provide a comprehensive approach for electric mobility in public transport systems (e.g., buses, trolleys or trams) and other hydrogen applications such as hydrogen forklifts, vessels and mobile machinery e.g. forklifts or cranes.

The Program is for commercial EVs and charging infrastructure only. Privately used passenger cars, motorcycles and vehicles are not financed*. Commercial vehicles result in a much larger impact in terms of reduced GHG emissions and air pollution compared to private vehicles. The Program will focus on medium-sized trucks (e.g., vans) and larger vehicles. Concessional finance for privately used vehicles is socially inequitable and can result in a mode shift from public transport to private cars, which is not desirable.

The Program only includes 100% EVs either as BEVs or as FCEVs powered by hydrogen. Hybrid electric vehicles (HEVs) and plug-in hybrid electric vehicles (PHEVs) are excluded. Lahn is an intermediate technology. The average monitored emission reduction of hybrids in only around 20% (see Annex A). A review of ~500 PHEV buses shows that the average emission reduction for PHEVs is even lower than this, with a maximum emission reduction of 30%. This means that PHEVs are not an appropriate technology to achieve the Program’s objective. The Program only includes 100% EVs either as BEVs or as FCEVs powered by hydrogen. charge rate of PHEVs which means more than 1 hour to re-charge their already small batteries. A recent study of ICCT revealed that commercial PHEVs only run 20% of real-world driving on electricity (ICCT, 2020). Also, PHEVs are more complex and expensive to maintain.

The project will focus on reducing GHG produced by using renewable electricity in electrolyzer water. This is pure enough for fuel cells used in transport application without further processing. This is the only method which can ensure zero emissions from transport sector. The project will also address issues related to hydrogen production, storage, utilization and storage technology with all its issues of energy usage as well as permanence of carbon capture and its nuclear waste. It will also address the issue of water use in electrolysis process. Water use for green hydrogen is 10 times less than brown H2 use natural gas, coal or lignite for production. Pink, purple or red H2 is based on electrolysis using nuclear power. The main motive force to foster e-mobility in the countries included in the Program are:

* Reduce GHG emissions.
* Improve air quality and reduce health problems associated with pollution.
* Reduce dependency on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks;
* Positive economic and social impact due to reduced health costs, less fuel imports and job creation;
* Increase energy security by reducing dependence on foreign sources of energy;
* Assess option of the hydrogen economy for the country.

## MARKET ASSESSMENT

ICF has realized a benchmark study on commercial e-mobility to assess the technical options, major business models and market potential in China, India, Europe and Latin America. (Annex 2)

The major results of the benchmark study on Battery Electric Buses (BEBs) are:

* Large-scale deployment of BEBs is still limited to few countries but picking up very rapidly. While PR China still dominates in numbers of buses deployed, India, European and some Latin American cities are experiencing rapid growth.
* BEB deployment requires a system design optimizing bus types and charges. Investments need to be made in the charging infrastructure and fleet management systems.
* Favorable BEB business models include bulk purchasing of buses and leasing structures limited to the bus or the bus operator. The main challenges for BEBs are high upfront investment, low utilization rates due to vehicle ownership and operations is interesting for BEB deployment as it resolves the problem of high-upfront investment and low utilization rate. However, there are several barriers to entry such as lack of public transit investment funds and traditional finance institutions. However, the applicability of such business models might be limited in some countries.
* The total e-bus system requires a 2-3x higher investment than purchasing fossil buses[8]. This incremental investment can potentially be recovered with lower Operational Expenditures (OPEX) – however, the fuel and energy savings are capital intensive. The BEB fleet is more expensive to operate as the batteries are normally financed at (60–90%) a higher debt load; and a higher guarantee demand (e.g. due to sensor failure). The high upfront investment in charging infrastructure and bus depot infrastructure is to a large part sunk costs) The operator municipality could potentially recover these costs by increasing fleet size, reducing the number of competing operators or operations quicker a larger part of the fleet. The large incremental CPEX thus poses more risk and more uncertainty for investors. However, the BEB fleet can be operated with lower fuel prices and maximum lending levels linked to their tax-revenue base.
* Total cost of ownership (TCO) is generally comparable or better than fossil units. This is related to lower CO2 impact and longer lifespan of BEBs with results depending on various critical assumptions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## B

Including the bus life-time, maintenance costs, energy usage, energy costs and battery replacement costs. All of these factors influence the result and are not yet well known. TCOs are based in general on static calculations not reflecting the dynamic nature of the system.

* The payback time of BEBs is in general very long (8 years) and sometimes exceeds the commercial vehicle lifespan thus making investments uneconomic. The Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) is in general below the World Bank’s minimum threshold for acceptable projects.
* The risks of the new technology are not adequately reflected in the FIRR. Loan payments for e-buses are well known to be higher than those for conventional buses. This is due to the higher cost of the bus itself, but also because of diesel prices, maintenance costs as well as bus performance including bus availability rates, high upfront investment and long payback times.
* The risks related to BEBs can be divided into three main categories: (i) risks related to the technology performance (e.g. actual electricity consumption influenced strongly by heating and cooling systems, battery degradation, etc.), (ii) risks related to the cost of energy (which is in a wide range); (iii) risks related to the lifespan of equipment (including the bus itself, batteries and charging equipment).
* (iv) risks related to maintenance and repair costs including high availability rates.
* (v) risks related to fuel prices.
* (vi) operational risks due to reduced reliability (e.g. buses not available on all routes and at all times).
* The lack of large fleets results in skepticism concerning the reliability and cost-effectiveness of BEBs as a potential tool for reducing GHG emissions, especially in countries where there is no country without deployment of large fleets.
* No operator wants to be the first-mover and take the risk, although many acknowledge that this might be the only way forward.

Regulations are often not conducive for BEB investments e.g. the length of concession contracts is often too short (e.g. 5 years) or there is no clear policy on how to deal with the transition from fossil fuel buses to electric ones for leasing companies) or tariff systems do not take into account EVs. Many cities for example give concession periods that are shorter than the payback time of BEBs, which makes it difficult for investors to make investments and commitments to make e-bus investments more attractive than fossil bus investments.

Countries with large fleets of conventional buses have a higher potential for BEB deployment because of the incremental CAPEX of BEBs and in most cases also linearize the financing infrastructure and other BEB system components. However, even in these countries, it is difficult to make BEB investments happen as a mass scale will take at least 5-10 years until BEB investment costs are comparable to those of fossil units and performance risks are perceived by operators to be manageable.

The high upfront investment cost of BEBs makes them less attractive for investors. The initial fleet is scaled back quickly after deployment of initial large fleets. This together with the global price decrease of BEBs and charging infrastructure gives a clear indication that the market will not be able to scale up without additional support needed temporarily.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 22

## Benchmark Study on Electric Vehicles

### Key Findings and Considerations:

#### Mitigation and Utilization of Taxes:
- High frequency: More than 1 shift allows the cab which can be in operations for 24/7.
- Slow charging at home is high; thus, most cases not a feasible option. High-range EVs require overnight charging to ensure sufficient range for daily use.

#### Charging Infrastructure and Policies:
- In many countries with urban e-cabs fleets support cover all day and night.
- Investment in charging infrastructure is required as well as building charging networks at least at an urban level.
- Non-monetary incentive policies are also an important driving force. These include privileged ranks at popular parking lots, free access to public transport, and reduced tolls for electric vehicles.

#### Economic Incentives:
- Electric vehicles require a systemic and combined approach of incentives of financial and other nature.
- The establishment of a well-planned fast-charging infrastructure with priority for e-cabs and good accessibility is required. This will also reduce the range anxiety aspect of e-cabs.
- Without public finance, a fast-charging network in the desired capacity cannot be established.

#### Major Results of the Benchmark Study on Electric Vehicles:
1. Small electric vehicles have a TCO that is lower than that of conventional units, especially due to low maintenance costs.
2. Battery degradation is not as significant for short-distance and inland waterways or coastal activities.
3. Long-haul maritime transportation requires other solutions such as fuels (e.g., methanol, green ammonia or green hydrogen).
4. The major challenges with BEVs are the limited range and high cost of battery replacement. Electric vehicles used for passenger transport, fishing or cargo transport within these conditions where battery electric propulsion technology can be realized in the short term at commercial levels.

#### Hydrogen Usage for Transportation:
- Hydrogen usage is more promising in long-haul heavy-duty vehicles where BEVs have serious shortcomings due to low energy density of batteries resulting in high battery sizes with a high weight and a large volume.
- The major challenges are the development of hydrogen refueling infrastructure, including the replacement of diesel trains on rail/low frequency tracks and short-medium distance maritime transport.

#### Challenges for Hydrogen:
- The major challenges of FCVs are that they require a streamlined infrastructure and harmonized efforts along the entire value chain of hydrogen production, storage, distribution, delivery, and usage.

## PROGRAM STRUCTURE

Through the Program, GCF financing of 200 MUSD will leverage 250 MUSD in ICB and national financing to support around 18 low emission projects with total costs of 400 MUSD.

## FINANCIAL SUPPORT INSTRUMENTS AND TERMS

Funds will be provided, alongside ICB Sovereign Investment Loans and Grants, to borrower Host Countries or other entities that are eligible for GCF funding either directly or through a LIP. In each case, provision of funds will be to SB-Projects that comply with the eligibility criteria and conditions given (see “Project Eligibility Criteria” below). The level of support is determined per project type to get to the maximum concessionality level (see the following table).

### Table 2: GCF Loan and Grant Co-Finance Shares of Total CAPEX of Project

| Project Type | max. GCF loan share | max. GCF grant share | min. co-finance share |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Component 1: Increase climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure | 0% | 50% | 20% |
| Component 2: Increase climate resilience of grid with H2 and GHG reduction | 30% | 50% | 20% |
| Component 3: Electrified integrated urban mobility (public transport) | 40% | 25% | 50% |
| Component 3: Electrified integrated urban mobility (private vehicles) | 70% | 0% | 60% |
| Component 3: Electrified integrated urban mobility (public transport) | 70% | -30% | 30% |

No investment grants are given by the GCF for commercial EVs including e-buses, e-cars or other electric fleets. Such vehicles do however need financial support which shall be obtained from government sources. Pilot projects and FCEV and H2 infrastructure projects will be subject to a higher level of concessionality due to their high costs and high risks. All pilot projects must include a business design which allows for widespread commercial replication.

The proposed terms for GCF funded Sovereign Investment Loans are related to GCF financial terms for high concessionality and low concessionality. GCF high concessionality terms will be applied to sovereign investments regardless of the project type.

The same financial hurdles are if vehicles are operated and owned by a private or a public entity. Program finance will not result in competitive distortions or favoring of specific private service providers as loans for taxis and private

### Footnote
* The sum of maximum GCF loan and grant share is not equal to 100% minus co-finance as GCF shares are maximum i.e., in general the project cannot receive from loans as well as grants the maximum share. Based on the initial project design, the maximum share of GCF funding can be higher than 50%. The maximum GCF loan share and the maximum to finance share add up to 100% in case of not using any investment grants.
* High concessionality: Tenor 40 years, grace 10%, pricing 7%. Low concessionality: tenor 20 years, grace 5 years and pricing 8.79%.

| USA | 90% of incremental cost of entire e-bus system subsidized |
| Colombia | 100% of incremental lifetime cost of e-bus system (CAPEX plus OPEX) subsidized in Phase II |
| Chile | 100% of incremental cost of entire e-bus system for Phase I (200 buses) subsidized with monthly incentive payments; 40% additional incentives for extended operating periods for e-buses compared to conventional units (+40%) and additional grants in lenders |

**Source:** Green Climate Fund

Countries consistently subsidized 80-100% of the entire e-bus system incremental cost for the initial phase. The experience shows that once a large fleet is established subsidies can be reduced gradually and even be eliminated but the competitive price of a bus combined with the experience of mass operations of buses. Concrete examples are:

* **In PH China, the average subsidy level at 120 yuan per bus decreased within 5 years from 150,000 USD per bus to zero.**
* **In India, under FAME 2017-2019, e-buses were subsidized with 60% of incremental costs. This was reduced to less than 40% in 2020 and to only 35%, or 40% of a lower absolute value meaning absolute subsidy would reduce by more than 50% within 2 years.**
* **In the UK, the average cost for low carbon buses (until 2017) subsidized 90% of the incremental cost and the 2nd subsidies reduced to less than 40%.**
* **Chile substituted for the first lot of 200 electric buses 100% of incremental costs. The next lots received no subsidy but a monthly incentive payment of 35 USD per bus. This was gradually increased over time and is now translated into higher payments per distance driven or per service unit for e-buses (TransMilenio, Colombia example).**

Countries with a e-bus fleets which go beyond pilots have put significant incentives in place. The following table summarizes incentives provided by different countries.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

## OBJECTIVES, IMPACTS AND OUTCOMES

The Program supports a paradigm change to low-emission and resilient transportation systems. The Program shall contribute to the achievement of the following outcomes:

* An urban transport system which is less vulnerable to extreme climate events by implementing climate-resilient urban transport infrastructure which is expected to reduce the vulnerability of cities and their transport systems.
* (An) electric grid which is more resilient to climate events through the possibility of using hydrogen and EVs as a source of energy.
* Reduced GHG emissions from commercial vehicles. EVs have significantly lower lifecycle GHG emissions than fossil-fuelled vehicles included in the Program. Mode shift to NMT and electric mobility results in additional GHG reductions.

* Improved air quality, with reduced NOx and PM2.5 emissions, a more efficient use of urban land capital, and a more flexible approach to planning frameworks for low carbon and climate-resilient transport.
* Improved air quality: EVs are a major source of PM; and NOx, a crucial instrument to achieve clean air in cities.
* Reduced energy dependency: EVs use significantly less fossil fuels relative on imported fossil fuels. They therefore contribute to reducing the country’s economy's exposure to fuel price volatility.
* Increased energy efficiency: BEVs are up to 4x more efficient than fossil vehicles. Electrically powered vehicles have a higher overall efficiency compared to gasoline levels; however, localized grid needs to be addressed.

The expected GCF fund-level impacts are reduced GHG emissions through increased access to low-emission transportation options in cities and transport services (1-2 MVEVs over the asset lifetime of investment).

### SOURCES

Initial subsidies cover at least 50% of the incremental cost. The case of various countries however also clearly shows that a full package including electric vehicles, urban fast-charging networks with DC chargers for 50 kW and more (non-financial incentives such as free parking or reduced road tax are also included), and charging infrastructure in public buildings and residential areas is necessary to achieve the desired results. Stand-alone vehicle subsidies are clearly not a solution to the problem and should be matched with regulatory measures and/or financial incentives for urban fast-charging networks, which will therefore only invest in taxi projects where a roadmap towards full electrification is available and linked with regulatory measures.

### DELIVERY CHANNELS AND NEW BUSINESS MODELS

Sovereign Investments Loans (funded with GCF and/or IDB resources) and Investment Grants (funded with GCF resources) are the two main delivery channels for this Program. The specific terms of each transaction will be set in the corresponding Subsidy Agreements. The possible contractual structures that could be used for the Program, depending on the type of Sub-Project, which will be reviewed, analyzed, detailed, and/or modified as needed on a case-to-case basis is mentioned in Section 6.4 of this Funding Proposal.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 26

## PROGRAM COMPONENTS

The Program is structured in 4 components:

- **Component 1:** Increase climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure
- **Component 2:** Conduct a cost-benefit analysis to assess the potential and viability of such interventions for increasing the grid resilience
- **Component 3:** Develop modal shift towards urban mobility
- **Component 4:** Technical assistance

### Component 1: Increase climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure

The outcome of this component is (1) Urban transport system less vulnerable to climate extremes.

To increase climate resilience of the EUITS, it is important to understand the climatic hazards conditions at which its infrastructure is exposed and how vulnerable it is to climate stresses. The design and implementation of climate resilient transport infrastructure can provide such measures that mitigate the risk of transport systems failure. Then, the Program will develop a cost-benefit analysis methodology for assessing the potential and viability of such interventions.

The proposed methodology is based on the following steps: (1) A qualitative disaster and climate change risk assessment for the EUITS (see Annex 2). This step includes an evaluation of the vulnerability of the transport system to extreme weather events. (2) An economic analysis for the pre-feasibility stage of the implementation of urban interventions considered as adaptation measures, including a cost-benefit analysis methodology. The proposed methodology will be applied in two scenarios: under climate change scenarios and adaptation alternatives, for (i) the network of routes of the bus system and (ii) the network of routes of the metro system. The economic analysis will include an evaluation of the costs associated with considering nature-based solutions, which can be derived from projects underway in the city. The methodology defines climate resilient transport infrastructure as a set of measures that are designed to reduce the vulnerability of the EUITS to extreme weather events and other hazards. These measures should be integrated along the route and its components (terminals and stations) and make an economic evaluation of the costs associated with considering nature-based solutions, which can be derived from projects underway in the city's public transport system. To contribute towards local transformative adaptation processes, the Program will develop a Decision Support Risk Assessments and Management Plans (DSRAMP) as input to develop at least one regulatory system for the EUITS urban transport system. The DSRAMP will include a set of guidelines for the planning, design and operation of the system to make it reliable to future climate.

The Program will also support the city in developing a strategy that includes:

- (1) A prioritization list of higher investments in vulnerability reduction
- (2) Fewer days to restore EUITS's public transport service after climate shocks due to investments in climate resilience

### Component 2: Conduct a cost-benefit analysis to assess the potential and viability of such interventions for increasing grid resilience

The Program will support the city in developing a strategy that includes:

- (1) A prioritization list of higher investments in vulnerability reduction
- (2) Fewer days to restore EUITS's public transport service after climate shocks due to investments in climate resilience

This component addresses the barriers of limited commercial attractiveness of e-mobility and performance risks of EVs and also includes specific investments on adaptation such as V2G and infrastructure for peak shaving, both measures aimed at increasing grid resilience.

The output of this component is (2.1): H2 and V2G pilots are used to assess their viability of mass deployment to increase grid resilience.

(2.1) V2G projects with FCEVs and CO2 stabilization measures implemented: (2.1.2) V2G projects with grid stabilization measures implemented; (2.1.3) Feasibility of V2G and H2 storage as cost-effective solutions for increasing grid resilience; (2.1.4) Development of a business case for the large-scale usage in transportation which work along the entire value chain of the hydrogen economy including hydrogen production, distribution, refueling, and vehicle usage; (2.1.5) Feasibility study on the development of a market which allows for massification on commercial terms. Private usage vehicles including passenger cars are not financed.

### The activities of this component

(2.2) H2 pilot projects: (i) The program encompasses only commercially used vehicles with a focus on heavy trucks and buses, mobile refueling stations, stationary fuel cells, and hydrogen production infrastructure; (ii) H2 pilot projects are used to assess the technical and commercial viability as well as barriers to and potentials of using hydrogen in transportation which work along the entire value chain of the hydrogen economy including hydrogen production, distribution, refueling, and vehicle usage. The program encompasses only commercially used vehicles with a focus on heavy trucks and buses; (iii) H2 pilot projects are used to assess the technical and commercial viability as well as barriers to and potentials of using hydrogen in transportation which work along the entire value chain of the hydrogen economy including hydrogen production infrastructure, a stationary fuel cell and other components to assess the technical and commercial viability and cost-effectiveness of hydrogen refueling stations, mobile refueling stations, stationary fuel cells, and hydrogen production infrastructure; FCEV vehicles, filling stations and equipment for hydrogen storage, stationary fuel cells, and hydrogen production infrastructure are not financed.
