## Brief Pilot Project Paraguay (see Annex 2c)

The project will implement a green hydrogen pilot project which shall be the catalyst for the development of a hydrogen economy in Paraguay. Paraguay has vast renewable energy as well as water resources while fossil fuels need to be imported at high cost. The project will demonstrate that it is possible to produce green hydrogen from renewable energy and use this hydrogen economically.

The project includes production of green hydrogen based on electrolysis at 3 strategic locations where there are large amounts of renewable energy available. The pilot project will also include a demonstration of the competitiveness of green hydrogen with fossil fuels in the power sector, as well as in other sectors such as transport. The cost-effectiveness and competitive advantage of hydrogen will be assessed.

The pilot project shall produce some 120 tons of H2 annually and includes 1 heavy duty vehicle (HDV) and 5 light-duty vehicles (LDVs). The HDV will be used to demonstrate the use of hydrogen in the power sector. The LDVs will be used for demonstration purposes, including the use of hydrogen fuel cell cars to support grid at times of peak load by producing electricity from hydrogen: (i) a fuel cell can act as both an energy source and a load; (ii) the fuel cell can also be used to charge batteries in electric vehicles; or (iii) it can be used with the LV switch board to electrify the building. The project also includes TA to create capacities on hydrogen technology of 10-20 MUSD for Paraguay, including training and capacity building activities as well as technical assistance for the adoption of hydrogen technology commercially viable.

The total budget of the project is $11 MUSD or which 9.5 MUSD for the pilot project including grid resilience measures; 1 MUSD for TA and 1.5 MUSD for project administration.


## Brief VGP Pilot Barbados (similar project to Jamaica)

EVS can enable utilities to balance loads through V2G technology which is a particularly useful application for intermittent renewable energy sources such as solar and wind. EVs can be charged when VRE supply provides excess capacity, or when power demand is low. When power demand is high, they can inject power into the grid through V2G technology. The project will demonstrate the use of EVs to provide ancillary services (e.g., frequency response) in the grid. EVs can also be used to bolster disaster resilience: EVs can be used to transport doctors and supplies during natural disasters such as hurricanes, floods or earthquakes; EVs can be used for charging stations and the commercial viability compared to other storage options. The project consists of 10 electric vehicles (EVS) which will be deployed in Barbados. The EVs are expected to provide ancillary services through long-persistent grid connection times which can be used for load balancing. Together with the 10 buses bi-directional charging stations and 25 charging points, this project includes a total of 35 MUSD for vehicles; 7 MUSD for chargers and connections and 1 MUSD for testing and pilot project management.

### Component 3: Electrified Intelligent urban mobility

The outcome of this component is (3) Reduce GHG emissions from commercial e-buses. This component addresses the barriers of limited commercial attractiveness to invest in e-buses, performance risks of e-buses, limited know-how and experience with cost-effective measures to increase the resilience of urban transport systems; and lack of financial support for investments to reduce climate vulnerability of urban transport infrastructure.

The outputs of this component are (3.1.1) mobility is integrated with PT & NMT measures to foster mode shift to low carbon transport, (3.2) EV fleets are deployed with innovative business models to enable their mass application. The project will demonstrate the use of e-buses in Barbados and Jamaica as well as other countries through relevant urban mobility measures such as pedestrianization, bus lanes & route structuring; (3.2.1) Deployment of 4 e-buses; (3.2.2) Deployment of e-buses with order-based charging infrastructure; and (3.2.3) Deployment of pilot projects with electric vehicles.

The goal is to develop large-scale fleets of public transport e-buses that provide a reliable and sustainable service to the city. This can include different e-bus technologies and bus sizes (slow, fast, optionally charged buses, hybrid technology). The fleet will be deployed in a single city with an urban population between 500,000 and less than 20 bus stops. Business models and financial structures are defined per project based on local circumstances.

This can include separation of ownership and operations of assets, e-bus leasing systems or PPPs. E-bus projects will be considered for funding if they meet the following criteria: (1) a comprehensive capacity building training and awareness measures (as explained above) as well as improved NMT, micro-mobility, and mobility accessibility. Alternative and new business models are encouraged to ensure that the project is financially sustainable in the long term. E-bus mobility facilities service the independence and mobility of all residents and are key to promote sustainable, connected cities. They can be used by people with disabilities, older adults, young children, pregnant women and men. Public spaces for mobility such as bike lanes, pedestrian lanes, mixed mobility lanes (buses, bikes, 3-wheeled vehicles), sidewalks, etc., are essential in creating a safe environment that promotes social resilience.

All projects include a comprehensive capacity building for training and capacity building part and serve as a model for other cities. # Brief Panama City Project

The Panama City project aims at providing a resilient public transport service in Panama City. The project builds on previous analyses and ongoing pilot projects in the city. To increase the resiliency of the urban transport system the urban transit network will be expanded, upgraded, and integrated with other modes of urban mobility.

The project will (i) introduce 100 12m buses-and its associated infrastructure to the city distributed over 7 bus routes which have been presented in the World Bank study[1] for design and develop public spaces around public transport stations; (ii) design and implement a new electric micro-mobility fleet of 500 e-bikes and 300 scooters, as well as charging infrastructure to provide public transport and alternative modes of urban mobility such as NMT and electric micro-mobility; (iii) design and implement an electric taxi fleet for the Republic of Dominican; (iv) design and implement a new bus rapid transit system with dedicated lanes and stations to increase climate resilience of the EUTCs[2] and to increase institutional capacity to design, implement and operate climate-resilient urban transport systems and assess their impacts and associated costs.

In order to implement this project different studies have been conducted: a quantitative risk assessment for hazards such as landslides, floods, and storms for the public urban transport system; and its adjacent areas to identify adaptation measures (see Annex for details on the methodology); urban mobility and public space analysis around the different bus routes; an electric taxi fleet for the Republic of Dominican; and an electric micro-mobility fleet that is accessible and safe, and incorporate infrastructure connecting public transport modes to alternative modes of urban mobility such as NMT and electric micro-mobility.

The quantitative risk assessment will analyze hazards such as floods[3] and extreme heat[4] for the public urban transport system in Panama City. This assessment will properly model and integrate the four components which make up a risk assessment: (i) exposure, (ii) vulnerability, (iii) climate change impacts, and (iv) adaptation measures under different climate conditions; (iv) conditions including climate change projections from ensemble Global Circulation Models[5]; and (v) adaptation measures. The analysis will be conducted for the public urban transport system in Panama City taking place in Panama City. The assessment will report which components (road infrastructure, stations of terminals, etc.) are most vulnerable to climate change impacts and what adaptation measures can be implemented to reduce these climate-related hazards and develop a sensitivity analysis to determine the criteria to prioritize each of the critical components. This prioritization will allow for an efficient allocation of resources in order to implement adaptation measures that define which bus route to prioritize, with special attention in estimating the increased costs of the implementation of adaptation measures, climate change impacts and the potential reduction in losses following the implementation of adaptation measurements in the public urban transport system.

## Brief: Electric Taxis for Dominican Republic

2 Private taxi operators are interested in incorporating around 500 electric taxis into their fleet. The taxis shall be equipped with charging infrastructure through an existing national system. A pre-condition for successful operations is the establishment of an urban fast-charging infrastructure in San Domingo. The project includes the following:

[1] Hazards and Risk Study and Green Gasoline User Inventory under the IDB Emerging and Sustainable Cities Initiative from 2016: Analysis of electromobility solutions for public transport in Panama, Deloitte 5: Final report and recommendations. World Bank, 2017.

[2] Electric Taxis for Dominican Republic: Electromobility Solutions for Public Transport in Panama. Report prepared by Deloitte Consulting Limited, 2018.

[3] Data available from the baseline studies for the IDB Emerging and Sustainable Cities Program (ESC) in Panama City, https://esc.idb.org/documents/programmes/docs/paper_doc_plan_action_2017.pdf

The activities of this component are (4.1.1) Urban interventions realized with a gender perspective; (4.1.2) Implementation of new business models for e-bus systems; (4.1.3) Design and promotion of EV conducive policies; (4.2.1) Apply methods to identify the most appropriate business model; (4.2.2) Identify key enablers for increased grid resilience; and (4.2.3) Training & CB of stakeholders.

The component activities are coordinated with other components, including those that address non-conducive to e-mobility deployment, lack of skills and competencies for e-mobility in the workforce, limited know-how on mainstreaming climate resilience into design, planning and operation of IEC/TSs and ineffective efforts to achieve gender equality. For more information on potential business models, see section 4.1.2. The activities are coordinated with other components that address different business models with stakeholders and identifies the most appropriate business model based on the project context.

This component and associated activities provide technical assistance to enable effective financial assistance and to create a policy and business framework conducive for massive deployment of EVs. Activities are coordinated and related to other components, including those that address non-conducive to e-mobility deployment, lack of skills and competencies for e-mobility in the workforce, limited know-how on mainstreaming climate resilience into design, planning and operation of IEC/TSs and ineffective efforts to achieve gender equality. Activities include capacity building such as training to improve IECS/TS operations, improvement of existing IECS/TS policies, development of new policies, identification of key enablers for increased grid resilience, knowledge management strategies to facilitate the creation of benchmarks, regulations and norms for designing and implementing e-mobility systems.

To address the technical assistance needs of each country and of each Sub-Project in particular, it is contemplated to work through TA packages or a mobility at a regional level. National and local level, TA activities for increasing resilience will be carried out by the respective national agencies and/or other relevant stakeholders. Technical assistance cooperation granted by the IDB with resources of the Program.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 31

such as the role of e-mobility in achieving 100% renewable electricity generation, V2G projects and usage of electricity, hydrogen or ammonia for vessels.

The Program will realize under 4.2.1, 4.2.2 and 4.2.3 TA activities within the area of increasing the climate resilience of EU/EEA countries by: (i) developing cost-effective solutions to increase the resilience of transport infrastructure of cities in transport infrastructure linked with e-mobility development; and to increase grid resilience through projects on electrification of transport infrastructure, including charging stations and V2G projects; (ii) providing training to improve the climate way resilience is maintained in existing processes for planning, designing, and operating transport infrastructure; and (iii) supporting the implementation of best practices and lessons learned from best-practice studies, and outreach events in this area that may lead to new regulatory systems for PT designers and operators.

The main action plan (active 4.1.1) will boost women's participation and gender awareness in the electromobility sector. They will: (i) increase women's access to economic opportunities through the development of technology and employment skills; (ii) provide training on gender equality and inclusion for all stakeholders involved in the selection of the projects; (iii) increase gender awareness to recognize women as part of the electromobility sector and address their needs through the development of gender training, which must be included in any CBI activity in the framework, including the development of a gender action plan for each project financed under this call; (iv) take a gender perspective in the frameworks, including EV conducive policies which will incorporate explicit requirements for 5 E-buses to be deployed by 2030 and 10% of all electric vehicles sold in the EU to be used for public transport; (v) promote sustainable urban mobility through the establishment of women's networks; and several communication activities; and (vi) increase gender awareness among stakeholders involved in the selection of projects, including the development of gender tools and gender case studies and the support in the development of gender action plan for the projects financed.

Under the activity 4.2.3 training and CB of stakeholders a Regional Sectional Common Skills for Electromobility (RSCS) will be developed to provide common skills for electromobility, including: (i) the development of guidelines for the development of human capital studies; standards, from the trainers, online courses, etc.) available to member countries; (ii) the development of a training package and learning resources for each country, including their packages and learning resources, allowing to assist each of the member countries in the development of their human capacity; (iii) the development of a gender action plan for each project financed and recommendations for gender equality and inclusion in new skills and positions in the workforce will be established.

### PROJECT ELIGIBILITY CRITERIA

All Sub-Projects financed by this Program must meet the following eligibility criteria:

In terms of eligible financial intermediaries for investment Sub-Projects:
* IIB eligible borrowers that include within their mandate the development and/or financing of local infrastructure, including transport infrastructure at a sector or regional level, including low carbon infrastructure, mobility and renewable energy.
* For climate change adaptation / resilience focused subprojects, which target a reduction of GHG emissions, eligible investments should qualify as climate change mitigation finance as per the Joint MDBs IDFC Common Principles for Climate Adaptation Finance Tracking. These should intend to address the identified risks, vulnerabilities, and impacts, and financed activities. At least 50% of the total project cost should qualify as climate finance.

In terms of financial instruments (Sovereign Investment Loans and Investment Grants):
* GCF total financing: not more than USD30 million per Investment Sub-Project.
* The Program aims to finance at least one Sub-project in each Host Country.

* Maximum GCF loan and grant and minimum co-finances share of total costs per project in each component and per sub-project as defined in the relevant sections of this Proposal. As an example, co-financing requirements per Sub-project in component 2: where with 20% co-financing for total Sub-projects (including DB Co-financing and other Co-financing) for H2 or VGS pilot projects.

## In terms of Investment Grants:

* Investment grants are not intended to finance any commercial activity including e-buses, -bikes or other electric fleets in components 3.2.1 and 3.2.2.
* Pilot electric and FCEV vehicle programs shall receive limited Investment Grant support by the GCF related to test pilots of up to 50 vehicles with high miles. All pilot projects must include a business design which allows for widespread commercial replication.

* Investment grants are on finance also adaptation measures in Components I and D.

## Eligibility criteria per component:

* Applicable to Components 1,2 and 3d:
    * All Sub-projects must prove a reduction in GHG emissions against a baseline and demonstrate a commitment to long-term emission reductions. The approach to be used will be determined in Annex 2A to the Proposal and is based primarily on UNFCCC methodologies registered under the Clean Development Mechanism.
* Each Sub-project within Components I, 2 and D will need to be screened against a climate related risk following DB's Disaster and Climate Change Risk Assessment Methodology (DCRA). Projects resulting from this screening that pass the Risk Classification will complete a climate risk assessment.
* Sub-projects with exception of pilot projects must comply with economic viability threshold values of projects in accordance with IOB practices.

## Safeguards:

* Compliance with DB’s environmental and social safeguards policies as outlined in the environmental and social management framework (see Annex C). All Sub-projects must have a supportive sustainable development impact.
* Sub-projects must be in compliance with relevant national and local laws and regulations.
* Each Sub-project shall have a gender action plan taking into account guidelines developed in Annex A.

In the case of Colombia, the Program will limit financing to intermediate cities without established mass rapid transit systems.

Gender: All sub-projects financed by the Program, including pilot projects, must include the IOB’s gender alignment requirements which are:
    * (i) an analysis identifying relevant gender gaps,
    * (ii) gender actions plans and/or gender action strategies,
    * (iii) where applicable, a disaggregated budget (disaggregated indicators do not qualify as gender related indicators), and
    * (iv) allocated resources for the implementation of these gender actions plans or strategies.
    * The Program will assess whether the project has demonstrated that it has the capacity to deliver on gender issues. If the input is not satisfactory, the IOB could provide additional support or suggest the hiring of an external consultant, depending on the project’s complexity.

The sum of maximum GCF loan and grant share is not equal to 100% minus co-finance as GCF shares are maximum (i.e., in practice, they can be less than 50%). The minimum co-financing requirement for each component and per sub-project is defined which can result in some projects having a higher grant (and lower loan) share and other projects having the opposite. The maximum GCF loan share and the minimum co-finance share alone add to 100% in case of not using any investment grants.

## Component A: Increase climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure

- **ERRA = 12%**
    - GCF Proceeds will finance only the incremental cost of adaptation-related intervention as calculated in project's economic analysis (the Disaster and Climate Change Risk Assessment Methodology) will generate an economic assessment for the adaptation measures that will inform the project’s economic analysis.
- **Component B: Increase climate resilience of grid with hydrogen (H2) and V2G pilot projects**
    - Only H2 pilot projects with usage of 100% renewable energy will be financed and where no conflict with freshwater availability exists.
    - H2 pilot projects in areas with limited freshwater availability must proof that the H2 production activity will not reduce freshwater availability.
- **H2 projects:**
    - Hydrogen for fuel cells and electricity for hydrogen production will only be supported in countries with a grid factor below 0.1 kgCO2/kWh (currently Panama and Costa Rica) or where additional renewable electricity is generated to support the H2 project for hydrogen production.

## Component C: Electric vehicle urban mobility

- **EVs financed:** Only BEV or FCEV commercial vehicles including vessels and mobile machinery are financed (no vehicles for private usage).
    - Retrofits: For road-based vehicles retrofits are only eligible if security can be duly justified according to national reciprocity agreements.
    - Taxes and ride-hailing vehicles are only financed if the city or country has a roadmap and regulatory measure which results in a full conversion to electric taxis and/or ride-hailing vehicles latest by 2040.

## Other eligibility requirements

- According to the ESS classification, Category A Investments (pursuant to the Environmental and Social Risks Categories) are excluded from eligibility for financial support under this Programme.
- For Sub Projects for Global Credit Program and consistent with IDB’s ESS classification only ESS Category C or below projects will be eligible for financing.
- The EE shall commit to only finance with resources from this Programme projects that, in addition to complying with the eligibility criteria previously described, comply with all pertinent regulatory requirements of each Host Country and/or Region where the project is located.

Pass through GCF concessionality

Any Sub-project structured as a global credit program needs to ensure that final borrowers can benefit from the concessional terms provided by the GCF. To this effect, a methodology to ensure the transfer of this concessionality will be developed in close collaboration with IDB and GCF. The objective is to ensure that eligible project final borrowers effectively benefit from the improved financing conditions—at least one of the terms previously mentioned (i.e., interest rate, term, or structure).

The preparation process of this customized methodology to transfer concessionality would consider local market conditions and the specific context of each Sub-project, so that the concessionality is used effectively address the main constraints in the project. The key elements for the design of the methodology will be: (i) the level of interest rates; the financial structure selected and the concessionally transfer approach (and thus the


---
**Page 40**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 34

## B

### Methodology (B) will be focused at supporting access to lower interest rates; if the main challenge is linked related to tenor, the financing solutions and methodology will be focused at supporting access to longer tenors.

An indicative approach to a methodology* aimed at ensuring the transfer of concessionality through the enhancement of concessional interest rates and/or terms is presented below. This will be further tailored and expanded, as needed, during each Sub-project preparation process:

- To ensure end-beneficiaries access concessional interest rates:
  - If LFs are already competitive with respect to their existing financial products for similar loan recipients (who are themselves normally already more attractive than comparable products offered by other lenders), then no further concessionality will be provided.
  - If LFs in Tier 2 operations will be required to declare (1) the average interest rates applied to their loan portfolios, and (2) the average tenors of these loans. In addition, if LFs are willing to provide GCF resources will be offered a fixed interest rate lower than said average. This aims to ensure that LFs can offer concessional interest rates.
  - With regards to tenors, for EEs and LFs the same logic applies but in this case, declared information should also reflect the prevailing average tenors in similar loan portfolios, and requirements to provide longer tenors on GCF-funded loans.

The AIs will require each report how the concessionality would be passed onto the loan recipients (final borrowers) using the following criteria:
  - For Tier I: lenders focus on loan recipients, the difference between EEs financing conditions (interest rate and/or term) and LFs’ financing conditions (interest rate and/or tenor), and the comparison with co-financing, and to financing conditions of loans without GCF resources.
  - For Tier II: lenders focus on loan recipients, the difference between EEs financing conditions (interest rate and/or tenor) and LFs’ financing conditions (interest rate and/or term); and b) financing conditions of GCF-funded loans, and to financing conditions of loans without GCF resources.

Data on the described reports will be sourced from a combination of publicly available data, internal EE data, and LFs' disclosure, as applicable.

Given these considerations, whenever final borrowers decide to take on a loan from a GCF-supported financing line, the concessionality provided will be deemed adequate. Adequate concessionality is driven by the decisions of the final borrower. The concessionality provided will be considered adequate if: i) the loan offered by LFs has either lower interest rates (or tenors shorter) than required by borrowers; which would go against its own financial interest and ii) the concessionality provided aims to i) reduce costs and subjecting for all actors involved, ii) provide a feasible and implementable application.

Each concessionality methodology will be incorporated in the Operating Regulations (OR) of the Sub-Projects. As such, concessionality will be applied on a case-by-case basis by the LFs. The AIs will have to report periodically and on the post of the application of the methodology of concessionality provided to final borrowers.

EEs are purchased instead of new conventional vehicles i.e., the old unit at the end of its lifespan and not retired or phased out prior normal retirement. The intervention of the Program is that instead of a new fossil vehicle a new EV will be introduced in the fleet. The comparison with the existing fleet includes an assessment of environmental impacts and financial calculations are a comparison of a new fossil against a new electric unit. The Program does not purchase vehicles but rather replaces them. In this regard, it should be noted that the cost of replacement is an example in the case of buses old municipal buses could still be used to replace even older rural or private transport

** The objective of such lower interest rate is to induce the uptake of eligible investments under the FAF, and changes current financial market conditions. This will be achieved by providing a fixed interest rate below the average interest rates in the cash flow profile of the investments, to be deemed to provide financial additional. NBF policy mandate is to ensure that concessional interest rates are not provided for investments with an expected NPV below zero. The risk appetite of LFs in assessing concessionality will be determined by the extent to which it allows LFs to maintain their current profitability levels from these types of activities. While always utilizing LFs’ own resources, LFs may provide concessional interest beyond its own existing financing conditions and extend that benefit to final-end beneficiaries.

### B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words; approximately 7 pages plus diagrams)

Sub-Projects will be implemented in Barbados, Belize, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay, Uruguay (the “Host Countries”). For the avoidance of doubt, the Funded Activity will not be implemented in any other country. The Host Country Governments will be responsible for ensuring that the Sub-Project is consistent with its national policies and procedures which may consist of a single Activity or set of Activities under the respective Component. The Funded Activity will be financed by the GCF through Technical Cooperation Grants, Financial Instruments including in the case of Sovereign Investment Loans, High Concessional Loan and Low Concessional Loan, as well as other financial instruments such as Non-Recourse Financial Instruments. Activities under Component 4 will be financed by Technical Cooperation Grants. For the avoidance of doubt, the Accountable Entity will not be responsible for any costs associated with the implementation of the Sub-Project. The Host Country Government and the Accountable Entity will jointly identify and select the Sub-Projects that meet eligibility criteria set forth in Section B.3 of this Funding Proposal and the overall Project.

### Host Countries

The Program will be implemented in Barbados, Belize, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay, Uruguay (the “Host Countries”). For the avoidance of doubt, the Funded Activity will not be implemented in any other country. The Host Country Governments will be responsible for ensuring that the Sub-Project is consistent with its national policies and procedures which may consist of a single Activity or set of Activities under the respective Component.

## Co-financing

The total amount of expected Co-financing for the Programme is estimated to be USD 250,000,000 (the ‘Co-financing’). The Co-financing will be provided by the co-financiers as set out below. Subject to such approvals being obtained and subject to the terms of the corresponding legal agreements), including the co-financing arrangements with the GCF Trust Fund, the Co-financing shall be provided by the following institutions or financiers:

- an estimated amount of USD 200,000,000 by the AE, including GCF own resources and other resources administered by GCF;
- an estimated amount of USD 50,000,000 by the Countries (the co-financing source under “Countries” is explained in the Budgets, Annex A).

### Co-financing

Co-financing joins jointly the amounts of estimated funding to be provided by the co-financiers and separately, the GCF own financing (as detailed Annexes 4).

## 1. Implementation Arrangements

### 1.1 Introduction

The GCF and the AE will enter into a Funding Activity Agreement (FAA) in the form of a trust agreement applicable to the GCF Trust Fund® based on the terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet of the Program. As provided in the Annexes, the FAA shall be subject to the terms and conditions of the Sub-Project Agreements entered into by the AE and the GCF for the financing of the Program. The AE will solely responsible for the management and administration of the Program and the implementation of the Sub-Projects under this Agreement. The FAA is in accordance with the form set forth in the AMA and the FAA. In this context, AE will manage, provide monitoring and supervisory mechanisms to ensure compliance with the terms and conditions of the FAA.

The IDB, in its capacity as the AE, will execute Subsidiary Agreements pursuant to the terms and conditions of the FAA, once the relevant Sub-Projects are approved by the IDB and for the implementation of Component 4 (except in cases where the GCF Trust Fund® is the sole financier). The Sub-Project Agreement will establish the applicable framework and conditions for execution of each Sub-Project.

### Selection and approval of Sub-Projects

Following a request from a specific Host Country to the AE, a Sub-Project will be selected and approved by the AE and included in the Program. Each Sub-Project will have its own Sub-Project Agreement that will establish the applicable framework and conditions for execution of each Sub-Project.

The AE will carry out all appropriate due diligence on each Sub-Project, including at checks and assessments on the Borrower’s capacity to execute such Sub-Project and on the identification of any risks associated with such Sub-Project that it ordinarily would carry out in any project/program financed by the AE and in accordance with the AMA and FAA.

## 1.2 Contractual and Implementation Arrangements

### 1.2.1 Components 1, 2, 3 and 4 of the Program

Sub-Projects under Components 1, 2, and 3 of the Program will be executed through EE. For the avoidance of doubt, all Sub-Project Agreements entered into by the AE in respect of Components 1 and 2.

For the avoidance of doubt, the Borrower and the Grantees are the EEs, unless otherwise specified below.

**Designated Country:** The EE shall be an eligible borrower (either the Host Country or public entity of the Host Countries) under the AE policy. If the Host Country is not the Borrower of the Sovereign Investment Loan, in accordance with the terms and conditions of the Sub-agreement, the EE may be a third party that has entered into a Sub-agreement with the Host Country, as the guarantor, agrees to jointly, severally and unconditionally responsible for the financial obligations under the Sub-agreement. The EE is also referred to as the "Sub-Project Implementing Entity" or "SIE". The SIE may be a public entity in the Host Country, in addition to the Sovereign Investment Loans between the Borrower and the AE, the AE will enter into an agreement with the SIE for the implementation of Sub-Projects. If the EE is a public entity under the Host Country, it is understood that private sector entities will not be eligible as a Borrower or grantee.

EEs, in respect of each Sub-Project, it is expected that the Borrower and/or Grantee will be the EE for the Sub-Project. However, if the Borrower/Grantee is the Host Country, the Host Country may act through one its sovereign organs or agencies as an EE.

There may be circumstances where a Sub-Project needs to be carried out, in whole or in part, on an entity other than that which has entered into the Sub-agreement. In such cases, the Borrower/Grantee and/or the relevant third party under the Sub-agreement, the relevant third-party EE will be responsible for the implementation of the Sub-Project. The AE may enter into a separate agreement with the Borrower/Grantee and/or the relevant third-party EE to ensure that technical execution of the Sub-Project is to be carried out in whole or in part, by a separate legal entity; the AE shall establish such arrangements as it deems necessary.

* **Option 1:** If permitted under the laws, rules and regulations applicable to the Borrower and/or Grantee and third-party EE, the third-party EE will also sign the Sub-agreements to confirm its capacity and to agree on its obligations. The Sub-agreement entered into by the Borrower/Grantee and/or the relevant third party EE shall not preclude the existence of an additional Execution Agreement on terms and conditions approved by the AE.

* **Option 2:** If Option 1 is not feasible, the Sub-agreements' set out above, shall cause the Borrower and/or Grantee to enter into a separate agreement with the third-party EE. The Borrower/Grantee will be required to ensure that the third-party EE is executing the Sub-Project in accordance with the Sub-agreement. The AE will be required to sign such an arrangement as it deems necessary, subject to obtaining the Borrower's/Grantee's and/or the third party prior written consent. Under this arrangement the AE will monitor and supervise the EE in order to perform its functions under the Sub-agreement.

* **Option 3:** The foregoing Options are not feasible, on a case-by-case basis in respect of the relevant Sub-Project the AE shall seek GCF's opinion on the implementation arrangement for that Sub-Project.

In any event, under the terms of the Sub-agreement, the Borrower/Grantee will be accountable for all the actions and obligations undertaken by the EE. The Borrower/Grantee is responsible to ensure that the EE complies with its obligations towards the Borrower/Grantee or the third-party EE as the case may be, shall be fulfilled to the satisfaction of the AE. The Sub-agreement will remain available remedies available to the AE in the event the Sub-Project is not executed as required therein.

The Sub-projects may be structured and implemented in the following manner:

* **A. Specific, pre-determined Sub-Projects** alone financing a specific project for specific purposes with implementation arrangements that are agreed upon by the Borrower/Grantee and/or the relevant third party (if applicable) of the Sovereign Investment Loan.

**Option 1: Public Sector-led Direct Delivery:** The AE will enter into Subsidiary Agreement(s) with the Borrower/Grantee, under which it will provide the Borrower/Grantee with a Sovereign Investment Loan and/or an Investment Grant.

Figure 3. Public Sector - led Direct Delivery

**Option 2: Public Sector-led Indirect Delivery:** The AE will enter into Subsidiary Agreement(s) with the Borrower/Grantee under which it will provide the Borrower/Grantee with a Sovereign Investment Loan and/or an Investment Grant, and the Borrower/Grantee will enter into a Public Private Partnership (PPP) agreement with a Public Private Sector Facility (PPSF). The PPSF will then enter into a Subsidiary Agreement with the Borrower/Grantee under which it will provide the Borrower/Grantee with a Sovereign Investment Loan and/or an Investment Grant, which will be the public side party (the "PTP EE"). Both the Borrower/Grantee and/or the PTP EE could be EEs under this option. The PPSF will also enter into a Subsidiary Agreement with the Public Private Sector Facility EE, which undertakes these tasks may be carried out by either the Borrower/Grantee or the PTP EE. The PTP EE will either (i) provide services to the beneficiaries directly or (ii) procure a public transport operator ("PTO") under a procurement agreement to provide such services. As the PTO is procured and has no discretion, it shall not be an EE.

Figure 4: Public Sector - led Indirect Delivery

**Option 3: Public-Private Sector-led Indirect Delivery:** The AE will enter into a Subsidiary Agreement with the Borrower/Grantee under which it will provide the Borrower/Grantee with a Sovereign Investment Loan and/or an Investment Grant. The Borrower will then enter into an Execution Agreement with a public private entity (the "Public Private Sector Facility EE"). The Public Private Sector Facility EE is responsible for implementing the Sub Project.

Figure 5: Public-Private Sector - led Indirect Delivery

## Sub-Projects for Global Credit Programs above financing multiple eligible investments through intermediary financial institutions or similar agencies (such as national development banks or "NDBs") in the Host Countries, which will act as the EEs.

The sub-borrowers that receive the resources from the financial institutions or similar agencies will be referred to as the Borrower. The EE will enter into a Sub-Loan Agreement with the Borrower and Final Beneficiaries in the form that can provide terms (e.g., tenor, price, amortization profiles, collateral requirements) more appropriate for the specific transaction.

To this effect, the IDB transfers the Sovereign Investment Loan and Co-Financing from IDB to the EEs in accordance with the terms of the Subsidary Agreements. If applicable, Co-financing other than IDB Co-financing, as specified in such Sub-Loan Agreement, will be provided by the Borrower. The EE will enter into a Sub-Loan Agreement with the Borrower and Final Beneficiaries in the form that can provide terms (e.g., tenor, price, amortization profiles, collateral requirements) more appropriate for the specific transaction.

When EEs enter into a Sub-Loan Agreement with the Borrower and Final Beneficiaries under this Global Credit Program, the EE will approve the financing of investments to be funded under the Global Credit Program. The IDB will provide the funds to the EEs in accordance with the terms of the Sub-Loan Agreement. Loans to be financed under the Global Credit Programs which will be passed down to the EE under the Subsidary Agreements will be subject to applicable conditions and criteria when selecting the Final Beneficiaries and Sub-Loans.

### Option 1: Global Credit Programs via LFsIs

The LFIs will enter into a Subsidary Agreement with the Borrower, which will provide resources in the form of a Sovereign Investment Loan (and/or an Investment Grant). The LFI will then enter into a further Sub-Loan Agreement with the Final Beneficiaries.

### Option 2: Sovereign Investment Loans for Global Credit Programs without LFIs

The EE will enter into a Subsidary Agreement with the Borrower, which will provide resources in the form of a Sovereign Investment Loan (and/or an Investment Grant). The Borrower will then enter into a Sub-Loan Agreement with the Final Beneficiaries.

The funding (in the form of loan) provided to the Final Beneficiaries either via LFIs or directly from the Borrower is referred to as the Sub-Loan.

## Additional Implementation Arrangements for Components 1, 2 and 3

Subsidary Agreements will contain provisions related to the financial terms and conditions, disbursements and use of resources, execution scheme of the Sub-Project, as well as the supervision, monitoring, and evaluation of the Sub-Project. Sub-Loan Agreements will also include terms and conditions for the Borrower and Final Beneficiaries, as applicable under each transaction. In addition, Subsidary Agreements will require compliance with IDB's policies and procedures, as specified in such agreements.

EEs will be responsible for the full execution of the Sub-Project in accordance with the provisions of the Subsidary Agreement. The EE will also be responsible for ensuring that the Borrower and Final Beneficiaries develop and implement a Program activity based on the relevance of their public mandate to the Program activities, especially in financial structuring and fiduciary management, and track recording, including with the IDB.

As per local policy, prior to the approval of each Sub-Loan, WFP's finance team will perform and/or update an Institutional capacity assessment on the corresponding EEs in accordance with the information provided in Schedule

## 1.2.2 Component 4 of the Program

Activities under Component 4 of the Program will be financed through a Technical Cooperation Grant, Technical Cooperation Sub-Grant or both, and will be linked to the AE by a separate Technical Cooperation Sub-Project, which may be linked to Sub-Projects under Components 1 to 3. For the avoidance of doubt, the Technical Cooperation Grants will not finance the preparation or execution of Sub-Projects at the national or regional levels. The execution of Technical Cooperation Sub-Grants and/or Sub-Projects will be subject to the terms and conditions set out in the TCG Guidelines and the Eligibility Criteria, if applicable. According to the IIB’s Technical Cooperation Policy, IIB may be required to enter into a Technical Cooperation Agreement with the executing entity or entities for the execution of the Sub-Project.

Whether the IIB would be the EEL, or a third party will depend on the specific Host Country context and beneficiaries’ preferences. The IIB will determine whether it is appropriate to use its own resources or to engage in co-financing deployment of specific Technical Cooperation Grants and define on the basis of what if the best execution option. If the IIB is not the EEL, it will be determined and assessed as part of the preparation of every Sub-Project to be designed under the Program.

### 3rd-party EEs, EELs for Technical Cooperation activities must be legally established entities. This includes (i) central government agencies; (ii) public sector institutions with a mandate to provide technical assistance in accordance with agreements with IIB; (iii) regional and subregional agencies established by the same countries; (iv) private companies that are not subject to any restrictions on their ability to enter into Technical Cooperation Agreements, including those associated with their membership of international or regional organizations or associations. When the IIB is not the Executing Entity, IIB will enter into Technical Cooperation Agreements, which may include a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) and/or an Agreement for Co-financing, with procurement policies for their use of GCF Proceeds and Co-financing.

According to IIB procedures, a formal diagnosis of the EEs (similar to the one performed for EEAs of Sovereign Investment Entities) will be conducted in order to assess legal and institutional capacity, fiduciary management, eventual execution risks and identify and implement mitigation measures.

### 2. EE’s of Approved Sub-Projects

Following the effectiveness of the FAA for this Program, GCF Proceeds and Co-financing would be allocated by the IIB in the context of Sub-Projects and multi-country Technical Cooperation Grants. The assessment of the institutional and technical capacity of the executing entities (EE) will be conducted as part of the due diligence process on each Project conducted by IIB and will follow GCF policies, procedures, and due diligence standards. Only after a Sub-Project has been approved by the IIB Board of Directors, the IIB will enter into a Technical Cooperation Agreement with the EE for the execution of the Sub-Project, which may be linked to Sub-Projects under Components 1 or 2. The GCF prior to the disbursement by GCF to IIB for a specific Sub-Project, the contractual arrangement and structural conditions will be reviewed.

As per IIB policies and procedures, prior to the approval of each specific project, IIB’s fiduciary team will perform and/or update an institutional capacity assessment on the EE. This assessment includes overall technical capacity, management of information systems, internal and external controls, and recommendations on any fiduciary risks identified.

In the case of Technical Cooperation executed by EEs, four basic areas are evaluated: (i) Management of Contingent Resources; (ii) Financial Management and Monitoring Capacity; (iii) Information Systems, Internal and External Controls, and Monitoring Capacity; and (iv) Knowledge Management and Strategic Communication Capacity. It should be noted that the IIB’s Technical Cooperation Policy does not require the EE to have a specific capacity in each of these areas but rather the management of resources from national and international donors, good practices, experience, and use of systems to strengthen them with a view to efficient and transparent project execution.

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately two pages)

The Program introduces a concessional financing mechanism that addresses barriers to the uptake of commercial EVs, especially in developing countries where there is limited access to charging infrastructure and grid. Currently fossil vehicles dominate the market. Except in Santiago de Chile and Bogota no large fleets of commercial EVs are available for public use. In most cities in Brazil and in Colombia only a few thousand electric buses operate without transit system. The paradigm shift towards electric vehicles requires new policies, regulations, and incentives to encourage the uptake of these vehicles but also new fueling charging infrastructures, changes in vehicle maintenance and operations and frequently also recharging infrastructure. This is especially true for commercial vehicles where the transition from fossil fuels to the commercial vehicle sector face such disruptive changes as it can affect their core business. Government on the other hand needs to ensure that the transition towards electric mobility is not only a short-term solution but also a long-term sustainable plan. They therefore often state political targets or objectives to promote e-mobility coupled with strategic plans and action programs to achieve these goals. These are usually based on pilot projects, demonstration implementation projects. Such initiatives are dependent on concessional funding to make them financially and from a risk perspective feasible. The Program is designed to support the development of national strategies, country-specific roadmaps and/or developing policies which are cost-effective and reduce actual barriers towards mobility. Stand- alone projects or stand-alone programs are not feasible in most cases as they do not provide sufficient scale for EVs. GCF concessional financing is critical to ensure this comprehensive e-mobility approach - in absence of concessional GCF finance, such projects will not materialize in the short or medium term. Also, IIB links the e-mobility


---
**Page 48**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 44

B

projects with a focus on urban mobility components and with investments to improve the resilience of urban transport infrastructure as well as measures to increase the grid resilience. GCF concessional financing is critical to strengthen the financial sustainability of EVs and other electric vehicles (EVs) in developing countries, where access to finance is imperative to finance prioritize measures to reduce vulnerability to such risks and build resilience for an optimal and safe operation of e-mobility.

The experience with the first 20 projects clearly shows that all countries with large fleets of commercial EVs (>100 units) have used, at least initially, financial support instruments to kick-start mass-EV deployment. The experience of countries is however very different in terms of the level and type of support required for mass-EV deployment. In some cases, it took 5-10 years due to more competitive prices of EVs combined with the experience of mass operations of such vehicles. Also, the number of subsidies can be reduced with adequate policies and regulations (e.g., concession periods for public and business users per vehicle) and by using a combination of financial instruments for different stages of projects which happen at a later stage as:

- Increasing prices of fossil fuels are increasing in all segments due to declining battery prices and increasing competition and supply of EVs. TCO parity with fossil vehicles is expected in the targeted vehicle categories between 2025 and 2030 and price tags of EVs will close to fossil-based models by 2030. TCO alone will in many cases not be sufficient to overcome the initial purchase cost barrier, which is still a significant challenge for EVs significantly higher and the risks are still perceived to be higher. However, decreasing battery costs are very important as they reduce the total cost of ownership (TCO) and make EVs more competitive with fossil vehicles.

- Reduced risks and costs for 2nd and 3rd projects due to showcasing of successful implementation projects and demonstrating that the technology is mature enough to be deployed at scale. This will also help in building business models and enabling with concessionary finance the uptake of large fleets which are critical to reduce these risks.

- Policies which are more conducive for e-mobility and restrict fossil vehicle usage. This allows EVs to be more operationally efficient, as they can be used for longer periods of time (e.g., 24/7) and this will also help in enabling investors to pour capital into this market as profit rates improve and risks get down. This will allow for widespread replication based on commercial terms in 5-10 years if the country has already gained experience with initial fleet deployment.

GCF concessional finance is critical to close the gap between fossil and electric vehicle technologies and to deliver a Profitable EVs business model. The key factors that determine the profitability of an EVs business model are: (i) profitablity and performance risks. Potential investors as well as FIs interviewed were only interested in guarantees; if these incur no cost and if guarantee levels are very high (100%).

Conventional financial institutions are also not willing to enter the market due to the novelty of the technology, the establishment of a new business model, and the lack of experience with EVs and related transaction costs. Concessional GCF financing including grants can provide for technical assistance to design and structure in an optimal manner the investment project, which will help in reducing the risk profile of the project and hence reduce exposure and increase profitability of the investment.

## B

For pilot projects involving electric vessels, hydrogen and V2G the current status is still at the learning curve. Pilot projects which have a potential to be replicated on a broad scale and which already have integrated a business plan for replication are considered supported. V2G and hydrogen projects also have the potential to increase the grid resilience through energy storage.

The multi-country approach is critical for a transformative and accelerated shift towards e-mobility which is again reinforced by the recent adoption of the Paris Agreement, the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) Declaration on Electric-Mobility calls for the deployment of electric vehicles (EVs) compatible with a 20% share of all new passenger car sales in developed countries and a 30% share in developing countries by 2030. This is also necessary given the scale of climate change challenges facing countries and the critical need to deliver transformative change at scale as quickly as possible while vehicle replacement rates are low thus resulting in long lock-in times for vehicles. The multi-country approach can be used to address these challenges through a combination of activities by countries with activities such as the realization of different knowledge materials including publications, webinars, technical assistance and training workshops. These activities will be carried out at national level through trainings and workshops. Through participation of various countries with a similar context the learning effect can be built up in a more efficient way than if each country would have to develop its own solutions from scratch. Such programs are important but experiences from other countries can be brought in much easier if the Program covers investments in pilot projects which are replicable on a broad scale and which already have integrated business plans for replication.

The multi-country programmatic approach resources can be used efficiently to highest priority countries and sectors within these countries. The multi-country approach is also critical because most countries are in most cases too small to warrant a Funding Proposal to the GCF with all transaction costs involved: IDB would be the lead implementing agency of such proposals. In addition, the multi-country approach will allow for synergies and can also capitalize on synergies and lessons learned across countries and vehicle sectors and can effectively ensure that the Program is not only replicable but also scalable.

GCF concessionary financing with technical and policy advice. Investments alone are insufficient to achieve the transformation in climate action needed by the transport sector. Strategic sectoral planning and policy reforms are critical for ensuring long-term sustainability of any technology. The GCF, with its emphasis on a paradigm shift in climate action, is critical for sufficient funding of the policy and technical assistance component of the Program.

### Concessionality of GCF Funding

The Program will benefit from technical assistance that will be made available to accompany each project and will ultimately serve to enhance the viability of the projects. The TA will also support the development of e-mobility roadmaps for countries, which will help to identify the most appropriate technology mix in line with a country’s targeted trajectory change. TA related to knowledge generation and dissemination will ensure an enabling environment for the Program.

Projects involved in the proposed Program will not be dependent on a continuous flow of GCF funds. Rather, The Program aims to achieve substantial reduction in the need for concessional financing in future projects. It is expected that the Program will have a significant impact on the development of e-mobility infrastructure and vehicles in countries which have kick-started the process. The use of GCF funds de-risks similar future investments and discourages reliance on concessional finance.

This Program is critical to reduce the cost of capital to offset partial incremental costs and mitigate the risks from initiating climate investments in a new technology. GCF’s ability to offer concessionary terms in investments enables projects to be implemented at lower risk, which can help to unlock private sector investment. The World Bank report in Annex 2a clearly shows that all countries with large fleets of commercial EVs (>100 units) have used, at least once, concessional financing for the purchase of vehicles. In most cases, these are loans or grants. For example, investment costs of e-buses were grant financed by countries by 80-100%, e-taxis have been subsidized on average by 50% and e-cars by 30%. The GCF can play a critical role in providing concessional financing for charging infrastructure and light commercial vehicles (LCVs) which have received as subsidy 75-100% of the incremental cost of investment. In addition, the GCF can provide concessional financing to support the development of e-mobility policies and tax incentives. The magnitude of subsidies can be reduced with adequate policies and regulations (e.g., concession periods) and business models per vehicle sector. GCF concessional finance combined with national support is critical

## Technical cooperation assistance is used as support for the investment components and for policy and knowledge management.

### Project sourcing and preparation: The Program will provide direct TA to projects that will potentially receive later financial assistance. TA can include pre- and full feasibility assessment, legal, institutional, financial and technical advice. This includes the development of a project design document (PDD) which will support the local governments in the planning of EV routes and charging infrastructures, and include concept designs for charging stations, vehicle-to-grid (V2G), energy storage systems, and waste management and disposal strategy. This also includes the option design of e-mobility ecosystems including charging networks and other infrastructure. The PDD will also include the identification and design of adaptation measures to increase the resilience of urban infrastructure.

- Capacity building: training and monitoring: TA is used to ensure delivery of adequate training from equipment suppliers, manufacturers, and service providers on the operation and maintenance of EVs in order to support optimal management of EV fees. This activity also includes performance monitoring of electric vehicles in technical workshops and field visits.
- Supporting national public policies for EV deployment: Technical support will be provided to the relevant national and local authorities in the countries and cities where the Program will support investments in order to set up, implement, and monitor a policy framework for e-mobility. This includes the development of enabling frameworks for e-mobility and a hydrogen economy. The Program intends to establish in all countries where not yet present, an institutional framework that supports the implementation of the EV strategy.
- Sectoral and specific roadmaps are a tool to support the identification of projects within their scope and aim to help inform and/or further detail the countries long term mobility strategies and their NDCs.

### Development of business cases for EVs: This is targeted for P2 to identify business models, modes which allow for increased influx of private capital and which lower financing costs and risks for operators, leasing models, inclusion of electric utilities in the business case.
- Advisory services in the linkage of e-mobility with increased grid resilience via usage of 2nd life batteries, hydrogen production, etc.

### Advisory services around increased resilience of urban infrastructure, the identification of potential measures, including the development of a strategy for waste management and disposal.

### Inclusion of gender aspects in all projects to be financed as well on a national and regional level.
- Support for development of human capital in the transition to a mobility through solutions such as studies, standards, the development of training programs, etc. The Program will also support the development of an EV strategy and regional outreach. The Program will develop knowledge products and share experiences and cases through workshops, seminars, webinars, etc., and will provide technical assistance for countries that wish to build a base for replication. The Program will not create own platforms or channels for information dissemination but using existing channels.

## B. 6. Fast strategy (min. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Exit Strategy

Repayments under concessional loans provided with GCF funds under the Program will be managed through the G2B-GCF Special Fund in accordance with repayment schedules set forth in IDB’s loan agreement. All loans will be monitored by IDB; GCF resources will be bifurcated to the GCF in accordance with the terms of the FAA. The repayments will be made on a monthly basis and will be used for the same purposes as the corresponding revenues that for conventional units – thus once the system is established all operators will continue using the assets.

Program sustainability

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 3.0 | PAGE 47

It is expected that by 2030 commercial EVs as targeted by the Program are fully commercial viable and more attractive financially than fossil fuels. This is accelerated with the Program which provides for kick-starting the process thereby creating a virtuous cycle of demand, supply and investment in EVs. The Program also aims to provide a platform for further investments in e-mobility. The main Program impact is to initiate this process much earlier than under BAU. However, the Program sustainability is given through the market development of EVs going towards a commercially attractive proposition. Figure 6 shows the projected electric and diesel investment cost per urban bus (real constant USD of 2020) for further years.

The figure shows that the projected investment cost relative to diesel units is shown in the graph. Whilst the investment cost is still expected to be higher for electric buses than for diesel buses, the payback period is significantly lower and the total cost of ownership (TCO) will be significantly lower than for fossil systems (in many countries the TCOs are already today lower but not sufficient i.e., the payback periods are too long). This means that the concession period of PT operators thus not making the investment financially attractive under a risk viewpoint).

## Figure 6: Projected Electric and Diesel Investment Cost per Urban Bus (real constant USD of 2020)

| Year | Electric buses |
| --- | --- |
| 2015 | $380,000 |
| 2020 | $340,000 |
| 2025 | $300,000 |
| 2030 | $260,000 |

Source: Grutter Consulting

It is expected that by 2030 e-buses will be close to sticker price parity with diesel units, whilst total system costs per e-bus are also expected to be around 45-50% higher than a fossil unit. However, the significantly lower OPEX of electric buses (due to lower maintenance and fuel costs) means that the TCO is significantly lower for electric buses.

The sustainability of further concessional finance: TCO costs will be significantly lower and payback periods of incremental e-bus investments are expected to be shorter than fossil systems. This means that the concession period of PT operators can be reduced if the investment risks of e-buses are reduced. This is possible through the establishment of new business models and the provision of total system solutions which also include maintenance contracts thereby reducing uncertainties for investors.

Similar trend of costs projections exist for electric vans, passenger cars and light commercial vehicles (LCVs). The cost of purchasing an electric vehicle is significantly higher than a fossil equivalent which is not borne by the vehicle purchase (except some charges which are pre-paid but paid through usage fees) but through the provision of total system solutions. For example, FCV costs are expected to be comparable with the vehicle price and the creation of charging infrastructure is expected some years later.

At the sectoral level the Program will deliver road maps for electric mobility in public transport and public charging infrastructure. The Program also aims at creating a long-term strategic policy environment conducive towards increased investments in e-mobility. On a regional level the knowledge products and outreach events offer additional support to the Program’s objectives and contributes towards the creation of a non-reversible dynamic towards electrification of the sectoral sector.

## Capacity Building

Capacity Building is delivered under Component 4. This includes specifically:

- Capacity building and technical resources for training of operators, safety staff, maintenance staff etc.
- Technical assistance to support the procurement of equipment and vehicles from equipment suppliers or third parties for EV and charging operations and maintenance, safety trainings and capacity building in optimal use of the equipment and vehicles.
- Support for national enabling policies for EV deployment, advice on battery re-use, recycling and disposal, advice on vehicle standards, advice on urban mobility strategies, roadmaps (electrification of public transport, electrification of taxi and ride-hailing services; electrification of urban delivery services, electrification of long haul freight and passenger transport.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

GHG emission calculations have been made per project type and were then expanded to the entire Program based on the expected number of projects and vehicles per project type. GHG impacts included are well-to-wheel i.e., include upstream electricity generation related emissions as well as transmission and distribution losses of the grid. Areas of emphasis for the Program are (i) electrification of public transport (ii) increased use of electric buses and hybrid/PT ride-shares due to PT system improvements (iii) cycle lanes and increased usage of NMT (and electric-mobility infrastructure) which can be found in Annex 22 and the project reports in Annex 2.

### Impact of EVs

The GHG reduction is due to operation of EVs produced with investments of the Program. The methodology used to determine the impact of operation of EVs is based on the UNFCCC methodology AM-8/1C. The GHG impact is determined based on a well-to-wheel (WTW) basis, which includes emissions from electricity generation and transmission and distribution losses.

The baseline case assumes that all fossil vehicles and not the currently circulating units as the baseline case is that the operator would purchase a new fossil vehicle but with the Program purchases a new electric unit. Emissions of the old unit (if latter continues to operate) are the same in the baseline as in the project case and therefore not accounted for by the Program.

The figure below shows the median carbon grid factor of Program countries for different vehicle categories.

### Impact of PT Measures

The methodology approach to determine the impact of improved PT measures including system improvements as well as new investments in PT systems is based on a Global Environment Facility (GEF) (2015) approach. The analysis estimates the impact caused through mode switch from private means of transport to PT. Estimations of the incremental patronage of PT systems is thereby based on monitored results of already implemented projects. Measures which increase the frequency and capacity of PT systems, as well as measures that improve the quality of PT services (e.g., introducing and/or modernizing electric bus fleets and incentives for preference for PT systems in urban settings) are considered.

### Impact of NMT Measures

The methodology approach to determine the GHG impact of cycling lanes established in the urban development measures of projects is based on a Global Environment Facility approach (GEF, 2015) based on (i) km of cycle-lanes and (ii) number of cyclists. The analysis estimates the impact caused through mode switch from private means of transport to cycling. Estimations of the incremental patronage of cycling lanes are thereby based on monitored results of already implemented projects.

### Impact of Adaptation Interventions

The Program aims to build resilience in the EUTD to ensure cities have resilient transport to face climate hazards and prepare for extreme weather events. Measures to reduce vulnerability to climate risks aim to minimize the interruption of service and maintain the level of safety, frequency, and capacity of the system, while protecting the urban development infrastructure from damage caused by extreme weather conditions.

The impact of adaptation interventions will be reported as the cost required for rehabilitation and improvement of physical assets that will increase the resilience of the EUTD. Adaptation interventions will also be evaluated to determine their contribution towards achieving the overall objective of the Program.


---
**Page 60**
---

The climate resilience framework methodology to each project valorises its physical climate outcomes while placing it in the context of the local, project specific climate risks.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The paradigm shift of the proposed Program is achieved by having long-term outcomes an EV friendly policy environment where successful EU city and national policies have been replicated at the national and regional level; a market transformation towards electric mobility; and a transition to sustainable urban mobility systems with new business models. Accelerated EV investments take place due to experiences made, business models established, regulatory frameworks in place, and public acceptance of EVs. The Program will be implemented by 10 selected commercial EV cities (the countries included in the Program at this moment only have pilot EV fleets) and the improved mobility systems will be replicated across Europe.

The transition towards sustainable urban mobility systems is a key feature of the Program. The transport sector is transformed to a sustainable low carbon and climate resilient urban mobility system with a decrease in air pollution, noise levels and greenhouse gas emissions. The cities participating in the Program are establishing Green Recovery Plans due to the COVID-19 crisis as a vehicle to transition to society by investing in public transport infrastructure and services, electric vehicles, charging infrastructure and other measures that will green the economy. Electrically, sustainable and climate resilient mobility systems are thereby a core feature.

The potential for scaling up and replication is ensured at both the national and the international level through capacity creation and sharing of knowledge and learning. The Program will provide a platform to share experiences and reduce risks of EV investments and knowledge products. The experience of various countries shows that once fleets of EVs are operational, they can be scaled up in terms of number of vehicles and charging infrastructure.

The projects financed under this Program function as trigger projects demonstrating the commercial viability of EVs at scale. They will also provide a platform for sharing experiences on how to overcome barriers to market entry and create a purely commercial base viable. This is linked with decreasing investment costs of EVs reaching cost parity to fossil fuel vehicles, which in turn enables scaling up of investments.

The Program will be implemented through the following components:

- **TA Component**: The TA Component includes capacity building, technical resources for training and monitoring. Technical assistance is used to ensure delivery of adequate training from equipment suppliers or third parties for EV and charger operations and maintenance, safety training and capacity building in urban planning and transport management.

- **Knowledge sharing and learning**: The knowledge sharing component will be implemented through the different countries will be shared via multiple channels under the knowledge management strategy. This will be done at a national level by sharing experiences with other cities within the country and also on an international level by sharing documents and experiences between countries and also with countries outside of Europe. This will include workshops, seminars, webinars, conferences, publications, case studies and cases through channels such as webinar, electronic reports, case-studies, and workshops to disseminate experiences and lessons learned from the Program.

- **Contribution to the creation of an enabling framework**: The TA Component includes the support of enabling policies but also the provision of technical support will be provided to the relevant national and local authorities in order to set up, improve and/or enforce enabling public policies and legal, regulatory, fiscal and/or non-fiscal frameworks for a mobility. This will include the development of urban transport plans, roadmaps for electric vehicles, roadmaps for electrification of taxis, roadmaps for a hydrogen economy and for electrification of trucks as well as vessels. What this means is that the Program will provide support to cities in developing enabling policies and frameworks with investment projects forms a base for more specific targets, intervention instruments and actions allowing to take stock on progress made by the end of each year and to identify new opportunities for investments and initiatives beyond the Program's scope and will help to inform and/or further detail the countries long term mobility strategies.

- **Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies**: The TA Component includes activities such as roadmaps (see above) which outline an agreed upon set of pragmatic and realistic measures to achieve EV sectoral targets. This will include the development of enabling policies, regulations, charging infrastructure standards, pricing policies for EV charging, public transport regulatory aspects including aspects such as concession contracts (concession length, vehicle turnover guarantee), separation or vehicle

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 57

## Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with national climate change and energy strategies

The Program contributes one-quarter of the current global energy-related GHG emissions and is growing faster than any other energy end-use sector. Limiting the global temperature increase to below 2°C will require a significant reduction in the use of fossil fuels, particularly coal. The Program supports projects that are consistent with the Paris Agreement on climate change and the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). It also contributes to the implementation of the Electromobility and Climate Change & Call to Action, calls for the deployment of EVs compatible with a 20% share of electricity from renewable energy sources by 2030. The Program will not support e-bus purchases within Colombia in e-bus projects that limit intermediate cities without electrification.

### D. Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Program has as main input high-road GHG emissions. It contributes significantly to sustainable development goals (SDG) 3 ("good health and well-being"), SDG goal 7 ("affordable and clean energy"), SDG goal 9 ("industry, innovation and infrastructure"), SDG goal 11 ("sustainable cities and communities"), and SDG 13 ("climate action").

### Environmental co-benefits

Major environmental co-benefits are reduced pollutants and noise emissions. The major concern for all pollution in cities is the emission of NOx and PM2.5. The impact of the Program on these pollutants has been quantified based on combustion emissions from new fossil-based vehicles using the EU-CDPert model. This is a conservative approach as it assumes that all new vehicles are fossil-fueled, while the Program promotes the substitution of fossil fuel with an EV.

For the methodology used, the database as well as calculations see Annex 22. The following table shows projected reduction of pollutants per vehicle category:
