# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 58

## Social co-benefits

The major social benefit is improved air quality (thus reduced noise). Air pollution is a major problem in most Latin American cities affecting seriously human health. According to the latest urban air quality database, 98% of cities in low- and middle-income countries with more than 100,000 inhabitants do not meet WHO air quality guidelines. The average level of PM2.5 (particulate matter) is 3 times higher in these cities compared to the WHO guideline value equivalent to 7.6% of all deaths**. The poor are disproportionately affected by air pollution as they tend to live and work closer to sources of pollution, such as industrial plants, power stations, and road traffic. Children under five years old are also affected by poor air quality than men***. The transport sector is thereby a major source of urban air pollution. Road vehicles in Latin America emit 10 times more NOx (nitrogen oxides) per km traveled compared to Europe where diesel vehicles are extensively used (large mileage), have high emission factors and are basically diesel powered.

Other social benefits of the Program are related to increased patronage of public transport, NMT and micro-mobility resulting in less accidentality, less congestion, time savings and improved quality of life in cities. The increased resilience of urban infrastructure and connectivity also results in social co-benefits of people who have access to better services such as improved weather forecasting or distribution of mobile phones and of electricity supply.

The COVID 19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, from the health and economic sectors, but it is also offering new opportunities for an accelerated sustainable transformation of its energy, transport, and urban infrastructure while contributing to economic recovery. A recent report prepared by the International Energy Agency (IEA) estimates that the global economic impact of COVID-19 will be 3% lower than the pre-pandemic scenario due to the reduced impact of savings on carbon on price and maintenance. Reduced demand for fossil fuel use has also led to a reduction in air pollution, which is expected to lead to an increase in life expectancy. The report of McKinsey and Company estimates that the pandemic has raised interest in EVs (electric vehicles) among customers due to the fact now seemingly suddenly being able to experience clean air during lockdown****.

## Economic co-benefits

The monetary value of reduced air emissions (GHGs and air pollutants) has been calculated (see Annex 22 for the methodology and assumptions). The economic cost of pollution is calculated by assigning a monetary value to the emissions of PM10, NOx and CO. For each country based on local level of pollution at the ground level and the impact on health and mortality. The total economic benefits are estimated as follows: $400 million in avoided costs due to air contamination and how increased pollution increases mortality risks using the World Health Organization's dose-response relationship between PM2.5 and mortality. The cost of air pollution is also estimated at $13 billion, which was valued economically on the basis of stated preference studies as performed by the OECD. The global warming potential (GWP) of CO2 emissions has been calculated based on the GWP of 1 kg of CO2 equivalent.

The total economic co-benefits from reduced emissions of the Program are estimated at 400 MUSD out of which 74 MUSD due to reduced air pollutants and avoided health costs and the rest due to reduced GHG emissions.

Additional economic co-benefits not calculated at the Program level are for reduced subsidies to fossil fuels, minor usage of foreign exchange for import of fossil fuels, economic benefits of time savings, accidentality and vehicle operation cost reduction, increased usage of public transportation such as NMT and economic benefits of improved grid and urban infrastructure resilience.

At project level during design project and due diligence the total economic benefits are calculated per project based on the framework and approach of ESD.

# Gender-sensitive development impact

Gender-sensitive and development impact will be described in the gender-disaggregated targets in projects/targets. Each program country will follow the GCF’s policy on gender equality. Individual projects will also be guided by the template gender action plan as included in Annex I. The project includes actions to (i) increase awareness of the importance of socioeconomic inclusion and gender awareness in recovery plans, as part of the post-recovery sector and address their and (ii) include gender activities, specially sex-disaggregated data, in the projects financed with the development of the GCF.

## D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The following barriers hindering a take-up of EVs in the countries involved have been identified:

- High upfront investment;
- Lack of charging infrastructure investments;
- High risk of TVI investments (financial and technical risks);
- Lack of know-how on appropriate system design, business models, financial models and technical know-how;
- First-mover advantage: lack of experience in the country or comparable cities of the country;
- Lack of an enabling policy framework for EV deployment.

IDS has benchmarked costing studies on the different vehicle segments for different prior ranges and countries. The study shows that the cost of electric vehicles is higher than their fossil vehicle cousins in terms of TCO, relative profitability, capital requirements and risks. This again serves as base to structure the financial instruments required to reduce the risk and increase the affordability of EVs.

The benchmark study are that countries which have provided for financial incentives have been able to get on the path of mass market penetration of electric vehicles.

Contrary to renewable energy generation that is increasingly cost competitive, EVs are not yet commercially viable. Commercial EVs in program countries are at the verge of the market growth stage but will not take this step from pilots to commercialization without further support and assistance from the GCF. The transformation towards low carbon transport systems in other sectors is also dependent on the successful implementation of electric mobility. This is more evident for electric buses, which have reached a market stage whereas electric cars are still at the current stage.

Governments and transport operators also lack capacity and know-how on how to appropriate technology choices, the need for infrastructure investments and the ability to build up cost-effective cleaner EV deployment. This is clearly evident from public statements in favor of electric mobility which is however not followed up with concrete actions and implementations.


## D.S Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Section B1 already includes a short overview of countries including the carbon grid factor, population and vehicle data, etc., that are relevant to the GCF’s policy on gender equality and the need for socioeconomic inclusion.

In the framework of the elaboration of the Program Concept Note and the Funding Proposal, IDB has held meetings with key NDAs of the Program countries and key actors in e-mobility to ensure that the FP responds to the country's needs.


### Barbados

The GHG emissions of Barbados is 3.79 MCO2e excl. LUCF’s in 2018. Same year transportation emissions are 4.66 MCO2e (11% of total emissions excl. LUCF). Transport emissions have remained constant since 2010 (total emissions


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

also]) The country’s change risk profile of the country is dominated by coastal and weather effects, especially sea level rise, storm surge, and increased tropical storm and hurricane intensity and frequency. Barbados wants to put its efforts into reducing emissions from transport and energy sectors in order to achieve sustainable development including accelerated investments in green mobility (Government of Barbados, 2021: p. 12).

The updated NDC of Barbados has put as target for 2030 to be a 100% green and fossil-free island state. This is an ambitious goal that will require significant investment in the transport sector, including the electrification of vehicles by the substitution of fossil with zero-carbon emission fuels. The unconditional (conditional) target per 2030 is a reduction of 45% (67%) below 1990 levels and a 50% (68%) below 1990 levels for the period 2021-2030. The NDC also sets out year Rolls to Refuel Program (R2RP) which operationalizes the PDP are the relevant frameworks for Barbados’ resilience plan. The R2RP guides future development of the country’s GEF as a whole. The R2RP has no fixed timeline and is subject to review every 5 years, with the next review in 2026. This will guide the NDCs development. The updated NDC has as target that 100% of electricity produced is renewable by 2030 (as of 2019 67% of electricity was from renewables). The R2RP also sets out a timeline for electrification of vehicles.

Based on standard vehicle replacement rates even if 100% of all newly sold vehicles would be electric by 2025, the country will still have over 40% of its fleet as internal combustion engine (ICE) vehicles in 2030. This means that the transition burden will initially be oil-based. The NDC states that the starting point will be public buses and light duty / passenger vehicles.

The government already operates 50 or buses since August 2020.


## Chile

Chile’s GHG emissions excl. LUCF are 1,130 MTCO₂e. The emissions from the transport sector in the same year are 28MCO₂e (or 26% of total GHG emissions). Transport emissions have grown since the year 2000 on average annually by 4%. Chile has a National Strategy for Electrification of Vehicles (SENVE), which is part of the National Energy and Electricity Strategy, a document realized jointly by the Ministry of Energy, the Ministry of Transport and Telecommunications, and the Ministry of Environment. The SENVE sets out goals to promote the electrification of mobility. Electrification is currently being updated. Chile has also a National Green Hydrogen Strategy (MinEN, 2020). The objectives are to reduce emissions from transport by promoting the use of hydrogen in vehicles and to increase electric mobility by 2040 which would also result in higher GHG emission reductions of electric mobility (the 2018 carbon goal is to have 5% of all new cars sold be electric by 2030). The SENVE sets out a set of goals for the electrification of vehicles, including: (i) increase the number of charging stations; (ii) a set of transport related goals and measures to lower the sector’s emissions, namely electric mobility, shifting modes and reducing vehicle kilometers traveled (VKT); (iii) reduce the use of fossil fuels in public transportation. Chile has also climate pollutants. This can be achieved well with a mobile. With electric mobility the following targets are set: (i) 10% of all new cars sold will be electric by 2030; (ii) 50% of all new cars sold will be electric by 2040, and commercial, shall be electric by 2050. For hydrogen the targets are that 71% of cargo transport shall use green hydrogen by 2030 and 98% by 2050. The SENVE also sets out a set of goals for the electrification of vehicles including buses and taxis, and as of mid-2021 more than 800 buses operating primarily in Santiago de Chile.


## Colombia

Colombia’s GHG emissions for 2014 are estimated at 327 MTCO₂e. Transportation emissions (or 39% MCO₂e, 31% of the total) have grown with a growth of over 20%. Colombia’s NDC Update estimates that according to the reference scenario for 2030 emissions would reach 346 MTCO₂e. Within the mitigation goal Colombia commits to emit a maximum of 578 MTCO₂e in 2030, which is equivalent to an emission reduction target of -41% compared with the transport sector amongst others offers to achieve 600,000 registered electric taxis, buses, light commercial vehicles and heavy-duty trucks. Colombia also commits to promote the electrification of public transportation services such as compliance with a minimum quota of 30% of EVs in new acquisitions or contracts, taking into account the commercial offer in Colombia. According to the same law, the goals for the incorporation of EVs is that by 2025 at least 10% of all new cars sold will be electric and by 2030 at least 40%. The NDC also sets out proportions of 15% in 2022, 20% in 2027, 40% in 2029, 60% in 2031, 80% in 2033 and 100% in 2035. Congress of Colombia has approved the National Strategy for Electrification of Vehicles (SENVE) which is part of the National Energy and Electricity Mobility, which aims to promote the electrification of the transport sector. In addition to the above, the

### Costa Rica

Total GHG emissions of Costa Rica are estimated at 10.9 million tCO₂e in 2019 with land transport being responsible for more than 50% of emissions. Emissions under a BAU scenario are expected to increase by 45% by 2030. The updated NDC of Costa Rica includes as target an emissions of 8.1 million tCO₂e by 2030 and holds to the net zero target by 2050. The government has set a target for electric vehicles (EVs) in public transport, which is considered essential and a national priority. The updated NDC has quantified 2030 electric mobility targets for public transport, passenger cars, motorcycles, buses, and trucks. Costa Rica has also developed a National Strategy Plan for Electric Mobility which includes the development of charging infrastructure to support EVs. The government has set a target that by 2050 all new vehicles will be electric. Costa Rica has also developed a national plan for electric transport which includes the development of charging infrastructure and incentives for private EVs.

### Dominican Republic

In 2015, the gross GHG emissions of the Dominican Republic were 35 million tCO₂e (Gobierno de la República Dominicana, 2020) of which 35% (7.7 million tCO₂e) from the transport sector*. The updated version of the NDC includes a target for electric vehicles in public transport and private cars. The Dominican Republic has committed to GHG emissions in relation to a BAU scenario by 2030. In terms of electric mobility, the NDC-PR proposes: (i) promotion of electric vehicles in public transport; (ii) development of charging infrastructure for electric vehicles with electric and hybrid vehicles; (iii) introduction of electric buses for school transportation service; (iv) Regulation and creation of incentives to promote the use of electric vehicles by private individuals. The Dominican Republic adopted a National Strategy Plan for Electric Mobility (INTRANT, 2020) developed by INTRANT in collaboration with the IDB. The plan provides for a short-, medium- and long-term transformation of the transportation sector. The plan includes the development of charging infrastructure to support EVs. The Dominican Republic Ministry of Economy, Planificación y Desarrollo (2015) and aims at all modes of road transportation. The updated NDC has set targets for electric vehicles in public transport and private cars. The share of the private sector is 14%, 100% public chargers shall be operational by 2020. By 2030 the respective goals are 100% EVs in public transport and 50% of all private vehicles will be electric.

### Jamaica

Jamaica’s 2018 GHG emissions excl. LUCF are estimated at 19.5 MTCO₂e with transportation accounting for 23.5 MTCO₂e or 23% of total emissions. Emissions have grown since 2000 by annually on average 4.1%. whilst total emissions increased from 17.8 to 19.5 MTCO₂e. The updated NDC has set a target for electric vehicles in public transport and private cars. Jamaica’s NDC includes an unconditional target relative to a BAU scenario; Jamaica plans to increase its share of renewables in electricity generation by 2030. The country is also committed to increasing the use of renewable energy sources in the Caribbean. The adoption of EVs in the sector however aligns with the National Energy Policy goals for reducing GHG emissions and improving air quality through increased energy efficiency, electrification and utilization. Jamaica Public Service is deploying charging infrastructure and the Jamaican Agency for Utility Regulation is developing a regulatory framework to support the deployment of electric vehicles. The Ministry of Industry, Investment and Technology proposes a target of EVs up to 10% per cent of the mix by 2030 (around 50,000 vehicles).

### Panama

Panama’s 2018 GHG emissions excl. LUCF are estimated at 18 MTCO₂e with transportation accounting for 4.8 MTCO₂e or 27% of total emissions. Emissions have grown since 2000 by annually on average 3.4%. whilst total emissions have increased annually on the same period by 3.25%. The updated NDC has set a target of

## Engagement with Civil Society

In each country GCF has performed multiple interviews and meetings with major stakeholders including national and local governments, energy utilities, transport operators and civil society.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Program introduces a concessional financing mechanism that addresses barriers to the uptake of commercial EVs in developing countries. The main barrier is the high cost of investment in EVs compared with conventional vehicles. Concessional financial pricing is critical – in absence of concessional GCF funds such projects will materialize on the short or medium term. The e-mobility report (Annex 2a) provides a detailed analysis of the barriers to commercial EV adoption and proposes a range of policy instruments in the form of grants to kick-start EV deployment.

The main financial barriers for the potential beneficiaries which present alternative financing options are: (i) the low liquidity of EVs; (ii) high upfront costs, especially for long-range vehicles and very long payback times; (iii) the high risk profile of EV investments due to performance risks, asset risks (e.g. a sale value of assets), and replacement investments (batteries); (iv) lack of experience in financing EVs; and (v) limited access to finance from commercial banks and other investing entities. Commercial financing is thus not willing to enter this market. Conventional financial institutions are also not able to provide the long-term funding required for EV projects, which typically have a payback period of 10 years or more.

The Program aims at achieving substantial reduction in the need for concessional financing in future project. It is expected that the Program will be self-financing and will generate sufficient revenue to cover its own costs. The Program will provide for technical assistance to design and structure an optimal e-mobility investments and reduce design and implementation risks.

Projects involved in the proposed Program will not be dependent on a continuous flow of GCF funds. Rather, the Program aims at achieving substantial reduction in the need for concessional financing in future project. It is expected that the Program will be self-financing and will generate sufficient revenue to cover its own costs. The use of GCF funds de-risks similar future investments and decreases the risk profile of projects.

The FIRR and ERR of individual projects are determined during the project preparation and the due diligence phase. IBI will assess the financial soundness of each project. The Program EIRR is estimated at 20% (see Annex 3).

The NDCs of countries increasingly include targets for PT as well as improving PT. However, programs are often not lined with a regulatory framework and thus lack a business proposal that addresses these goals. Also, investments in mass transport systems are taking place in many countries; however they are often not linked up with the integration of NMT and micro-mobility. Most include climate resilient measures thus remaining focused on pure infrastructure investments with limited behavioral or paradigm change.

The program will work with local and national institutions in developing strategies for creating resilient transport infrastructure. It will also provide technical advice in designing and implementing business models that are conducive in encouraging the adoption of EVs.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Monitoring for the Program will be in line with IDB’s policies and the terms of the AMAFAA. Specifically, the implementation of each project will be managed and monitored at project and Program level by IDB's in-house staff, project management unit and procured consultants. As specified in Loan and Grant Agreements between IDB and its clients, monitoring will include reporting on the progress and results of the Program and the environmental and social performance of the project to the IDB on an annual basis, in line with IDB’s standard reporting requirements.

**Program Level**

IDB will prepare a Program level Annual Performance Reports (APRs) in the format specified by GCF detailing:
a) activities conducted during the year; status of implementation; potential issues and solutions;
b) progress against targets and indicators given in this proposal (see above).

The APRs will be reviewed by an independent panel, including rationale, description and screening against the criteria set out in this proposal. Impact potential estimates will be supported by a methodology note.

**Project Level**

A monitoring manual has been realized to provide a guideline on how to determine the impact of projects (Annex 1).

Evaluations

A mid-term review of the program will be conducted in implementation year 3 to take stock of emerging results from the TA activities, its portfolio distribution, project impacts and implementation of the first phase of investments under the program c) performance on safeguards and gender and social inclusion. This will be an independent review, undertaken by an external team engaged for this purpose. Based on the review, an updated Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan (GSIAAP) will be prepared and finalized.

A final program evaluation will be conducted after the end of the implementation period to evaluate program results and impacts.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Credit default risk

| Category | Category | Category |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Credit | Credit |
| Description | Inability of lender to service debt |

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

All loans from the program will be aligned with IIB loans, such that the credit or loan-default risk on repayment of program loans is low. To further ensure that all projects under this program are financially sustainable themselves and don't create a further burden on the lender, an economic and financial analysis of each project (with exception of hydroelectric power plants) will be conducted at the project level as part of the due diligence process for selection of Projects. The Program explicitly focus on developing financially bankable models at the project level itself.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: EV technology risk

| Category | Category | Category |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Technical and operational | Technical and operational |

**Description:** EWS do not perform as expected and the operator cannot realize standard operations.

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

IIB's comprehensive technical design and structuring of projects minimizes this risk. All technologies have been proven at least in pilot cases in other countries.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Lack of alignment of ESS

| Category | Category | Category |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Technical and operational | Technical and operational |

**Description:** Beneficiaries' failure to comply with national regulations and/or IIB and GCF environmental, social policy, gender requirements and other relevant standards.

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

All projects shall be proposed and implemented in compliance with IIB's Safeguard Policy Statement, which may be updated from time to time. Each project will have a safeguard component structured to meet IIB’s and GCF’s requirements. Environmental, Social and Gender Action Plans will be developed as part of the due diligence process during project selection. The implementation of such safeguards requirements will be co-ordinated through financing agreements, monitored by IIB.

### Selected Risk Factor 4: Design risk

| Category | Category | Category |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Other | Other |

**Description:** Limited capacity or experience to design and implement e-mobility projects by beneficiaries.

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

**Page 86**
---

Technical assistance will focus on supporting entities to identify, design and structure e-mobility projects. Detailed legal and technical due diligence will be carried out by the ICB to develop robust projects. Support will be provided under the Program to assist in developing favourable policies for deployment of e-mobiles.

## Selected Risk 5: Best practices

| Category | Prohibited practices |
| --- | --- |
| Category | Prohibited practices |

### Risk of projects being involved in illicit practices (money laundering, terrorist financing or other prohibited practices)

**Mitigation Measure(s):**

GCF funding will be provided as loans or grants to sovereign and sovereign-backed entities. As GCF funds are not provided alongside co-financing from other sources, the prohibition on undue diligence will apply at project level, which will also mitigate this risk. All projects shall comply with ICB’s Anti-corruption Policy and acknowledge that EU anti-corruption legislation applies in accordance with Article 12 of the Agreement. The ICB may impose any coercive practice relating to the projects, and recipients of the grant/loan shall cooperate with any such investigation and/or enforcement action. The ICB will ensure that all project documentation is reviewed for compliance with procedures, prior to the approval of each specific project; its fiduciary team will perform and/or update an ongoing risk assessment on a regular basis in accordance with the ICB’s Anti-corruption Policy, considering adequacy of information systems, internal and external controls, and recommendations on any fiduciary risks certified.

The Office of Institutional Integrity of the ICB (OII) plays a key role in the ICB’s integrity efforts. OII is an independent office responsible for preventing, detecting, investigating, and reporting prohibited practices, including fraud, misappropriation of funds, in all activities financed by the ICB. OII’s investigative team is responsible for conducting investigations into allegations of prohibited practices received from various sources as described below in Prohibited Practices. OII follows the Principles and Guidelines for Investigations, incorporated in the Uniform Code of Military Justice (UCMJ), and other applicable laws and regulations. OII also works closely with the ICB’s Anti-Corruption Task Force. OII has different channels to report allegations of prohibited practices, including an independent reporting hotline available 24/7 and an online form to report allegations of prohibited practices.

In addition, OII’s preventive team provides advisory services to project teams to identify, assess, and mitigate integrity risks in the design and implementation phases of projects. To ensure that all integrity risks are properly identified, received, and operational measures need to be taken as part of its preventive activities, OII carries out training for project teams on integrity risk management. The ICB has established a comprehensive framework and managing integrity risk in ICB-financed operations, executing agencies, and other external stakeholders.

Internally, the Office of Ethics of the ICB investigates ethical violations by Bank staff and ensures compliance with financial disclosure requirements, including completion of the annual declaration of interests or affidavit form for all Bank staff. The Office of Ethics also investigates allegations of prohibited practices by ICB staff, such as theft, embezzlement, bribery, fraud, and other forms of corruption.

The ICB has strong mechanisms to protect whistleblowers. The ICB’s Whistleblower Policy was approved by the Board of Directors of the ICB in 2011 and further strengthened in 2012. The ICB’s policy on whistleblower protection ensures that employees who report prohibited practices are protected from retaliation, including through Prohibited Practices or cooperate with Bank authorities in investigations, audits, or other inquiries. This policy also establishes the measures the ICB will use to prevent retaliation against employees and external parties that make a report.

None of the project activities will be undertaken in any jurisdiction which is subject to or affected by United Nations Security Council (UNSC) resolutions. The UNSC has established a list of countries, territories, or areas where it establishes that firms of a country or goods manufactured in a country may be excluded if by an act of compliance with a decision of the United Nations Security Council taken under Chapter VII of the Charter of the United Nations.

**Page 87**

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 83

#### Selected Risk Factor 7: Procurement
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Description:** Lack of compliance with procurement policies or limited capacity in procurement issues.

**Mitigation Measures:** All procurement to be financed under the program must be carried out in accordance with IDB's procurement policy and guidelines, and will be subject to a procurement capacity assessment by the Borrower and IDB. Procurement training, procurement capacity assessment and market analysis will be carried out in accordance with the IDB Procurement Guidelines. All consultants will be engaged following the IDB Guidelines on the Use of Consultants.

#### Selected Risk Factor 8: Forex
**Category:** Technical  
**Description:** Volatile local currencies versus the USD loans from the Program create financial viability stresses on underlying projects.

**Mitigation Measures:** All lending from the Program and IDB will be assessed for financial risks in project specific financial models which include sensitivity analysis to assess the impact of exchange rate fluctuations. The Borrower must ensure that project finances are able to cope with any future currency volatility. In addition, most sovereign loan projects, the selection of local currency loans, and the use of foreign currency denominated loans in local currencies also provide adequate cover on this risk, so sovereigns better able to match currency outflows and inflows.

#### Selected Risk Factor 9: Co-financing
**Probability:** Low  
**Description:** Co-financing from IDB or the national part does not materialize.  

IDB co-financing will be identified for every project due to all projects being selected through IDB's country policy dialogue with developing member countries to determine IDB funding allocations on selected priority projects to be supported by IDB financing, subject to sub-project eligibility criteria requiring minimum co-financing shares from IDB and/or national / other parties.

G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

IBD’s Safeguard Policy (SP) is a consolidated policy covering environment, involuntary resettlement, and indigenous peoples. It provides guidance on how to identify, assess, and mitigate environmental, social, and cultural risks for all projects. An environmental and social due diligence at appraisal helps IBD decide if the project should be financed and, if so, the way in which environmental and social impacts can be avoided or mitigated. The SP is based on international best practices and follows the approval process also identifies opportunities for additional environmental or social benefits. IBD seeks that projects are designed to avoid adverse effects on people and their environment while maximizing positive impacts. The SP sets out principles, rules, procedures, and guidelines for conducting environmental and social impact assessments (ESIA) and ESIA monitoring and evaluation. It provides a framework to describe the process for developing measures to avoid and mitigate potential adverse impacts as well as opportunities to improve the project’s design and implementation.

IBD is committed to the principles of transparency, accountability, and stakeholder engagement, and promoting adoption and implementation of these principles by its clients. Proportionate to the nature and scale and environmental and social risks associated with a project, IBD will engage in dialogue with stakeholders on the risks and impacts arising from projects, engage with stakeholders in a meaningful, effective, inclusive and constructive manner throughout the project cycle.

Each project under the Program will require a Stakeholder Engagement Plan, including a grievance mechanism. IBD will disclose on its website appropriate information on environmental and social safeguards aspects and due diligence for each project.

IBD works very closely with governments, authorities and civil society, to uphold the principle of country ownership via direct partnerships (regional offices). Via IBD’s Resident Mission officers in the field and via the country and sector strategies, IBD will engage with stakeholders on the risks and impacts arising from projects. The SP requires that IBD consults with stakeholders including amongst others the energy sector, electric utilities, the transport sector, and other relevant sectors. In addition to the above, IBD will also engage in dialogue with local communities where appropriate. For projects involving resettlement or land acquisition, IBD has undertaken extensive dialogues and discussions on intervention options with the selected developing countries’ national authorities and stakeholders. The SP provides guidance for conducting ESIA and ESIA monitoring and evaluation.

G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

A gender assessment has been realized for all countries as well as a gender action plan. This serves as guideline for each individual project to be funded under the Program and as a framework to develop a gender approach for the Program.

The outlook to both keeping the progress and continue reducing the gaps in gender equality is challenging after the COVID-19 pandemic. UN Women and other international organizations fear that recent gains in gender equality might be reversed, especially among women living in poverty or with disabilities. The UN estimates that 20% of women globally are at risk of violence during the pandemic.

Gender based violence has increased and women in lockdowns are living with their aggressor under the same roof as before. According to a study by the World Bank, 15% of women in India have experienced domestic violence since March 2020, up from 9%, adding that, to figures actual gender-based violence were worse during COVID-19 because of the fear of getting infected with the virus and the lack of access to health services. The UN estimates that 38 million women worldwide lead their household’s dynamics during the pandemic, with severe lockdowns in most countries in the region and schools and workplaces closed; while access to decent work has deteriorated for all.

According to UN Habitat, two-thirds of the population will be living in cities by 2050 (UN Habitat, 2018). Together with poverty and lack of education, this trend is expected to increase as well. The increase that the urbanization population is exacerbating. Lack of access to transportation and safety issues limits women’s participation in the labor market and


---
**Page 89**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 85

reduces their participation in the economy by 16.5%. Women turn away working opportunities depending on the traveling conditions: the work space (time spent, return timing, expenses). Besides providing access to education, health and jobs, the sector generates jobs itself but traditionally most opportunities are likely to remain in men's hands. The gender gap is also reflected in the transport system design. Gender differences exist in terms of safety features in the design of the transport system can constrain a woman’s decision to choose a certain mode of transportation. Women may be more concerned about their personal safety than men, and this concern often comes to the design of the transport infrastructures; the realities of women, the elderly and people with disabilities are not always considered when designing public transport systems. Gender differences also exist in terms of time between gender and urban transportation. There are gender differences in the purpose, time, mode of transportation, or travel distance for men and women. Women have fewer opportunities to travel long distances or work opportunities, and the differences comprised in this section are shared among the countries in the region.

The investments projects of the Program need to include a gender strategy with actions to increase women's mobility, promote safety and access to jobs. This Program can improve women’s agency in mobility if maintaining gender mainstreaming throughout its implementation. The pandemic has had an impact on all sectors; however, the COVID-19 pandemic is exacerbating the numbers. The urban transport system represents an environment where human interaction is unavoidable. Women are more likely than men to be affected by this situation: they use public transportation more often and for longer distances, they walk or cycle less frequently, they ride in buses, in the e-bus, while walking, waiting, to and from the stop of using charging stations; incidents will increase as a result of the pandemic. The pandemic has also had an impact on women’s access to jobs and employment: jobs generated by the construction and transport sector in the region. The pandemic complicates the return to work for women who have been out of the labor market, and it is expected that there will be a significant opportunity for women in these eight countries to access new jobs that will be generated. The investment projects for this Program need to include an increased ride-hailing market which is growing in the region, and adapt to vulnerable groups without access to technology.

The Gender Action Plan guarantees that these general recommendations are transformed into specific activities at the Program and project levels, and that they are measured and adequately monitored, and adjusted when needed.


## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max 500 words; approximately | page)

Financial resources from the GCF will be managed according to the general provisions of the AMA. DB has established the GCF Trust Fund ("GCF Account") internally, through which all GCF resources under this Program will be transferred to the IDB. The GCF Account is a separate account that will be used exclusively for GCF projects. Based on such sub-project disbursement requests, the IDB will request, for each underlying transaction, the commitment of GCF resources to benefit in the GCF Account as a specific sub-project.

The disbursements and reporting requirements are subject to the applicable GCF policies and procedures including reporting requirements, would follow IIBA policies and procedures, and applicable AMA and FAA requirements, which would be reflected as part of the Project Implementation Plan (PIP). The IDB will establish the disbursement period for eligible activities under the applicable components of the Program, would establish the disbursement period and set eligibility/implementation requirements, including regarding the executing structure of GCF funded activities within the EE.

The disbursements, reporting (including external audit monitoring), monitoring, and evaluation of the Project will be done in accordance with the IDB’s policies and procedures, among others the IDB’s Financial Management Guidelines (CP73-6) and reflected in the Term Sheet and FAA.

Disbursement of GCF resources to the IUB under the F&A would be subject to the IUB having approved the allocation of such funds. The disbursements will be made following the applicable procedures for concessional cooperation assistance and investment grants operations and following the effectiveness of the corresponding Subsidiary Agreements for each individual operation. F&A agreements are needed.

Loan resources will be disbursed to the corresponding EE as an advance of funds or as reimbursement of expenses at any time within the established loan disbursement period, in accordance with IIB policies. Proceeds discussed as repayment or expansion that were already made by the EE in accordance with the terms of the Subsidy Agreement will not be freed up by the EE in accordance with its mandate and for purposes that may be unrelated to the corresponding Sub-Project.

EEs will be required to submit audited financial statements within 120 days after the closing of each fiscal year (month) or, if later, upon request from the GCF. The audited financial statements must be prepared in accordance with independent firm acceptable to the GCF and in compliance with local regulation. EEs are also required to prepare and submit a report on the progress made by the EE towards achieving the development objectives set out for each sub-tasks, as well as progress with regards to the development objectives based on a set of predefined indicators (outputs). These reports will provide the basis upon which the GCF will have to decide for the GCF Account in favour of the AMA.

When acting as EE, IIB shall apply its policies for hiring of individual consultants and/or procurement of consulting services. The same applies when acting as other EEs to use application procedures and procedures for their use of GCF resources.

In accordance with the AMA, the GCF Account will be subject to an external audit requirement. No later than June 30 in each year, the IIB will deliver to the GCF an assertion report by the DB management and a report by the Bank's external auditor on the effectiveness of internal controls and on the accuracy of the consolidated financial statements for all trust funds under IIB's administration as at December 31. The Bank, together with the combined financial statements for all trust funds for the previous calendar year. Such audited financial statements are prepared in accordance with the applicable accounting standards and practices and subject to external audit reporting, as updated from time to time. The cost of such audits will be deducted from the resources of the Fund.

## G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all documents and information submitted by the Board to the GCF. However, certain confidential information contained within programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board. The following table sets out the categories of information that may be considered confidential under the IDP. Disclosure is not an absolute right; rather it is a presumption in favour of disclosure. In exceptional circumstances, where there is a reasonable belief that disclosure would cause significant harm or prejudice to the interests of the GCF, confidentiality is one of exceptions but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

- ☐ No confidential information. The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes**, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- ☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF; as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is required to provide a Disclosure Form (see below) and submit it with the funding proposal.

** Full copy for internal use of the GCF if which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the above conditions.
- ☐ redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

** Funding Proposal is public. List of confidential annexes is included in the Disclosure Form - Funding proposal annexes

# Secretariat's assessment of FP189

## Proposal name: E-Mobility Program for Sustainable Cities in Latin America and the Caribbean

### Accredited entity: Inter-American Development Bank (IDB)

### Country/ies: Barbados, Belize, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay, Uruguay

### Project/Program size: Large

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| Program serves nine countries in the Latin America and Caribbean (LAC) region with expanding e-mobility in public transportation. It provides concessional loans and grants to support cities in building public fleets as well as grid and infrastructure resilience. | Executing entity capacity to deliver on a strong pipeline of projects, subject to oversight by the accredited entity (AE), as required by the accreditation master agreement. The Inter-American Development Bank has extensive experience in supporting countries in Latin America and the Caribbean to manage this role well. Moreover, a robust indicative pipeline has been prepared and communicated with the Board. |
| Program prioritizes secondary cities and promotes innovative vehicle-to-grid and e-vehicle charging infrastructure, which are most investment flows into e-mobility in LAC. | The Program's methodology for estimating adaptation beneficiaries reached needs some further development. This will be subject to review the appraisal and report to GCF in the medium larger of the program. |
| Program integrates adaptation and resilience, including disaster and climate risk screening into transport planning and project designs, with a strong emphasis on gender-sensitive development and the introduction of electrified urban transport systems across the LAC region. | Strong potential impact from GCF and co-financed funds based on indicative pipeline of subprojects. |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum VI titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations", respectively.


---
**Page 110**
---

# Summary of the Secretary's assessment

## Programme background

a. The E-Mobility Program for Sustainable Cities in Latin America and the Caribbean (the Program) links electric vehicle (EV) deployment with sustainable low-carbon urban development and increased climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure and the grid (i.e., inter-sectoral integration). It is implemented by GCF in partnership with Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay. It targets sustainable urban mobility solutions that reduce greenhouse gas emissions from road transport, improve multimodal transport, electri-micro-mobility measures to increase the quality of life city centres and reduce air pollution. The Program also aims at reducing exposure to climate-related risks associated with more intense precipitation and flooding, heat waves, sea level rise and extreme weather.

b. The Programme has a strong and clearly articulated climate objective: Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) is among the most urbanized regions in the world with over 80 per cent of its population living in cities. It also contains some of the largest metropolitan areas, with an estimated 6–8 per cent living in areas that are at a high or very high risk of being affected by climate-related disasters. In LAC, floods and flash floods, droughts and heatwaves account for 93 per cent of all disasters that took place in the region over the last 20 years (World Bank, 2018). The frequency and intensity of these events are expected to rise as a result of climate change. In addition, LAC is also vulnerable to extreme weather such as hurricanes and extreme heat (e.g., major heatwaves affected the region, especially in many cities of South America, during summer 2021). These risks are exacerbated by climate change, with the United States of Caribbean, which are rising at a slightly higher rate than the global average (3.1 millimetres per year), experiencing Category 4 hurricanes like Eta and Iota affecting over 8 million people in Central America in 2020).

c. As climate-change impacts continue to evolve and intensify, urban transport infrastructure and systems will need to be adapted to reduce the risks of climate-related risks comprehensively managed. The Program aims to do so via its four components: (i) climate-resilient urban mobility solutions; (ii) electric vehicle deployment in public transport fleets and vehicle-to-grid (V2G) pilot projects; (iii) electrified integrated urban mobility, and (iv) technical assistance and capacity building.

d. The Program aims to steer a general shift towards low-carbon, climate-resilient, sustainable and attractive public transport systems, which can reverse the declining public transport mode share trend in Latin America and the Caribbean. It also seeks to promote the deployment of commercial e-mobility, including buses, taxis, institutional fleets, trucks and vessels. Private usage of vehicles such as private passenger cars and motorcycles are not supported.

e. The Program is implemented by GCF in partnership with Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay countries, with replication within and beyond its initial set of countries. The Program also creates added value through the development of a robust institutional framework, the creation of skills and competences needed in the workforce, and the establishment of a critical mass of commercial e-mobility stakeholders. It is implemented by GCF through simple aggregation of individual projects.

f. The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB), as an accredited entity for GCF, is seeking USD 145 million in non-reimbursable grants from GCF. In addition, GCF is requested to provide USD 55 million in non-reimbursable grants for investment subsidies for


---
**Page 111**
---

# Component-by-component analysis

The Program is structured in 4 components: component 1: Increase resilience of urban transport infrastructure (total cost USD 97 million; GCF cost USD 58 million with USD 36 million in loans and USD 17 million in grants); component 2: Increase climate resilience of the grid with H2 and V2G pilot projects (total cost USD 40 million, GCF cost USD 25 million with USD 19.5 million in loans and USD 5.5 million in grants); component 3: Electrified integrated urban mobility (total cost USD 285 million; GCF cost USD 87 million with USD 60 million in loans and USD 27 million in grants); and component 4: Increase climate resilience of the grid with H2 and V2G pilot projects (total cost USD 13.9 million, GCF cost USD 10.5 million).

This component addresses barriers to limited commercial attractiveness of e-mobility and performance risks of EVs. It includes specific investments in adaptation such as V2G and infrastructure for peak savings, which are both aimed at increasing grid resilience. The outcome is reduced greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from combustion engines and increased public transport and non-motorized transportation measures to foster mode shift to low-carbon transport.

The activities of this component are (2.1.1) H2 projects with Fuel Cell Electric Vehicles and with grid stabilization measures implemented; and (2.1.2) V2G projects with grid stabilization measures implemented, which work along the entire value chain of the hydrogen economy, including production, storage, distribution, and use in transportation.

The activities are (3.1.1) Construction of cycle lanes and micromobility measures; (3.1.2) Implementation of urban mobility measures such as pedestrianization, bus


---
**Page 112**
---

# GCF (33/19)/AM081

## Component 4: Technical assistance and project structuring (total cost: USD 13 million; GCF cost: USD 2 million)

### 35. This component addresses the barriers of (i) business models and policies non-conducive to e-mobility deployment; (ii) limited know-how on climate resilience measures in urban transport systems; and (iii) high upfront costs and unclear operational costs and benefits. It also addresses the performance risk of high upfront costs.

#### 36. The outcome of this component is (4) improved public and private sector capacity and regulatory environment for e-mobility. The output of this component is (4.1) A climate-resilient e-mobility framework in LAC region, with a focus on urban transport systems; and (4.2) Improved policy and regulatory environment for e-mobility.

##### 37. The programme management cost (PMC) is shared between IDB and GCF in proportion to the two organizations' grant contribution to the overall Programme, in accordance with GCF's Policy on fees.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 18. The Program is in broad alignment with the GCF mandate and intended for transformational action in low-carbon emission transport and planning in the LAC region, using the Avoid-Shift-Improve model. The Program is also consistent with the draft GCF guidance on urban transport systems. The Program complements other initiatives already under way in the LAC region, including potential readiness for a mobility. A detailed assessment of performance against investment criteria is described in the following.

#### 31. Impact potential

##### 38. The expected fund-level impacts for the total investment programme are rated as High. The Project is expected to contribute 75,426,002 tons of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) of direct emissions reductions and 9 million tonnes of CO2e avoided by reducing energy consumption in transport. This represents a reduction of 13 per cent of the total projected GPM2.5 and 8.190 tones of nitrous oxide (NOx) reduced. Economic benefits due to reduced emissions include USD 4 billion in health care costs avoided, USD 67 million in avoided road pollution. As its adaptation impact, the Program reaches 1.5 million direct and 8.9 million indirect beneficiaries.

##### 39. Transport contributes almost one-third of all global energy-related GHG emissions and grows faster than any other energy end-use sector. Transport-related GHG emissions already accounts for 10 per cent of global GHG emissions as of today. Transport-related GHG emissions in the LAC region are projected to increase by an average of 25 per cent between now and 2030, with 26 per cent in Barbados, 26 per cent in Chile, 16 per cent in Colombia, 37 per cent in Costa Rica, 18 per cent in the Dominican Republic, 19 per cent in Jamaica, 27 per cent in Panama, 19 per cent in


---
**Page 113**
---

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: High**

22. A paradigm shift is achieved via the development of the policy environment for EV, which can be adopted by and replicated at the national and regional level; a mature EV ecosystem; a charging infrastructure network; and an enabling regulatory framework, including fiscal and business models. The Program considers that transformative shifts in mobility towards e-mobility are possible only if all these elements are in place. However, many of them are excluded from the Program at this moment only have pilot fleets. Moreover, an improved understanding of the potential for EVs to contribute to climate change mitigation is required for technical assistance and capacity building, as anticipated in the Program’s activities.

23. The potential for scaling up and replication is ensured at the national and international level through (i) the development of a policy environment that supports the adoption of EV models; (ii) demonstrating improved profitability and reduced risks of EVs development; and (iii) developing an enabling regulatory framework, including fiscal and business models. This will ensure delivery of adequate training from equipment suppliers or third parties on EV and change in behavior towards e-mobility. The technical assistance component includes the support of enabling policies for EV deployment. Technical support will be provided to the national and local authorities in order to set up, improve and enhance the regulatory framework that supports the development and deployment of e-mobility. The Program therefore contributes significantly to the creation of a regulatory environment conducive to the adoption of EVs and helps to speed up the urban and transport development trajectories of the participating countries.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

24. The Program’s main migration-oriented target is to reduce transport-led GHG emissions and increase the adaptation and resilience of urban transport systems. The Program's adaptations will contribute significantly towards SDGs 7 (Affordable and clean energy), 11 (Sustainable cities and communities), and SDG 13 (Climate action).

25. Moreover, the Program contributes to reducing air pollution in cities by addressing major concerns for air pollution in cities: PM2.5 and NOx emissions. Additional non-quantified environmental co-benefits are reduced noise emissions and reduced water pollution caused by fossil fuel use.

26. The Program's major social benefit is improved air quality and reduced noise pollution. High levels of air pollution caused by the transport sector are a major problem for human health


---
**Page 114**
---

and well-being in most Latin American cities and will be addressed via cleaner and less-polluting transport options supported by the Program. Other social benefits of the Program are related to increased patronage of public transport and increased usage of non-motorized transport modes such as walking and cycling, which have been shown to improve quality of life for commuters and residents in cities. The increased resilience of urban infrastructure to climate change is a social co-benefit for people who would otherwise be affected extremely by extreme weather.

The Program's economic co-benefits arise from reduced emissions and are estimated at USD 48 billion over the lifetime of the Program, which includes avoided health costs and the rest is due to reduced GHG emissions. Additional economic co-benefits of the Program are for reduced emissions to fossil fuels, reduced usage of foreign exchange, lower operating costs related to increased patronage of public transport, lower fuel consumption in motorized transport, and economic benefits of improved grid and urban infrastructure resilience.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient
Scale: High

28 GCF support is assessed as being critical to the development of the financing environment for the EV sector in LAC. Most Program countries have limited experience in e-mobility and are not yet able to access private sector funding, including loans and government subsidies. GCF concessional human resources and technical assistance grants are required to help these countries develop a financing framework and identify alternative sources of financing for urban and transport sector projects and programmes.

29 The major financial barriers for the potential beneficiaries that prevent alternative financing from being accessed include: (i) high interest rates on loans; (ii) low financial internal rate of return and very long payback times; (iii) the high-risk profile of EVs, which are considered to be a new technology with limited track record in replacement investments (e.g. batteries); and (iv) high capital and investor’s capital demand resulting in high debt and risk exposure levels of the investing entity. Commercial funding is thus not willing to enter this market due to the lack of profitability and performance risks, while investors interested are but critical of the lack of profitability and/or the high-risk profile.

30 GCF support will help countries develop a financing framework for EVs and EV technologies and to deliver a Program which realizes investments combined with technical assistance. Guarantees do not resolve the EV barriers of lack of profitability and performance risks. All financial instruments are required to be aligned with the Program objectives and expand e-mobility in cities. The Program provides the financial and technical assistance support to help operationalize this vision.

### 3.5 Country ownership
Scale: Medium to high

31 IIB has held meetings with the national designated authorities (NDAs) of the Program countries to assess their capacity in sensitivity to ensure that the Program responds appropriately to needs. No-objection letters (NOLs) have been obtained from all Program countries as evidence of the NDA’s commitment and readiness for the Program. The NOLs are based on a set of factor, population and vehicle data, energy prices and major EV policies of each country served by the Program's outputs and outcomes. The Program is designed to support the climate policy objectives of the Program countries in their efforts to reduce emissions while moving out of the transport sector. The Program also responds to country ambitions regarding the building of resilience and adaptive capacity of urban environments and integrating climate risk


---
**Page 115**
---

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium to high

- The expected GCF fund-level impacts for a total investment of USD 450 million are (i) 7,547,628 tCO2eq of direct GHG emission reductions with a GCF cost of intervention of USD 36/CO2eq, and (ii) 1.9 million people benefiting from improved air quality.
- The program will also support the implementation of 100 projects that will reduce emissions valued at USD 418 million, of which USD 108 million is due to reduced local air pollution.
- The program will also support the development and deployment of 25 adaptation measures, representing 9 per cent of the entire population of Program countries.

The Program scores well against the Strategic Plan for the GCF 2020–2023 targets for mitigation impacts (1.4 million tCO2eq) and adaptation impacts (166 million beneficiaries/USD 1 billion). The Program helps GCF to meet the 2020–2023 targets of USD 500 million in mobilization and USD 750 million in leverage, as it is a cross-cutting Program. It has 44 per cent adaptation and 56 per cent mitigation. It includes Dominica Republic and Barbados as advanced developing countries recipients of funding.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

Programme background: The E-Mobility Programme aims to strengthen and improve the public transport sector, non-motorized transport, and electric mobility in the urban areas in participating countries. It will support the deployment of electric vehicles (EVs), hybrid buses, trucks including supporting hydrogen and vehicle-to-grid pilot projects. Among the key social co-benefits are improved air quality, reduced greenhouse gas emissions, reduced traffic congestion and noise emissions as well as water pollution caused by fossil-fueled vessels. Among the key social co-benefits are improved air quality, reduced greenhouse gas emissions, reduced traffic congestion and noise emissions as well as water pollution caused by fossil-fueled vessels. Among the key social co-benefits are improved air quality, reduced greenhouse gas emissions, reduced traffic congestion and noise emissions as well as water pollution caused by fossil-fueled vessels. Among the key social co-benefits are improved air quality, reduced greenhouse gas emissions, reduced traffic congestion and noise emissions as well as water pollution caused by fossil-fueled vessels.

The increased use of safe public transport, non-motorized transport and micro-mobility options are also expected to result in lower accident rates, fewer road congestion and improved quality of life for urban dwellers. The increased resilience of the


---
**Page 116**
---

infrastructure that will be supported by the programme also allows for increased grid resilience and lesser disruptions of services as regards mobility and electricity supply.

27. Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instruments: The Accredited Entity (AE) classified the programme as Category B. The Secretariat confirms the categorization and that the AE has prepared an ESAS and ESMAP. The Secretariat also notes that the AE has prepared an Environmental and Social Analysis and Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESAF/ESMF). The ESAF/ESMF will be used to identify, assess, monitor, report on, and mitigate the environmental and social risks associated with the implementation of the programme as well as the mitigation measures to address these risks and impacts.

* Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards: The programme is expected to comply with all applicable ESS standards, including IFC Performance Standards.
* ESS 1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts: The ESAS provides a description of the environmental and social risks and impacts of the subproject activities. These will be assessed, monitored, reported on, and mitigated during and monitored at the programme and operational activity levels. The selected EEIs (such as LFsIs and NIIIs) will also be used to assess, monitor, report on, and mitigate environmental and social risks and impacts of the subproject activities in accordance with GCF’s ESS standards applicable, and screen subprojects in accordance with the Programme’s exclusion list and applicable national laws.
* ESS 2: Labor and Working Conditions: Potential risks and impacts of the programme as regards labor and working conditions will be in relation to construction and operation activities of the subproject. The AE has prepared a risk assessment for the subproject, which indicates that workers could be exposed to physical and chemical hazards particularly in relation to batteries and e-waste handling, noise and dust generation from heavy equipment and machinery, as well as potential sectoral labor practices and working conditions checklist to be implemented on the ground. Proper health and safety measures will be put in place for all subproject activities. The AE’s national requirements as per the national and the AE’s requirements pursuant to the GCF’s policies will also be implemented and monitored.
* ESS 3: Waste Management and Pollution Prevention: The activities under the programme will potentially result to generation of waste water and hazardous materials. Additional risks associated with these include potential for increased generation of noise, dust particles, solid and liquid waste, including risk related to potential for fuel spillage and increased traffic due to the use of heavy equipment and machinery. Incidents on soil quality degradation are also possible as a result of construction activities. Potential risks in relation to water use may also occur particularly in the hydrogen production facility. Accidental spills may also be generated from the subproject activities. The programme intends to ensure that operators will be licensed and that appropriate environmental management plans will be developed and implemented for all subproject activities. Management plans will likewise be developed which shall include prior water balance studies, waste management plan, and spillage control measures. Environmental procedures will be aligned with globally accepted environment, health and safety (EHS) guidelines and good international industry practice (GIP).
* ESS 4: Social Inclusion: The programme is expected to have a significant impact on communities where the infrastructure will be located may experience adverse impacts as regards construction and operation activities. These include potential for increased traffic due to movement of heavy machinery, increased potential for traffic accidents due to movements of heavy machinery and vehicles, as well as decreased access to roads and other public services in areas surrounding the hydrogen production infrastructure. Possible conflicts between construction workers, security personnel and the local population may also occur. The AE will develop a social inclusion plan that will be detailed during the subproject identification and specific management plans will be developed. This will include measures as regards management of possible impacts generated by the presence


---
**Page 117**
---

### ESS 7: Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

The Programme will ensure activities related to involuntary resettlement (particularly physical displacement) and will use existing rights of way for electrical lines. However, there will be no physical resettlement of communities. The programme will ensure that all relevant environmental economic impacts, compensation plans and/or livelihood restoration plans will be accredited in accordance with the World Bank Group’s Environmental and Social Framework for Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement. The programme will ensure that relevant permits and land use rights are obtained before construction (e.g. electric vehicle charging stations and hydrogen plant) will be acquired by the borrower.

### ESS 8: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources

The Programme will not result in any significant environmental degradation because of enhanced entry to previously inaccessible areas which could result to land conversion. Nevertheless, the programme will ensure the exclusion of locating subprojects in critical habitats and protected areas. The programme will also include a supervision and monitoring programme to check on land use and land cover change in the locations where sub-projects are located. This will be done through remote sensing facilities used. Waste management plans will also be developed to ensure that no habitat degradation or loss of biodiversity occurs.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples

As indigenous peoples are present many of the countries where the project is being implemented (e.g. Panama, Colombia, Mexico), the Programme will ensure that they are adequately consulted and ensures that proposed sub-projects with potential adverse impacts on indigenous peoples will not be developed without their free, prior and informed consent. The programme will also ensure that a dedicated team of technical experts and stakeholder engagement strategies with indigenous peoples in case it will be necessary to ensure that the project is implemented in an inclusive manner. A multi-stakeholder dialogue process organized involving over a hundred participants. The following elements were developed in the FP Section of the ESS 7: small grants program for indigenous and traditional communities, (c) continued technical assistance to indigenous peoples including the inclusion of gender and diversity, (d) amplification of the dialogue process on the topic of indigenous ancestral lands and territories, (e) strengthening of the Indigenous Peoples’ Redress Mechanism and the Secretariat’s indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance in implementing this ESS.

### ESS 8: Cultural Heritage

The Programme's activities particularly during construction could adversely affect physical cultural resources. The programme will however ensure that they will not be developed without adequate assessment, mitigation measures and compensation supported. The EES environmental and social management systems (EEMS) will likewise ensure proper protection of cultural heritage sites. A cultural heritage impact assessment procedure will be operationalized should cultural heritage be discovered.

### Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) Safeguarding

The programme will ensure that all sub-projects will have a clear policy on workers and community members, and during the deployment of EV vehicles, the construction and operation of urban charging stations. The programme will also ensure that a SEAH risk assessment is developed as part of the sub-project Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) by borrower, financial intermediaries, EIS or implementing partners, where SEAH risks are identified. A grievance mechanism for workers and community members will be put in place on all sub-projects put into place with specific procedures to receive, register and


---
**Page 118**
---

manage SEAM allegations. The programme will also take additional measures to tackle sexual harassment and public transportation that are detailed in the Gender Action Plan.

### Implementation arrangements

Each subproject will be supported under the programme will have an assigned primary safeguard officer from the AE's Environmental and Social Management (ESM) team. This safeguard officer will provide technical support, including the engagement of support from external consultants. Each of the participating EEs is expected to manage their own ESM implementation in accordance with the AE's ESM requirements.

### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The AE has conducted stakeholder consultations during the preparation of this programme which included direct dialogue, roundtable meetings, presentations, and discussions representing government ministries, academia, private sector, and donor organizations. A stakeholder engagement plan will be developed for each subproject that includes planned future consultations and participation of stakeholders. The nature of engagement including the frequency and level of involvement with respect to the specific activities of this subproject as well as its stage of development. Subsidiary developers will also be required to disclose their ESM implementation plans, including stakeholder engagement (particularly project-affected people) to have a clear understanding of the underlying social impacts, and the potential opportunities and development benefits that the subproject may offer.

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a Program-level gender assessment and gender action plan and has completed the development of the updated gender policy. The gender assessment was conducted based on desk reviews with the expectation that primary data and stakeholder engagement-led gender analysis will be conducted for such country-specific projects as they are developed. The gender assessment aims to identify actionable interventions to enable the Program to achieve gender equality and women's empowerment.

The gender assessment provided some general gender-related realities, including (i) an enabling environment and legal framework for gender-related work in countries; (ii) employment opportunities for women in the energy sector and other sectors in these areas. It acknowledges challenges in access to data for the most relevant aspects of the Programme’s activities, such as the energy sector, the water sector, and the transportation sector, to name a few. Broader speaking, however, it acknowledges that the countries have gender policies and frameworks that support equal opportunities for women
