### 4.3 Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (Medium risk)

The Programme will be supporting EV deployment with sustainable urban mobility solutions, including e-buses, taxis, infrastructure and the grid in Latin America. GCF is investing USD 145 million in senior loans and USD 55 million in grants. The total co-financing amount is estimated at USD 300 million. The Programme will be implemented through a combination of AIFs and national governments in the form of senior loans, grants and in-kind contributions. It is


---
**Page 120**
---

expected that the All will provide co-financing of USD 195 million in senior loans and USD 5 million in grants. The All will be part wise with GCF.

52. The GCF concessional financing will address barriers to the uptake of commercial IVs, including performance risks (e.g., novelty of the technology), asset risks (e.g., resale value of assets) and market risks (e.g., lack of demand). The technical assistance and capacity building in this programme have been proven at least in pilot cases in other countries. The grant will (i) provide support to the All in developing a comprehensive strategy for the adoption of IVs, (ii) develop a framework for financing the deployment of IV technologies and structuring EV investments; and (iii) contribute to the regulatory framework development and capacity building needed to ensure that the All can meet the long-term policy environment and regulatory measures conducive to scaling up EV deployment so that it can be fully commercially viable and financially more attractive than fossil fuel units.

### Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme

53. The AE, IDB, has an extensive track record in implementing projects and programmes to reduce poverty and increase economic growth in Latin America and Caribbean. The AE has been supporting the development of electric vehicle infrastructure in the region for over the past five years. It has seven approved projects with GCF and is rated Aaa by Moody’s.

54. The EEs for this programme may include ministries, public entities, national development banks, or other financial institutions. The All will be eligible to receive project loans and grants. The EEs will be selected jointly with the AE and the national governments, and all EE will be subject to a due diligence process by the AE. In addition to the technical cooperation, the IDB may be the EE, it may enter into a subsidiary agreement with other EEs.

56. The GCF proceeds may be directly executed by the AE, or the eligible borrowers (i.e., first-level EEs) may transfer to the second-level EEs to execute the subproject. It may also be characterized as a co-financing project. Depending on the type of the subject matter and the type of the subproject, different contractual structures will be analyzed and modified on a case-by-case basis. Given that the programme is at the first level and the concessional nature of the EV market in the region, the AE/EE capability risks is assessed as Medium to High.

### Programme-specific execution risks (Medium risk)

61. Credit risk: The GCF loans will be either directly provided to the sovereign governments or to an All with a sovereign guarantee. In case the state of the AE does not provide a sovereign guarantee for the project, the All will be required to provide one. According to the information provided by the AE, the pipeline subprojects beyond year 3 are yet to be defined and may include additional projects such as public-private partnerships, investment loans and grants. The AE also stated that some of the pilot projects (e.g., V2G) will not be commercially oriented but will instead be used for testing technology. Therefore, there is a risk that the All may not have sufficient resources to meet its obligations under the programme. It should be noted that the impact calculation be readjusted after the midpoint of the Programme implementation period when there is better visibility for the subsequent pipeline.


---
**Page 121**
---

### Co-financing risks

The co-financing amount from each co-finance is not confirmed, including AFC own resources as it is subject to the approval of allocation of funds to each individual Sub-Project on case-by-case basis. Depending on the contractual arrangement, the potential impact of low co-financing will be mitigated by the inclusion of a term in the contract that states: if the amount of co-financing is lower than currently estimated, the impact envisaged will also be decreased. The ability to implement activities within the approved budget and the availability of co-financing will be critical to implemented funded activities. Therefore, to mitigate the risk of low co-financing, the term sheet includes a clause requiring the AE to report to the GCF on the co-financing received for each Sub-Project. This is in accordance with the requirements set out by the Board of GCF Programmes and Co-funding by the 4th anniversary of the FAIS as a condition precedent to disbursement of funds.

### Foreign exchange risks

GCF and IBRD lending will be in USD, and loans to the final beneficiaries may be in local currency. The volatility of local currency is high in some participating countries. Therefore, the AE should ensure that the beneficiary has the resources however to repay. The sovereign government will assume the currency risks, and GCF will assess financial viability on a project-specific basis.

### Carbon credit

The current term sheet does not include an explicit covenant to cancel or retire carbon credits to be financed by the GCF. Any sale of carbon credits would reduce the impact of the Programme and should be properly monitored to account for effective mitigation.

## Programme visibility and concessionality

### 4.3.4 Programme visibility and concessionality

GCF-financed sovereign investment loans will be provided with low and high concessional terms, and the high concessional terms will be used for funding adaptation activities. No concessional funds will be used for commercial EUs, but they will be used for pilot EVs and adaptation measures. The detailed financial structure, a methodology to pass down the GCF concessionality will be submitted by the AE for each sub-project. It is important that the methodology is to ensure that final beneficiaries are sufficiently benefiting from the GCF concessionality.

### Compliance risk (high risk)

The beneficiary countries are not subject to United Nations Security Council restrictive measures or other international sanctions policies, which means that any services provided with sanctioned entities are excluded from any Programme activities. Office of Risk Management and Compliance (ORMC)/Compliance notes that some beneficiary countries (Indonesia, Pakistan, and Vietnam) have been identified by the GCF as having jurisdictions with strategic deficiencies in their respective anti-money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) frameworks. ORM/Compliance recommends performing enhanced due diligence and monitoring on all transactions to ensure that no red flags are associated with their activities or any potential for red flags were associated with their abilities to fulfill their responsibilities. The AE also intends to perform a risk assessment in order to identify the appropriate level of enhanced fiduciary risk management throughout the programme implementation. The AE will implement in practice the above recommendations and will ensure that all parties have the opportunity to report any irregularities which may arise. ORM/Compliance has conducted a review of the Programme in accordance with relevant Board-approved policies, and based on available information, no red flags were identified. The AE is requested to provide written objection to this request proceeding to the next steps for processing.

#### 4.4 Fiduciary

**70.** The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) is the AE for the Programme. Several Indicators will be used to monitor the performance of the programme, including at this stage, IDB as an AE, may also carry out the functions of an EE in technical cooperation activities.

* For the execution of sovereign investment loans and grants, each EE will be responsible for the financial management of the loan or grant (including the funding agreement). Eligible EEs shall be identified jointly with national governments based on the reference list provided by IDB. The EEs will be responsible for the implementation in line with fiduciary management, and track record, including with the IDB. For all technical cooperation activities, the EEs will be responsible for the implementation in accordance with other EEAs. The EEs are not fully known at this stage; condition precedents have been added to the funding agreement to ensure that the EEs selected by the host country require co-financing information to be submitted before each disbursement, along with pipeline information.

* For each of the Programme's components, IDB as AE will evaluate and, where appropriate, approve the subprojects. For the approval of each proposed subproject, IDB will apply its applicable policies and procedures (including those related to the financial management). In case all the eligibility criteria mentioned in the funding proposal and the funded activity agreement (FAA) at the time of approval. During the implementation period, following the entry into effect of the FAA, IDB will monitor the progress of each approved subproject and approve new subprojects in accordance with the provisions and conditions established in the FAA. The subsequent disbursements for each approved subproject shall be made through the following instruments: (i) sovereign investment loans; (ii) sovereign investment grants; and (iii) technical cooperation.

**71.** Monitoring for the Programme will be in line with IDB's policies and the terms of the accreditation master agreement (AMA)/FAA. Specifically, the implementation of each project will be monitored by the Project Officer assigned to the Program level by IDB in-house staff; project management unit and contracted consultants.

**72.** The disbursements, reporting (including external audit reports), monitoring and evaluation shall comply with IDB's policies and procedures, including the IDB's Financial Management Guidelines, and reflected in the term sheet and the text.

### Results monitoring and reporting

The funding proposal is a cross-cutting programme aimed at reducing emissions to the tune of $2547692 (CO2eq) from low-emission transport and benefiting 103 million people with measures in Latin America, Central America, the Caribbean, South Asia, East Africa, and the Caribbean.

The funding proposal has been found to use the revised template in line with the IMF requirements. The sections on Theory of Change and the logical framework have been aligned with the suggested structure and format. The AE had been responsive in addressing the feedback received from the secretariat. It is recommended that the revised template and logical framework would allow to capture the desired results, facilitate results monitoring and periodic reporting.

However, the estimation of adaptation benefits and beneficiaries in relation to the funding proposal remains a concern. There is a need to develop and present a methodology used for estimating the adaptation benefits and identifying the beneficiaries under the programme. As it is a cross-cutting programme, the adaptation benefits and beneficiaries need to be analysed in a systematic manner and duplication must be avoided. In that respect, the following covenant is suggested:

* The AE shall provide to the ACF a revised methodological framework for the estimation of benefits and identification of the beneficiaries under the funding proposal and those under the program, and how beneficiaries are identified against those particular adaptation benefits. The revised document should be submitted in accordance with the requirements of the ACF.

With respect to the monitoring and evaluation plan, the secretariat's feedback on budgetary revisions have been found to be addressed. Monitoring and reporting at project level and their alignment with the logical framework is recommended.

Implementation timetable: The implementation timetable has been provided in the required structure and format. The revised plan contains the milestones and deliverables that are envisaged for each year of the programme, hence implementation which in turn is expected to facilitate progress monitoring and reporting.


### Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 29 August 2017 (the "AM"), and it became effective on 30 March 2018.

The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all necessary approvals to implement the programme. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the Funding Proposal to the Board (4th August 2017), the Accredited Entity should provide to the secretariat either a certificate or legal opinion from the Accredited Entity in form and substance satisfactory to the


---
**Page 124**

Fund confirming that all final internal approvals by the Accredited Entity have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to implement the programme.

### a1. The proposed programme will be implemented in Barbados, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay. The GCF has signed a bilateral agreement with each of these countries except for Paraguay. Paraguay is one of 20 countries that have not signed a bilateral agreement on privileges and immunities with Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominica, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico, Nicaragua, Panama, Peru, Suriname, Uruguay, Venezuela, and the United States. GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against expropriation or expatriation in these countries, which risks need to be further assessed. Specifically:

- **Chile:** A draft bilateral agreement is under negotiation with the last contact in October 2019.
- **Colombia:** A draft bilateral agreement was disputed to the government of Colombia in April 2019.
- **Costa Rica:** A draft bilateral agreement is under negotiation and the government of Costa Rica confirmed it was reviewing the draft agreement in April 2022.
- **Dominican Republic:** A draft bilateral agreement was disputed to the government of the Dominican Republic in October 2020.
- **Jamaica:** A draft bilateral agreement is under negotiation with the last contact in October 2020.
- **Panama:** A draft bilateral agreement was dispatched to the government of Panama in July 2020 and the government of Panama confirmed its review of the document in December 2020; and
- **Paraguay:** A draft bilateral agreement is under negotiation with the last contact in October 2020.

### a2. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have been informed that the GCF has not received any complaints regarding non-activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. In such cases, the GCF will only be able to make payments to the grantee if the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against expropriation and expatriation in the countries or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

### a3. The Funding Proposal is a programme to be implemented in nine countries and consists of several sub-projects to be developed after the GCF Board approved of the Funding Proposal. Therefore, the specific details regarding the project activities, including the number of projects and their locations, are not known yet. The Funding Proposal presented a few possible structures and scenarios for implementing the programme. In all these scenarios, the relevant sub-project may be implemented by an executing entity (Executing Entity) that is different from the Accredited Entity. The Executing Entity will enter into a Sub-Agreement with the grantee (Sub-Agreement) to channel the GCF funds. The Executing Entity will not have contractual privy with the Executing Entity. The Accredited Entity will enter into another agreement with the executing entity (Executing Agreement). The grantee and/or the grantee’s agent will be responsible for entering into a Sub-Agreement with the executing entity (Executing Entity “Sub-Agreement”) is to channel the GCF proceeds. The borrower and/or the grantee of the Sub-Agreement will be responsible for entering into an Executing Agreement with the executing entity. The Accredited Entity will not be a party to the Sub-Agreement or the Executing Agreement. The Accredited Entity explained that the borrower and/or grantee will be responsible for ensuring that all payments are made in accordance with the terms of the Sub-Agreement by the Executing Entity in accordance with the Sub-Agreed Agreement and will require the Executing Entity to report to the Accredited Entity. The absence of contractual privy between


---
**Page 125**
---

The Accredited Entity and the Executing Entity pose certain risks such as, the Accredited Entity not having the right and ability to monitor and supervise the performance of the executing entity and the project implementation process, and not able to have a direct recourse against the executing entity. Further, the GCF cannot exercise its rights under the AMA in respect of the Execution Agreement.

To mitigate this risk, the Secretariat has agreed the following options in the item sheet:

(a) If permitted under the laws, rules and regulations applicable to the borrowers and/or grantors, the Accredited Entity may be able to enter into a separate agreement with the Subsidiary Agreement, thus creating contractual privy with the Accredited Entity and the Accredited Entity may be able to exercise rights in respect of the AMA.

(b) If Option 1 is not feasible, the Accredited Entity will be required to approve the terms and conditions of the Execution Agreement and no changes will be made to the Executing Entity’s obligations under the AMA. The Subsidiary Agreement and the Execution Agreement should be linked in a way that it effect enables the GCF to exercise its rights under the AMA, and the Accredited Entity is able to perform its functions under the AMA and FAA, and the Executing Entity to report to the Accredited Entity.

(c) If Options 1 and 2 are not feasible, the Accredited Entity shall seek GCF’s instructions on the implementation of each sub-project on a case-by-case basis (“Option 3”).

It is further noted that, in accordance with the law, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 120 days after the Board’s approval confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and it has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme;

(b) Signature of the F&A in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of the Board approval, or the date the FAA has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

## List of proposed conditions (including legal)

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission by the F&A to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 120 days after the Board’s approval confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and it has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme;

(b) Signature of the F&A in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of the Board approval, or the date the FAA has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP189

## Proposal name: E-Mobility Program for Sustainable Cities in Latin America and the Caribbean

### Accredited entity: Inter-American Development Bank (IDB)

#### Country/ies: Barbados, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay, Uruguay

#### Project/Program size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

#### Overview:
The E-Mobility Program for Sustainable Cities in Latin America and the Caribbean (the “Program”) seeks to promote a structural shift towards low-carbon, climate-resilient public transport systems by reducing barriers to mass electric vehicle (EV) deployment. The Program will support the development of integrated urban transport systems in 10 cities across six countries in the Latin America and Caribbean (LAC) region along with financing for the increased climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure and corresponding grids (i.e., integrated electrified urban transport systems). The Program aims to act as a market catalyst, leveraging IDB’s technical expertise and financial resources to support public-private partnerships. The cities involved are: Barbados, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay, Uruguay), with replication potential in other LAC countries.

#### 1.1 Total financial size of the Program is USD $49 million, with GCF expected to provide USD $99 million, approximately 44 per cent of total funds. The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) will finance USD $50 million through a loan facility and USD $23 million in grant funding. IDB will co-finance with USD $18 million in concessional loans from GCF and USD $14 million in non-reimbursable grants. IDB will co-finance with USD $67 million in concessional loans from GCF and USD $50 million in non-reimbursable grants.

#### 2. The total financial size of the Program is USD $49 million, with GCF expected to provide USD $99 million, approximately 44 per cent of total funds. The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) will finance USD $50 million through a loan facility and USD $23 million in grant funding. IDB will co-finance with USD $18 million in concessional loans from GCF and USD $14 million in non-reimbursable grants. IDB will co-finance with USD $67 million in concessional loans from GCF and USD $50 million in non-reimbursable grants.

#### 3. The secured concessional loan portion of the Program will be co-lent to executing entities at GCF public sector concessionary rates and terms to finance e-mobility and related adaptation investments. These are defined as investments that are not eligible for concessional investments for which the participating country has no revenue. Less concessional loans are for mitigation investments, such as renewables. Both loans for adaptation and loans for mitigation are to support the public sector.

#### 4. The USD $15 million in non-reimbursable grants requested of GCF will go to investment subsidies for project preparation activities, including technical assistance, including project origination and structuring. This component addresses the barriers of limited access to financing and lack of capacity at executing entities to prepare projects with specific adaptation investments such as V2G and infrastructure for peak savings, both of which are aimed at increasing grid resilience. A goal of this component is to make electric grids more resilient to climate events with new energy storage options.


---
**Page 127**
---

The Program is structured in 4 components: (1) Increase climate resilience of urban transport infrastructure; (2) Increase climate resilience of grid with H2 and V2H pilot projects; (3) Electrified integrated urban mobility; and (4) Technical assistance and project structuring to improve the sustainability of public transport systems.

Impacts In our view, the Program has a well-articulated climate objective. We note that transport contributes almost one quarter of the current global energy-related greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. The transport sector is also the largest source of CO2 emissions in LAC and accounts for 10 per cent of global GHG emissions, with a significant portion coming from the transport sub-sector. In terms of countries, Brazil accounts for 37 percent of total emissions in LAC; 12 per cent in Caracas; 26 per cent in Chile; 16 per cent in Colombia; 37 per cent in Costa Rica; 18 per cent in Ecuador; and 40 per cent in Peru. In terms of cities, Buenos Aires accounts for 27 percent in Argentina and 15 per cent in Uruguay. Transport emissions are expected to continue growing under a business-as-usual (BAU) scenario, fueled by increasing private car ownership (corresponding to an increase in vehicle kilometers traveled).

Absent policy and price interventions, transportation GHG emissions in LAC could reach 11 gigatons of carbon dioxide equivalent (GtCO2e) by 2050. Under the 2°C scenario, the 2050 decade’s technology pathways as shown within funding proposal demonstrate that transport electrification approaches could have significant positive impacts, with a reduction in emissions to around 7 GtCO2e. Achieving this level of ambition under 2°C will require changing the transport emissions trajectory. Latin American countries have already started to make progress on this front and are expected to continue doing so.

Moreover, LAC is among the most urbanized regions in the world with over 80 per cent of its population living in cities. Cities in LAC are projected to continue growing in the coming decades, which will further increase their contribution to GHG emissions and reduce their potential for economic growth. For this reason, as climate change impacts continue to evolve and intensify, it is essential that urban transport systems be designed with climate resilience applied so that climate-related risks are managed. Consequently, climate-resilient infrastructure is identified as a priority in nearly every single nationally determined contribution within the LAC region.

The Program expects to contribute 7552.386 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) reductions by 2050, which translates into an estimated reduction of USD 24/tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent. The GHG reductions result from the operation of EVs procured with investments from the Program. The tolls generated will be reinvested back on a well-balanced, whole-of-system approach to urban transport (i.e., reducing production costs; transit and distribution services). Areas of emission reductions are: (1) impact of replacing fossil fuel-based vehicles with electric vehicles; (2) reduction in emissions due to electrification due to system improvements; and (iii) cycle lanes and increased usage of non-motorized transport.

Latin America is best-suited for the Program’s interventions. The region has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean transport and e-mobility. The region also has one of the smallest shares of fossil fuel-based vehicles in the world, which makes it easier to electrify (coupled with growing wind and solar deployment). Therefore, the adoption of an e-mobility strategy is a key opportunity for Latin America to reduce its GHG emissions.

The carbon intensity of electricity generated by Program countries is mostly favorable. With favorable conditions, LAC countries can achieve GHG emissions on a well-to-wheel basis by 70-80 per cent compared to fossil fuel alternatives.


1 Vergara L., 2015.


---
**Page 128**
---

11. As to adaptation impact, the Program’s adaptation measures reach 1.5 million direct and 9 million indirect beneficiaries, representing 9 per cent of the entire population of Program countries. Program funds are expected to fund mitigation and adaptation measures at a 5:46 per cent ratio.

12. Overall impact potential is assessed as Medium to High. The Program could make a significant contribution to altering the GfLc pathways of these countries through low-emission transportation systems in line with their Nationally Determined Contributions targets for each country.


### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: High

The Program seeks a paradigm shift by promoting and financing robust EV ecosystems in the target countries. These efforts include enabling financial and business models that shift transport from fossil fuels to electricity, as well as supporting the development of EV infrastructure.

The participating countries in the Program currently only have pilot EV fleets with significant headroom to grow and scale. The Program also seeks to facilitate mass EV deployment by funding an integrated approach for the transition to low-emission transportation systems, including grid upgrades. Moreover, an improved ecosystem for EVs requires a suite of technical assistance interventions, including training for policymakers and other stakeholders, as provided under component 4 of the Program.

We note again that transport contributes almost one quarter of the current global energy-related CO2 emissions and is projected to increase by more than two-thirds over this century. Limiting the global temperature increase below 2°C will require changing this transport emission profile. The Paris Declaration on Electric Mobility and Climate Change & Call to Action calls for a combination of interest in shifting to mobility, only Chile and Colombia have notable e-bus fleets, and in both cases these are pilot projects. In the context of the broader electrification agenda across all transport sectors, participating countries will not be able to achieve their decarbonization plans. For example, without significant EV deployment it is thus consistent with relevant national climate-resilient development pathways.

The Program intends to couple funding for a mobility and climate-resilient integrated electric vehicle ecosystem in the target countries with support for low-emission transport infrastructure. This combination of efforts is intended to accelerate the e-mobility market in the participating countries, while also supporting the transition to low-emission transportation systems at scale, along with the potential to transform transportation at scale and earlier than under a RMI scenario.

The Program creates added value through capacity-building aimed at training equipment operators, fleet managers, and other relevant stakeholders on vehicle safety, usability, and management of EV fleets. Additionally, the Program will provide support to national and local authorities to set up, improve and enhance enabling public policies and legal, regulatory and fiscal frameworks for EVs. This includes supporting the development of a robust and coherent regulatory environment that is conducive to transforming the transport development trajectory in line with the Paris Declaration on Electric Mobility and Climate Change & Call to Action’s planning, design-and-decision-making processes for urban transport projects that consider climate-related factors such as air quality, public health, road safety, energy efficiency at all levels and the infrastructure level – that is more resilient to expected climate impacts (intense precipitation events, heat waves, etc.).

Finally, it is our view that part of the paradigm shift potential involves the demonstration effect of successful e-mobility interventions. The experience of various countries


---
**Page 129**
---

shows that their fleets of EVs are operational, the technology is taken up quickly if the business environment is conducive: The projects financed under this Program are intended to have such a catalytic effect by demonstrating the commercial viability of EVs.

### Sustainable development potential Scale: Medium to high

The Program's primary goal is to reduce GHG emissions and increase the adaptation capacity and resilience of urban transport systems. In so doing, it seeks to contribute significantly towards achieving the following United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 9 (Industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG goal 11 (Sustainable cities and communities).

#### Environmental co-benefits Significant environmental co-benefits are achieved through reduced air and water pollution and reduced noise emissions. By replacing and displacing fossil fuel transportation vehicles with electric vehicles (EVs) in urban areas, the Program reduces nitrogen oxides and nitrous oxide emissions. Air pollution is a major problem in most Latin American cities, with levels exceeding World Health Organization guidelines by 20–30 times in some cases. In Mexico City alone, air pollution causes an estimated 4.2 million deaths around the world are due to excessive exposure to fine particulate matter (PM2.5) and ozone. Children and young people are disproportionately affected by air pollution as they tend to live and work closer to its sources. Children under the age of five are particularly vulnerable: Replacing fossil fuel combustion vehicles with EVs thus has a significant impact on reducing child mortality.

#### Social and gender benefits The Program's major social benefit is improved air quality and reduced noise pollution. Other social benefits are related to increased patronage of public transport systems, which can lead to improvements in the quality of life in cities. The increased resilience of urban infrastructure and electricity grids, which would otherwise be vulnerable to climate change impacts.

Each Program investment will be required to meet IGF's parity on gender equality. This policy includes actions and targets to (i) increase women’s access to economic opportunities; (ii) increase gender awareness to recognize women as part of the electability sector and address gender biases, and (iii) promote gender action plans in all projects financed with the development of gender toolkit and case studies as well as support in the development of gender equality strategies.

#### Economic co-benefits The Program's economic co-benefits arise from reduced emissions and are estimated at over USD 400 million, of which a quarter is due to reduced air pollution. These benefits include avoided health costs, increased productivity, and additional economic co-benefits not calculated at the Program level are reduced subsidies for fossil fuels, distributed energy generation, and other public services. The Program also generates time savings, fewer accidents and reduced vehicle operating costs related to increased usage of public transport, improved urban freight transport, and economic benefits of improved grid and urban infrastructure resilience.

The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) assesses this Program as having Medium to High sustainable development potential with significant social, economic and health co-benefits.


---
**Page 130**
---

1.4 Needs of the recipients

Scale: High

25. Most Program countries have limited experience in e-mobility via small-scale pilot bus programs and are seeking to engage significant government stakeholders. The Program provides long-term financing at concessional rates and technical assistance grants, which are required given the fiscal constraints of Program countries and limited alternative sources of financing for urban and rural transport.

26. Contrary to renewable energy generation that is increasingly cost-competitive, EVs are not yet competitive with conventional vehicles in most markets. The key barriers involved have been identified: (i) high upfront investment requirements; (ii) the low financial profitability of EV investments (low returns, very long payback times); (iii) high technical risk of EV investments (high failure rates and limited availability of spare parts); and (iv) a lack of know-how and enabling environment frameworks; and (v) first mover problem (no local track record).

27. The Program is intended to provide financial and technical assistance support for alternative funding vehicles with a higher risk appetite, such as the Program. This is even more pronounced in countries where there are limited or no other viable financing options, which are still embryonic and thus not suitable for commercial finance as yet.

28. All Program countries have expressed a commitment to decarbonizing the transport sectors and expanding e-mobility in cities. The Program is intended to provide the financial and technical assistance support to help operationalize this vision.

29. The independent TAF assessors view this Program as High in terms of the needs of its recipients.


1.5 Country ownership

Scale: Medium to high

30. IIB has held meetings with the national designated authorities of the Program countries and key actors in e-mobility to ensure that the Program aligns with each country's needs. Additional meetings are planned for 2024, including a workshop on e-mobility involving local governmental bodies, energy utilities, civil society and transport operators.

31. No objection letters (NOLs) have been obtained from all participating Program countries.


From our review, the Program responds to country ambitions on integrating climate risk adaptation in infrastructure and decarbonizing the transport sector.

In the AE, IIB has been working closely with the national designated authorities of these countries for close coordination with existing initiatives, thereby ensuring complementary and coherence. In fact, the IIB has been working closely with Costa Rica since 2013, with a focus on energy efficiency in buildings; with Jamaica since 2011, improving the institutional and regulatory environment for the accelerated deployment of electric vehicles; IIB's support has been key to the development and set-up of enabling regulations for the deployment of electric buses in both Costa Rica, the Dominican Republic and Jamaica.

The participating countries in the Program have committed to providing on-finance and in-kind contributions in support of the Program. This is a good indicator of country ownership. Nevertheless, the firmness of these commitments is unclear, and while the funding proposal is very specific, it does not include any indicative figures for the contribution from each country alongside specific e-mobility investments that are yet to be concluded. The IIB has a working paper (WP) on this topic which will be updated in 2024. Given the uncertainty around this into consideration, we would prefer to see greater clarity and assurance among participating


---
**Page 131**
---

1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The Program introduces a concessional financing mechanism that addresses barriers to the uptake of commercial EVs and supports a transformative shift to low-carbon transportation. GCF concessional funding is used for project preparation costs, which are unlikely to materialize in the short or medium term in the absence of GCF financing.

Total investment in the Program is USD 449 million, of which USD 197 million would be provided by the GCF. The total cost of the Program is estimated at USD 523,386 MioCO₂eq of direct GHG emission reductions with a GCF cost of intervention of Z6 USD/CO₂eq. This cost is relatively low compared to other projects in the pipeline (e.g., USD 100–400 per tonne CO₂eq) and represents an overall cost-effective investment (46 per cent for adaptation), which has an impact on the high GHG abatement cost. Additionally, the Program is expected to generate significant co-benefits, including a reduction in regional air pollution. Economic benefits due to reduced emissions are valued at USD 418 million of which USD 108 million is due to reduced local air pollution. Lastly, the Program's adaptation measures benefit both urban and rural populations, as well as the entire population of Program countries.

The Program’s leverage ratio is 1:12.25. This is based on the initial project pipeline for mitigation activities over a period of six years of indicative projects. This ratio may improve as the project pipeline matures and extends to the first six years of implementation. The direct mobilization potential (DMP) is USD 30 million, which is less than the GCF’s indicative request. We generally discourage market-marketing that has low mobilization impacts, as a core mission of the GCF is to leverage private sector investments in sustainable development projects. However, the Program targets replication; therefore, indirect mobilization potential (e.g., initial investment costs, co-financing, and other enabling factors) is expected to be significant (e.g., improved enabling framework), together with market piece development, key for a mass update of the project pipeline. The Program’s adaptation measures are also an area where mobilization impacts can occur in future (albeit this is difficult to predict or measure).

We also draw comfort from the fact that projects involved in the proposed Program will not be dependent on a continuous flow of GCF funds. Rather, the Program aims to achieve a substantiation of the need for commercial financing in future projects. It is expected that, over the course of implementation of the Program, the private sector will gradually move towards commercial viability in countries which have kick-started the process. The use of GCF funding can help unlock investments and demonstrate the viability of co-investment in commercial terms.

The funding proposal is assessed with a rating of Medium in terms of efficiency and effectiveness.


### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel


---
**Page 132**
---

### Based on the assessment above, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal. However, the independent TAP makes the following recommendations that will further improve the success of this funding proposal:

- The independent TAP recommends that equity and/or in-kind contributions from the participating countries be confirmed.
- The independent TAP recommends that the assessment of adaptation benefits be further developed. There is a need to develop and present a methodology used for identifying, quantifying, and valuing adaptation co-benefits associated with climate-resilient investments.
- The independent TAP also notes that the Program pipeline is indicative at this stage. The success of the Program will depend on how countries are able to identify priority interventions and take on financing from the Program. This has yet to happen so there is always some risk that the facility could be over funded if uptake is dampened.
- The independent TAP also notes that the GCF is currently in a period of transition, particularly in the LAC region, and therefore it expects IIB to manage the pipeline well. However, the independent TAP recommends that IIB should continue to develop and implement financing mechanisms in the future that allow it to deploy capital in tranches against actual capital needs rather than committed funds. This should provide for greater flexibility of GCF funds and reduce fee obligations.

## Overall remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel:

We thank the ITAP team for their thorough, timely, and constructive feedback.
Regarding the two main networks, we would like to highlight the following:
- **Contributions from the participating governments:**
  - Participating countries contribution, along with other co-financing resources, will be confirmed at sub-project level once specific projects will be discussed and negotiated with each country.
  - The IBB has been working closely with Ministries of Energy and Transport in Latin America and the Caribbean to create a conducive regulatory environment for an accelerated deployment of electric mobility and the GCF Program will support this effort by providing technical assistance and capacity building in such an important area for the decarbonization and increased resilience of LAC.
  - Furthermore, as stated by ITAP under the criteria “Needs of the recipients,” “all Project countries have demonstrated a commitment to co-ordinating transport actors, and to expanding e-mobility in cities.”
- **Assessment of adaptation benefits:**
  - The methodology for estimating physical assets made more resilient to the effects of climate change and $14bn avoided losses of economic assets due to the impact of extreme weather events is based on a combination of two indicators. These are population of Project countries.
  - The methodology to calculate these three indicators is part of the funding Proposal package (Annex 28).

Given its programmatic nature, the assessment of adaptation benefits will be further developed in the sub-project phase. The specific adaptation benefits are related to the type and scope of interventions which, as the pipeline at this stage indicate, have not been defined yet.
- The IBB has also started developing a Disaster Risk Management Plan (DRMP) that will develop an estimate of direct beneficiaries for each adaptation measure and indirect beneficiaries for each adaptation measure. The DRMP is also the tool to identify potential climate change risks and vulnerabilities during the supervision phase of the sub-project and is part of the Environmental and Social Assessment.
- More specifically, the methodology to estimate the number of beneficiaries for each sub-project follows the steps considered in the IBB economic analyses and involves the following: (i) identifying the direct beneficiaries who will receive these benefits as well as indirect beneficiaries and (ii) determining result matrix’s metrics (output and outcome indicators) that will be


---
**Page 135**
---

included in the project Monitoring and Evaluation Plan (MEP) to monitor identified direct beneficiaries.

The Operations Manual will ensure that each sub-project has a localized climate risk and vulnerability assessment. To achieve the goal of financing climate-resilient infrastructure, all eligible sub-projects will complete, during the preparation phase, the identification of adaptation measures and/or climate change resilience interventions to reduce vulnerability and build resilience. IDB will follow the IDB's Disaster and Climate Change Risk Assessment Methodology (ICRA Methodology).

# Gender Assessment

## E-Mobility Program for Sustainable Cities in Latin America and the Caribbean

### 1. INTRODUCTION

Transportation is not gender neutral. Men and women have different socio-economic roles and responsibilities, which are associated with different patterns of use, access and transport needs. Inadequate transport systems can restrict women's access to education, economic opportunities and healthcare.

The nine countries O included in the Program E-Mobility Program for Sustainable Cities in Latin America and the Caribbean - Barbados, Chile, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Jamaica, Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay present similar development indicators in education, employment and political representation. However, significant gender gaps continue to exist for women due to cultural and structural barriers in these countries that limit their participation in the electromobility sector.

### 2. GENDER CONTEXT

Legal, regulatory and institutional framework: The nine countries have ratified or acceded the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW)1 and its optional protocols. They are also parties to the Inter-American Convention Against Torture, Punishment and Eradication of Violence against Women (Convention of Belem Do Paraí). Their constitutions address equality before the law and states no one can be discriminated against on the basis of sex. Most countries have performed a normative reform effort to establish an adequate framework for gender equality but have some gaps in public policies for promoting gender equity However, normative advances still face a deeply patriarchal and androcentric culture that is reflected in many social and institutional practices.

### 3. SOCIOECONOMIC INDICATORS

Education: The literacy rate average 95.5% in the countries of the program and is similar between men and women in all the counties except in Jamaica where the gender gap reaches 9 points of difference (M: 92.7% H: 83.4%). (See Table 1 for detailed information on the indicators described in this section).

1 CEDAW is an international treaty adopted in 1979 by the United Nations General Assembly. Described as an international bill of rights for women, it was initiated on 1 September 1981 and has been ratified by 189 states. The Convention is administered by the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) Commission of Women (CM) of the Organization of American States at a conference held in Belém do Pará, Brazil on 7 June 2004. It is also an rights aiming international treaty that criminalizes all forms of violence against women, especially sexual violence


---
**Page 137**
---

Regarding educational attainment, most of the countries present educational gender gaps for men. In primary education, most countries have similar rates for women and men with the highest gender gap in Barbados, Costa Rica, and Uruguay with more than one child out of two being literate in Jamaica with 81%. In secondary education, the number of women enrolled is higher in all the countries with gender gaps between 8 and 1 points. Paraguay has the lowest enrollment (M:66% M: 65.8%) and the smallest gender gap. Barbados has the highest enrollment and one of the biggest gender gaps (M:74% F:70%). In tertiary education, women are more likely to be enrolled in higher number of enrollments among women with large differences in the number of people enrolled between them, for example, Barbados has 90.6% of women enrolled in tertiary education and 43.0% of men, while in Jamaica 43.7% of women access this type of education compared to 19.9% of men.

Though the percentage of women enrolled in tertiary education is superior to the number of men, the type of education elected is very different, with a low representation of women in STEM programs or engineering careers. Women represent between 6.75% and 12.19% of attainment in STEM and between 4% and 10% in engineering.

Employment. Although women have a higher rate of education than men, they face major inequalities in the labor market. All countries present gaps in the participation of women in the labor force These gaps are wider in some countries such as Costa Rica where only 58.3% of women participate in the labor force compared to 83.9% of men, and smaller in others such as Barbados, where women represent 75.2% compared to 80.4% of men.

The percentage of unemployment among women is higher in all countries. The largest gender gap in unemployment occurs in Jamaica with 16.2%, while the lowest are unemployed compared to 9.29% of men. In Barbados, unemployed women double unemployed men.

Part-time employment has one of the widest gender gaps. All the countries present large gaps that show women occupying these jobs. For example, in Costa Rica 40.1% of females work part-time compared to 70.05% of men. Similar figures can be observed in Colombia (M: 38.72% F: 18.79%) and Dominican Republic (M: 36.2% F: 22%). The gender gap is also present within national sector varies among countries with higher number of women in Chile and Costa Rica and higher number of men in the rest of the countries. There are still gaps in the time women and men dedicate to paid work. Women keep dedicating more time than men which limit their access to paid jobs or increase their vulnerability.

Political representation. Women are poorly represented in the parliaments of their countries. They represent between 15% in Paraguay and 45% of parliamentarians in Costa Rica, following the trend in the rest of the world. (Fig 1)

Violence against women. The percentage of ever-partnered women who have suffered intimate partner physical and/or sexual violence is high in Latin America and the Caribbean. In Colombia and Costa Rica more than 60% of women have suffered that type of violence. Chile is


---
**Page 138**
---

the country with the lower number of women who had reported violence with a 6.7% of prevalence.

# 4. WOMEN-LED BUSINESSES

There is mounting global evidence of a correlation between gender diversity and superior business results and the investment community (Exclusively) has recognized these growth opportunities by investing $6 trillion for Latin America in 2025 in a world in which women participate in the market economy to an identical extent as men. However, gender diversity in LAC is currently low, particularly at leadership levels. The same study concluded that publicly listed companies with higher female representation yielded 44% higher return on investment and 47% higher return on assets.

Despite this evidence, 63% of LAC companies declared that gender diversity is not a strategic priority.

In the countries of the programs, the presence of female top managers in firms is low, with a participation between 4.5% in Chile and 25.4% in Barbados. The figures are, however, very different among the companies where women are majority owners. In Colombia and Paraguay women represent more than 60% of the capital, while in other countries the percentage varies between 24.7 in Panama and 43.4 in Barbados and Costa Rica.

Women represent more than 40% of the economically active population in LAC. Only 17% of them are entrepreneurs in the formal economy. The main barriers they encounter to forming formal companies are (i) difficulties in leaving the informal sector (more than 60% of LAC women work informally), (ii) lack of access to credit and other financial resources, (iii) lack of potential, (iv) gender barriers that prevent them from working longer hours outside the home, (v) ignorance of the financial market and existing opportunities, (vi) reduced levels of financial education, (vii) lack of female role models in business, (viii) lack of support networks and (ix) difficulties to access markets.

According to a study of 25 entrepreneurs in 15 countries in LAC, a high number of women entrepreneurs in the region feel that they have been discriminated against because of their gender (27% versus 4% for men). 50.6% of the women surveyed affirmed that there is a general inequity in entrepreneurial opportunities, including access to resources, adequate training and business support.

The main reason women-led businesses fail is lack of access to capital. LAC is the region with the second highest rate of bankruptcies of companies run by women worldwide. While most male entrepreneurs indicate that they have closed their companies due to low profitability. Women entrepreneurs point to the difficulty of obtaining financing as the main impediment to


---
**Page 141**
---

continue with their businesses. The credit gap in LAC is $5bn for women-led micro businesses and $91bn for women-led SMEs*. The Global Banking Alliance reports that women clients show a stronger saving behavior (16% more likely than men to save for futures), with lower risk (on average, they are more likely to repay loans) and high growth and lower attrition. Evidence shows that financial intermediaries that implement programs that effectively meet the needs of women see excellent financial returns.

# ACCESS TO FINANCIAL RESOURCES

Women tend to face more barriers to access financial services, including accounts in financial institutions, obtaining debit or credit cards, and loans from formal banking institutions for education and enterprise. Limited access to formal financial services and higher loan burdens are due to women's lack to provide collateral for the loans, lack of financial literacy and higher risk aversion in comparison to men; furthermore, these issues deepen when the legal framework to protect creditors and borrowers is weak**.

Currently, the supply of gender-lens financial products in LAC is limited since, on one hand, only a few banks consider gender as a criterion in their investment analysis and, on the other hand, no efforts are made to search for the growing portfolio of women-led businesses. Furthermore, financial intermediaries are dominated by men, especially at decision-making levels.

Despite this, a gender-lens investing for women-led business is emerging in various types of assets in LAC. In public markets, bonds are being created for socially responsible investors interested in promoting gender equality. In private markets, the microfinance sector has a long tradition of gender-lens investing, such as with Microfinanciera Compartamos. Community banks have worked with institutional investors, including multilateral development banks (MDBs), to address the small and medium-sized-enterprise (SME) credit gap faced by women-owned businesses. These banks often provide loans for working capital or business expansion, which are funded by MDBs. In addition, other investments in private markets such as venture capital (VC) and private equity (PE) funds are growing very fast in the region, especially in Brazil, Mexico and Colombia***.

IDB's program Women Entrepreneurship Banking (web) facilitates access to financial services for women-led businesses through a network of banking intermediaries. It has supported 19 banks in 12 countries, approving nearly $800 million in commitments for $5 million in technical assistance, demand-and-market products and services for women-led MSMEs*. The results from the participation in the program are making progressive advancements serving their female customers.

*IDB, ADB, FinDev Gap Report. 2017
**Burkhard, Linnemann et al., Gender Lens Investing: How Finance Can Accelerate Gender Equality in Latin America and the Caribbean. IDB Invest. 2019.

Pro Mujer is a leading microfinance organization in Latin America dedicated to improving the livelihoods of women in Latin America through a combination of financial services and educational services. This integrated approach includes financial services (loans, savings, insurance), training in basic business skills and entrepreneurship, health education (e.g., on gender-based violence, disease prevention, nutrition, family planning) and access to preventive healthcare screenings (cancer screening, diabetes testing, blood pressure checks). In 2017, Pro Mujer disbursed more than USD 393 million in loans and served 276,000 women. Pro Mujer has been officially recognized through a communal bank and its clients have on average repayment rate of 97%.

In 2010, IOB structured and subscribed a Gender-focused Social Bond issue by Banco Davivienda SA (Davivienda). The deal, worth $100 million had a 7-year maturity. This is the world's first issue of a gender-linked bond based on achieving outcomes that are specifically used by the issuer to finance the growth of its women-led SMEs portfolio (WMSMEs), as well as the purchase of social interest houses by women in Colombia.

### Mobility Context for Women

#### 1.1 Women and men patterns
Transportation is not gender neutral. Men and women have different socio-economic roles and responsibilities, which are associated with different patterns of use, access and transport needs. These differences in general behavior in travel and behavior have found patterns that are consistent across developed and developing countries[1]:
- **Women** travel shorter distances and are limited to a more restricted geographical area.
- Women spend less time traveling and cover shorter distances to go to work. They tend to locate their businesses closer to home than men do, which is more likely than men to work at home and less likely to engage in "extreme commuting".
- Women combine more non-work travel while men's mobility revolves around paid work activities.
- **Women** make many multi-stop trips, while men mainly follow more direct, clear patterns from home to workplace. 
- Women are more likely to travel during peak hours.
- Women use public transport more.
- Women walk more than men.
- Women drive less than men.
- Women use cheaper and less efficient modes of transport.

[1] World Bank. 2020. Why does she move? A study of women's mobility in Latin American cities.

### Drivers of gender differences in mobility

The main factors driving these gender differences in transportation include: (i) women's lower financial capacity that makes them more dependent on public transport due to a greater participation in part-time roles and unpaid care work; (ii) the lack of a car owner's financial capacity, and ultimately their inability to pay for public transport; (iii) the unfavorable infrastructure for multi-stops journeys due to women's mobility of care (family errands, care for the elderly, the ill, kids, and their community); which makes transport more expensive for women that pay a higher percentage of their income on transportation. Women also find it more difficult to access informal trips as these are shorter on average; (iv) the greater disadvantages in access to transportation faced by women, since they walk more and depend more on public transport, poor pedestrian pathways, and inconvenient access to transport facilities impact more their daily journeys which are already time-consuming; (v) women also find it more difficult to access appropriate transport for informal job locations; (vi) the fewer transport options for women that rely more on circumferential public transport routes, to which transport operators give lower priority in comparison to the radial commuter routes that connect

12 Granados, Isabel et al. 2015. The relationship between gender and transport. IDB
13 World Bank. 2020. Why does she move? A study of women's mobility in Latin American cities.


---
**Page 144**
---

directly to downtown areas and that often fail to provide adequate coverage of first and last-mile connectivity; and (iv) the lack of personal safety on public transport impacts women that tend to adjust their travel patterns and behavior according to security considerations, including the fear of sexual harassment.

The mobility of care

The mobility of care [1] is not sufficiently accounted for in transportation datasets. The "mobility of care" includes all travel resulting from home and caring responsibilities: shopping for daily living (excluding leisure shopping); household maintenance, organization, and administration; escorting others to appointments or events such as medical visits, school, etc.; and more trips can be hidden under other headings when considering the purpose of trips, such as leisure, strolling, visits, or other trips. Additionally, for the purpose of designing and calculating ridership, data collection methods focus traditionally in one trip pattern (travel to and from work or to and from school) and do not account for the mobility of care because it is fractured in multitude purposes and fall in different categories (shopping, escorting, etc.).

The Figure 3 visualizes the traditionally collected data on trips purpose on the left and the data collected using the concept "mobility of care" on the right. The number of trips for care work and those for paid work are similar for this example's data.

## Care-related trips are considered

Care-related trips are considered as a separate category of public transportation use.

Traditionally, surveys have undervalued the "reproductive" trips, considering only the "productive" trips for the design of transport systems. The chart on the right reclassifies public transportation trips by care and non-care trips into one single category. This new categorization recognizes the importance of caring work and allows transportation engineers to design systems that work well for all segments of the population, improve urban efficiency, and guard against global warming16.

Bogotá, México City and Lima have been classified as the 3 cities in the world with most dangerous public system for women according to a survey17 of 6,550 women in 15 of the 20 biggest capitals in the world, as well as New York. The survey questions related to safety at night, verbal and physical harassment, public response to abuse and confidence in authorities.

The violence in public places, particularly on public transportation, reduces the freedom of movement for women and girls. It restricts their ability to attend school or work and to participate fully in public life, limiting access to essential services and to social and cultural leisure opportunities, and has a negative impact on their health and well-being by limiting the area of travel, increasing their travel costs, constraining social interaction and work choices, and increasing the time they allocate to look after their children.

Six out of 10 women reported to have been harassed in public transportation in Latin American countries. The most common places for harassment are the waiting stops or at all times, including on the street. The riskiest areas apart from the waiting stops were those near bars, often full of men and pedestrian tunnels, according to several studies in Latin American countries. Women's fears are exacerbated by poor public lighting and having to use informal transport, especially at night.

Bus stops are also an issue for women in some cities, particularly on the street or in areas that are typically unsafe for women. For example, in Buenos Aires, women experience 5 times more harassment in buses than in the subway, situation similar, although on a smaller scale, in Santiago de Chile13.

13 Allen, et al., 2018: Bus es menos seguro.Un estudio sobre la seguridad personal de las mujeres y el transporte público en tres ciudades de América Latina. CAF y FIA Foundation.


---
**Page 147**
---

Safety and security are major considerations women make when deciding which mode of transport to use, when alternatives are available[1]. In Buenos Aires, 7% of women would avoid using public transport if there was a high presence of men (in the vehicle or wagon or loitering in the street)[2]. Women also fear being exposed to air pollution and vehicle emissions systems[3]. Although women recognized unwelcome sexual advances as an issue during their trips, a frequent tendency is to regard men’s behavior as normal or to blame the victims. In many cases, women expresses that it was their responsibility to avoid triggering sexual harassment by wearing certain clothes[4].

On the other hand, evidence shows that air pollution impacts women and girls differently and, in some cases, more severely than men and boys. Factors involved may include biological differences, socioeconomic disparities, and unequal gender norms that affect both exposure and solutions[5]. Differences in mobility patterns among women and men make women more vulnerable to exposure to vehicle emissions due to women rely more on public transport and walk more than men.

### 3.1 Labor market

The future of mobility offers the opportunity for faster, cleaner, cheaper and safer transportation. Accompanying those potential changes could be dramatic shifts in the workforce not only in the assembly lines and networks of supply companies, but new opportunities will arise with the creation of jobs linked to the development of infrastructure and the deployment of new energy infrastructure developments needed for the installation of millions of charging points that require changes in the existing infrastructure and the planification of cities; the energy production, transmission and distribution of electricity that is consumed by EVs as well as all the digital innovation necessary for the operation of these vehicles. The fourth industrial revolution is already transforming how they are digitized and women must be trained for this change. The fourth industrial revolution is transforming the world of work, rapidly shifting the boundary between activities performed by humans and those carried out by machines[6]. A study conducted by the International Monetary Fund (IMF) in 30 countries (OECD, Singapore and Cyprus concludes that 11 percent (26 million women) of the female workforce and 9 percent of the male workforce have high automation risk in the next two decades[7].

2. Women’s participation in STEM fields and misperceptions of career pathways, driven by perceptions and misconceptions, only a low percentage of female students choose the STEM fields.

As well as the challenges for retention and career advancement:

1. Glass ceiling blocking ascent to leadership positions. There are persistent barriers to improving women’s representation in senior executive positions and on boards of directors.

2. Work schedules that favor men. Rigid work schedules are one of the key barriers women face, given that, in most societies, they are expected not only to excel at work but also to reliably perform many tasks outside of work, including child rearing, other care-giving responsibilities and various household chores.

3. Wage inequities.


10.2 Transport Gender Lab from the IDB

The IDB created the Transport Gender Lab (TGL) to achieve the incorporation and strengthening of the gender perspective in transport that seek collective solutions and respond to measures and challenges that transcend member countries, with the goal of obtaining greater development benefits and at a lower cost.

* The TGL focuses on generating and disseminating knowledge about the design and implementation of initiatives, activities and/or studies in favor of gender equality in the transport sector. It also seeks to promote the exchange of experiences between cities involved in transportation in the region. It includes a website that gathers all the information available for cities to have direct access to this information. As a lab, it promotes collaborative work among cities through cross-fertilization on good practices and challenges related to gender equality in transport.

The transport Gender Lab classifies these initiatives on seven main areas: Access to justice, prevention, new technological tools, education, knowledge, infrastructure with a gender perspective, and overcoming opportunities. The IDB provides technical support and financial resources to cities so that they can develop context-oriented projects and data gathering to promote a gender perspective in transport. The TGL focuses on two main areas: Women as users and women as part of transportation systems. The cities involved are Bogotá, Colombia; Buenos Aires, Argentina; Cali, Colombia; Guatemala City, Guatemala; Lima, Peru; Mexico City, Mexico; State of Hidalgo, Mexico; Jalisco, Mexico; Quito, Ecuador; San Salvador, El Salvador; Santiago de Chile and Santo Domingo. Thanks to the joint effort of the member cities and entities of the network, more cities and systems in Latin

# WOMEN AND ELECTROMOBILITY

There are few data about women and electromobility, especially in Latin America. Most of the information is related to the US and the European EV market programs. Although the electromobility program does not include private vehicle, the information below is related to women's perception on the EVs and their willingness to buy an electric car or hybrid car.

In the US, only 20% of EV purchases are made by women, even though they purchase more than 50% of new cars. Women influence about 80% of the car buying decisions in a household. In Britain, analysts at AutoCar found that despite women forming a much higher proportion of customers on the site than in traditional dealerships, they are four times less likely to buy an electric vehicle or a hybrid car online than men - and the gap is widening. The top demographic of 2019 EV


---
**Page 152**
---

owners are middle-aged white men earning more than $100,000 annually with a college degree or higher and at least one other vehicle in their household[2].

The reasons for understanding the low attraction of women to EV cars are now well researched. But according to different articles, analyses, and interviews to women, the main reason is that EV cars are not practical for them. Women buy a car if they are not shopping for themselves alone. Women drive more trips with passengers than men do and make more multi-stop trips as well. They are more concerned that men with safety, price, and cargo space to fit the kids. None of the EV cars in the market meet all the requirements. That explains why they are mainly purchasing SUVs than EVs.

One 2018 study by AAA showed women are more likely to cite environmental concerns as a reason for buying an EV than men, percent 90 to 68 percent. But it did not address how many women intended to buy an EV compared with men.

### 7.1 New electromobility initiatives to follow up

There are emergent initiatives in electromobility in Latin America and around the world that can offer some good practices to apply in this program during the next months.

Unconventional jobs on electric mobility for women:

In 2021, supported by the IADB the District Mayor's Office of Bogotá launched the Project for qualification of female drivers as urban transport in Bogotá, a strategy that takes advantage of the energy transition of the transport sector to generate green jobs with Gender approach. The program will make it possible to educate, train and recatgorize the driver's license of 450 selected women, so that they can work with the new District Transport Operator (SITP). With this initiative, in addition to generating employment opportunities for women, the city hopes to reach a goal of 5% of female drivers in the SITP, when today only 1% of the SITP drivers are women. The Inter-American Development Bank, in collaboration with the Center for Economic and Social Studies – CEDE - of the Universidad de los Andes, supports this program, financing and providing technical assistance to the work carried out by CEDE to carry out an impact evaluation of the program, on the quality of women's lives and their performance as SITP electric bus drivers.

EV taxis in Habana

Zacua is the first mexican electric car. It wants to ensure Mexico’s transition towards the use of environmentally friendly energies, designing and producing zero-emission, small, functional and aesthetic urban vehicles

Founded in 2017, the brand opened its assembly plant in Puebla in mid-2018. The assembly line is artisanal: each car is assembled by hand. The Mexican firm has 35 women in its engineering center where they work both in the design of prototypes, development of new projects to the placement of different parts that give life to the cars. The artisan assembly is carried out by a 100 percent female team.

Ola Electric Mobility

It is a new electric-scooter factory that aims to build 10 million two-wheelers annually, or 15% of the world’s scooters by 2022, in an operation run, managed and staffed entirely by women. Led by Bhaskar Aggarwal, its slogan is to provide the world “uban mobility,” a carbon-negative footprint, and “zero emissions,” the founder said. The first group of workers started in September at the factory in Krishnagiri, India, which will cost $330 million to complete. “At full capacity, Futurefactory will employ over 10,000 women, making it the world’s largest women-only factory and the only all-women automotive manufacturing facility globally,” he says.

Mi Transporte Eléctrico

In Jalisco, Mexico, a short public transportation route in the country made up of electric buses called Mi Transporte Eléctrico (My Electric Transport), with a capacity to transport up to 20,000 people a day, started up. The units, driven by 43 women and 36 men, have universal accessibility and a battery-powered electric motor. For three weeks, drivers, with experience in Sistren (public transportation system in Jalisco), received training to become part of the personnel that will serve in these units.

# Women of EVs

An electric vehicle industry that supports diverse people and practices, while creating a welcoming and fair workplace environment with a leadership pipeline from every part of the value chain. WEVs "ellVates" women who are working in the EV industry are making a global movement to "her" from the ground up. A space to coalesce women globally towards the urgent need to rapidly shift to electric transportation. Chapters in United States, Australia, New Zealand and Canada.

# Women in Green Hydrogen

Founded in 2020, Women in Green Hydrogen (WiGH) is a network of women with the goal of connecting, empowering, and boosting the visibility of women in the sector. Women in Green Hydrogen currently counts 1,444 members and 453 experts in its database. They offer a hydrogen industry-specific global mentoring program for women in junior/middle management positions. More than 300 women from over 42 countries joined the last networking event on "Wind of Change" in April 2021.

# Gender Assessment by Country

Data availability is a challenge shared by the nine countries. The Gender Assessments by country were built with available data. Some of the data currently missing include employment data, by position levels in the transport sector per country; mobility data; violence against women in the public transportation sector; and gender pay gap data. The Program will conduct surveys to fill the data gaps and produce new and gender-aware data as a new resource for policy and decision-makers.

All projects (concessions loans and grants for pilot projects) financed by the Program will conduct a detailed and context-specific gender gap analysis. This gender analysis will be more detailed than the country assessment as it will include qualitative (interviews, focus groups) and quantitative elements (census data, demographics, income, etc.). Additionally, these analyses will be conducted in the project interest areas. The gender gaps might vary depending on the type of project and the context. Using preliminary information from the country assessments. Based on that analysis each project will define actions to close the gender gap identified and project indicators to measure the progress.
