# BARBADOS

## Legal, regulatory, and institutional framework

Barbados Constitution (1966) and its reforms states every person in Barbados has individual rights and freedoms without discrimination as to race, origin, political opinion, color, creed, or sex.

The country has a Bureau of Gender Affairs to promote the integration of the gender perspective in national development policies and plans. Barbados has also ratified in 1993 the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995). The government has also implemented Shelter for Abused Women, counseling spaces and research[28].

### Law/Document
#### Domestic violence (1993)
- **Content**: Provides provisions for the protection of victims in the case of intimate partner violence.

#### Constitution of Barbados (1966) and reforms
- **Content**: Every person in Barbados has individual rights and freedoms without discrimination as to race, origin, political opinion, color, creed or sex. Extends the classes of persons who are victims of domestic violence; ensures victim-recent counseling or therapy facilities for the problems of perpetrators in the appropriate rehabilitative programs; establishes the duties and powers of members of the Police Force, among others.

#### Domestic Violence (Protection Orders) (Amendment) Act, 2016
- **Content**: Extends the classes of persons who are victims of domestic violence; ensures victim-recent counseling or therapy facilities for the problems of perpetrators in the appropriate rehabilitative programs; establishes the duties and powers of members of the Police Force, among others.

#### The Employment Sexual Harassment (Prevention) Act, 2017
- **Content**: Protects employees in both the public sector and private sector from sexual harassment at their workplace. Provides for the reporting of sexual harassment and method of resolving such cases; among others.

#### The Trafficking in Persons Prevention Act, 2016
- **Content**: Updates the legislation to prevent, suppress and eradicate trafficking in persons, especially Women and Children.

#### Sexual Offences Act Cap 154, 2018
- **Content**: Amended law for intimate partner violence/domestic violence, to provide for the circumstances under which a man can be prosecuted for raping his wife.

#### The Barbados National Strategic Plan 2005 – 2020 (2005)
- **Content**: Among its objectives, it proposes incorporating the gender perspective in all areas of national development plans and policies so that women and men can benefit equally from existing opportunities.

Educational gaps

The completion rate for primary female education in 2012 was 99.5%, higher than men with 97.5%. However, in lower secondary education men reported a higher completion rate with 98.5% compared to 97.9% women[3]. There are no significant differences in the rate of out-of-school children between males and females at secondary education, since the value is 2.3% for men and 2.5% for women[4]. The gender attendance gap in tertiary education was 10%, with a female completion rate for 2012 was 65.8%, higher than ratio for men with 40.29%. Younger cohorts (below 40) are more educated, but males mostly stable[5].

Economic Opportunities

In 2017 the labor force participation of women aged 15+ was 62%, meaning a lower participation compared to men with 70%. For the same year, there were no significant differences between men and women regarding the unemployment rate of people aged 15+, with 10% for women and 9% for men[6]. There are even employment levels in 15-25 age range[7]. By 2017 employment in service-related activities had a higher women participation with 89% of the female working force, compared to only 43% that have male counterparts. In agriculture and fishing, there were only 9% of women. And agriculture have 2% of women working force and 4% of male[8]. On average, women's income is about 60% of men’s in Latin America and the Caribbean, but in Barbados women’s income is about 86.8%. According to the Barbados Survey of Living Conditions 2016, 4.15% of the female population is in extreme poverty compared to the 2.74% of men. This trend repeats for poor women which represents 31.92%, while for men the value is 15.96[9].

# Educational gaps

Chile has made important advances regarding education indicators for both, women and men. In the education category there is a slight gap in favor of women. However, female labor force participation rate is significantly lower than men's net primary school enrollment rate for men and women are 95% compared to 80% for both genders and the completion rate is slightly higher for women with 92% compared to men with 90%.41 In 2017, net secondary school enrollment ratio was 88% for women and 85% for men; gross secondary school enrollment ratio (of persons age group) was 98% for women and 83% for men. Gross tertiary school graduation rate is 13% for women and 12% for men in 2016.

According to the Gender equality education plan 2015-2018, the differences in the dropout rates are more significant when comparing the level of basic and secondary education; which, when comparing by sex, the reasons that explain the dropouts are quite different. In addition to the economic factors, women have a greater difficulty in balancing their studies with work. Additionally, in Professional Technical Education, women make up more than 80% of the specializations in secretarial, early childhood education, nursing, and social service areas. Men, on the other hand, are concentrated in the areas of construction and works, topography, systems analysis, and computer science.

The most significant differences between higher education by women in 2016 were linked to health, design, and social sciences; while the most masculinized correspond to the area of science and mathematics

## Access to Financial Resources

In 2017, 77% of men in our country had accounts in financial institutions. In comparison to 71% of women (% of population over 15 years old); for the same year, more men than women obtained loans from financial institutions (17% and 10% respectively).42 Chile's gender gap regarding access to loans from the formal financial sector is higher than Latin America and upper-middle income countries (9% of women vs. 11% of men; for both).43

Loan collaterals and guarantees tend to be stricter for women and regulatory requirements limit their access to financing. In Chile, these barriers impact the percentage of people who have access to emergency funds and women are disproportionately affected (only 12% of women vs. 55% of men have access to emergency funds). Additionally, only 13% of Chilean women have savings to start, operate or expand a farm or business, significantly lower than the percentage of men (21%). Economic Opportunities

The labor force participation rate gender gaps have had modest improvements between 2000 and 2017. Female labor force participation rate (of women over 15 years old) in Chile had a considerable increase, while the rate for men ages 15-24 stayed the same for women. In 2017, labor force participation rate (% of population over 15 years old) was 74% for men and 51% for women, in comparison to 76% and 38%, respectively, in 2000. For young populations ages 15-24, female youth labor force participation rate has decreased (2000: M: 67%; F: 47%. 2017: M: 61%; F: 45%). Furthermore, working conditions are similar between men and women; a slightly higher percentage of women are salaried workers (M: 71%; W: 72%), the percentage of women and men in vulnerable employment is slightly higher for women (M: 23%; W:25%) as well as unemployment rate (M: 7%; W:8%). The ILO estimates that female labor force participation is significantly lower than male in the transport, storage, and communication sectors (of the total of people employed in these sectors, 80% are men and only 20% are women), and in the mining and quarrying, electricity, gas and water supply (of the total of people employed in these sectors, 88% are men and only 12% are women).

# Gender Based Violence

The results of a 2015 survey from the Observatory against harassment in Chile showed that 3 of every 4 people in Chile suffered in the last 12 months some kind of sexual harassment on public spaces. When it is analyzed by gender, 85% of women experienced it and 55% of women. 23% of the respondents suffered severe harassment such as persecution, exhibitionism, manipulation.

Young women were more affected than young men: 70% of them had been sexually harassed in the last year, half at least once a week and two out of 10 daily. In 93% of cases, the harassment is perpetrated by a man. Of the total number of victims, 99% of the women and 50% of the men have been harassed by a man or a group of men.

In Chile, 18.5% of LGBTQ+ people aged 15 or older have been sexually assaulted at some time in their life for being perceived as an LGBTQ+ person (MSP, 2013). The personal experiences of victims of LGBTQ+ people in Chile occur in public spaces such as streets, parks, or transportation [20.6%].

# COLOMBIA

## Legal, regulatory, and institutional framework.

The Political Constitution of Colombia on the Article 43 establishes that "women and men have equal rights and opportunities; women may not be subjected to any kind of discrimination", and in its article 13 "that the State will promote the conditions for that equality is real and effective and will adopt measures in favor of discriminated or marginalized groups."

The National Development Plan 2018-2022 "Pacts per Colombia" for the first time, specific chapter 4.10 "Gender Equality", which through (b) lines of policy addresses aspects to advance in the guarantee of the rights and interests of various sectors of women: (i) Strengthening gender institutions in Colombia (ii)Education and economic empowerment to eliminate gender gaps in the world of work (iii) Care, access to justice and participation and co-responsibility; (iv) Gender equality in decision-making processes and decision-making; (v) Promotion of sexual health and reproductive rights for children and adolescents; (vi) Rights of women to a life free from violence; (vii) Rural women as agents of transformation in the countryside; (viii) Equality of women for the construction of peace.

Policy for the Prevention, Protection and Guarantee of the Rights of Women during the Armed Conflict.

National Public Policy on Gender Equity and creates an Intersectional Commission for its implementation.

Mandates norms of awareness, prevention and punishment of forms of violence and discrimination against women.

### Educational gaps

Colombia has made important advances regarding education indicators for both, women and men. In the education category there is a slight gap in favor of women. Net primary school enrollment rate declined slightly between 2000 and 2017 (2000: Women 94%, Men 95%; 2017: Women 93%, Men 91%), but the completion rate increased from 68% for women in 2000 to 76% in 2017. Net secondary school enrollment rate was 62% for women and 76% for men, and gross tertiary school enrollment ratio (% of relevant age group) was 65% for women and 56% for men. Gross tertiary graduation rate was 30% for women and 21% for men in 2017, in comparison to 5% and 4%, respectively, in 2000. Female gross enrollment in tertiary education has been increasing, especially female gross enrollment in STEM fields. The female shares of graduates from STEM decreased from 37% to 34% between 2000 and 2017.

### Access to Financial Resources

In 2017, 49% of men in the country had accounts in financial institutions, in comparison to 41% of women (% of population over 15 years old); for the same year, more men than women obtained loans from financial institutions (16% and 13% respectively). Colombia's gender gap

Gender Based Violence

According to the SIEDCO (Sistema de Información Estadística, Delincuencial, Contraconvencional y Operativa de la Policía Nacional) between January 2019 and January 2022, 97,373 cases of sexual violence were reported. Of that total, 85% (83,873) were committed against women, while 15% (14,364) against men.

The Transliminal sexual harassment against women survey, conducted in 2017, found that women's security perception when using the system was low: 30% of female users feels very insecure and 54% feels insecure. Additionally, 65% of surveyed women said they had suffered sexual harassment by drivers and 89% felt unsafe to use public transport in Bogotá.⁷⁰

To address the gender gaps in the sector, the Transportation Ministry created the Sectoral Committee for the implementation of the Gender Equality Public Policy. This Committee is responsible of incorporating women's needs in the sector's plans, projects, and strategies.⁷¹

COSTA RICA

Legal, regulatory and institutional framework


Costa Rica is one of the countries in Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) that has historically demonstrated an important commitment to gender equality and the empowerment of women.

In 1986, the country ratified the "Convention for the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women" (CEDAW) of 1979 and its optional protocol in 2001. Subsequently, it approved a series of laws and regulations with the aim to achieve this objective. Some examples can highlight: The Reform of Articles 58 and 60 of the Electoral Law in 1996 that obliges political parties to contemplate a minimum quota of 40% of women in different political positions; the establishment of the National Institute for Gender Equality (INEG) in 1997 as part of supervising all policies related to gender issues, the implementation of the National Policy for Gender Equality and Fight (PIEG) 2007-2017 and respective action plans; and the creation, to date, of 67 Municipal Women's Offices and 26 gender units in Ministries, in the Legislative Assembly, in the Judiciary, the Costa Rican Institute of Electricity (ICE) and the Supreme Electoral Tribunal[72].

| Law/Document | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Labor Code (1944) | It establishes that the amount of the salary in each class or category must be equal. The same quality and no differences can be established based on sex. |
| Law for the Promotion of Social Equality for Women (1990) | It establishes that the State must promote and guarantee equal rights between men and women in the political, economic, social, and cultural fields. |
| Law 7.621 reforms the Labor Code (1996) | It establishes working parents will have the right to support their children from birth until they are three years old. Those with limited economic resources will also have the right to receive a subsidy from the State. In art.94 the employer is prohibited from firing pregnant and lactating women. |
| Creation of the National Institute for Women (1998) | Compulsory paid maternity leave during the month prior to delivery and the three months after. The worker has additional rights such as the right to a three-month license. Parental license. |
| Law of Care for women in conditions of poverty (1998) | Promotes and protects the human rights of women. It establishes a national policy for gender equality and equity. |

# Political Constitution (Reform of 1999)

It is a fundamental right that women be treated in equal before the law and no discrimination contrary to human dignity may be practiced.

## Municipal Code (Reforms of 2008)

It is established that municipalities must promote local public policies for the promotion of women's rights and citizenship, in favor of gender equality and equity.

# Gender Based Violence

* Law 8.805 (2010). Amendment to the Law Against Sexual Harassment in Employment and Teaching
* Law 8.925 (2011). Amendment to the Law against Domestic Violence.

# Creation of the Gender Equality and Equity Management System (SIGEG), national technical standard (2013)

It is a tool for organizations that decide on a voluntary basis, to get involved in the process of recognizing the gender equality gap. It allows them to subsequently apply corrective measures to eliminate them.

# National Employment and Production Strategy 2014

National Development Plan (2014)

* Defines as one of the pillars to achieve gender equality and equal citizenship: the ability of women to generate income, manage their own assets and resources (economic autonomy).

# National Policy for Gender Equality and Equity 2007-2017

Promotes affirmative actions for vertical and horizontal mobility in the public sector. Such as definition of minimum percentages for hiring women in managerial positions, training of women in highly segregated professional areas (scientific and technological careers).

## Educational gaps

Specifically regarding advances in educational participation, the gender gap in the enrollment of girls and boys in primary school is almost non-existent (96.2% and 96.1% in 2014, respectively). However, at secondary and tertiary education, the gap begins to increase, with women having a higher percentage of enrollment in both levels (88.3% vs. 76.2% and 58.8% vs. 47.3% in 2014, respectively.)79

## Economic Opportunities

73 Banco Mundial (2014). Gender Data Portal: Costa Rica.


---
**Page 170**
---

Although Costa Rica has witnessed advances in terms of gender, one of the most important challenges for the country is its low WTP to help close these gaps in female economic participation. The latest data from International Labour Organization (ILO) 2012 reveal that female labor participation (43.5%) is well below male participation (75.9%), being one of the countries in the region with the lowest female participation in the labor market. Additionally, when this analysis is done by areas, it can be seen that the gaps between men and women in rural areas are even more pronounced than those found in urban areas. The differences between rural and urban women are also important (8.6 percentage points). This is since formal or quality job options in rural areas are extremely limited due to limited access, among other things, and to this are added the gender stereotypes that still attribute to women the responsibilities of housework, home care, and reproduction. In fact, 30% of women work in lower quality jobs, compared to 15.1% of men.

To this it can be added that the country presents an important occupational segregation of gender. In fact, women are overrepresented in sectors such as household, education and social professions; sectors that tend to be low paid but also underrepresented in areas such as transport and construction where men have higher representation with more stable jobs and better social benefits. For example, women represent almost 70% of all employees in the education sector but represent less than 15% and 5% of employees in the transport and construction sectors respectively. In the same line of thought, a figure by occupation by area for the year 2014, the percentage of participation of women is in the construction and transportation sectors is even lower: 0.8% and 1.5%, respectively.

It can be concluded, then, that rural women have less access to jobs and their own income, which results in a greater situation of vulnerability and poverty. In 2014, 22.7% of rural women are in a situation of poverty compared to 21.4% of men and 46.4% of rural women do not have their own income, compared to 31.2% of urban women and 12.4% of rural men.

Access to Financial Resources:

In 2017, 75% of men in Costa Rica had accounts in financial institutions, in comparison to 61% of women (6% of the population 15 years old); for the same year, more men than women obtained loans (15% and 13% respectively).


---
**Page 171**
---

Loan collaterals and guarantees tend to be stricter for women and regulatory requirements limit their access to financing.[80] In Costa Rica, these barriers impact the percentage of people who have access to emergency funds and women are disproportionately affected (only 37% of women vs. 54% of men have access to emergency funds).[81] Additionally, only 26% of Costa Rican women have savings to start, operate or expand a farm or business, slightly lower than the percentage of men [19%].[82]

### Gender Based Violence

In Costa Rica, the number of victims under the Law Against the Domestic Violence increased in 2020, 2,549 more victims than 2019, reaching a total of 10,779 individuals, of which 76% were women.[83] In 2020, 19 femicides were recorded, representing 33% of females being victims of violent deaths.[84]

Legal, regulatory, and institutional framework

The 2010 Dominican Republic Constitution establishes equal rights for men and women (art. 39, 42, 62), including the right to work and to personal integrity. The country has the National Plan for Equality and Gender Equity 2020-2030 (PLANEG III) lead by the Ministry of Women created by Law 86-99.

The PLANEG III is aligned with the National Development Strategy and the Sustainable Development Goals. The Plan promotes economic decision making and physical autonomy of women18. Regarding domestic violence and gender-based violence, Law 24-97 (art.309, 330-333) recognizes it as a crime and defines its penalties in these cases. To promote women participation in elections, electoral laws have been modified to allow the appointment of women that have to be part of the candidates and for occupy deputy or municipal positions12.

Law 87 (2001)
It establishes that the Dominican Social Security System (SSS) will develop childcare services to care for the children of workers from 45 days old until they are five years old.

National Plan for Gender Equality and Equity 2007-2017 (PLANEG II)
The plan defines 7 priority national issues through which gender equality is promoted by women in Dominican society should be addressed. For each of these national issues, the current context is analyzed in relation to the objectives set forth as a basis for the construction of equality and equity between genders, and from this a set of objectives is derived that seeks to address the identified problems. From there, areas of intervention and priority lines of action are identified that should guide the concrete actions to be developed by public and private co-executing institutions of PLANEG II.

Political Constitution (2010)
Women and men are equal before the law. Any act that has for object or effect the nullifying, limiting, or annuling the recognition, enjoyment, or exercise under equal conditions of the fundamental rights of women and men is prohibited. Necessary measures will be promoted to guarantee the eradication of inequalities and gender discrimination. The State guarantees the equality and equity of women and men in the enjoyment of their rights to work.

Promulgation of resolution No. 214-13
Extension of maternity leave to 14 weeks.

Criminal Code (Law 550/2014)
It typifies Femicide.

Educational gaps
Although gender gaps in the access and level of education of women have reduced in the past year (for example, literacy rate for people aged 15+ is at the same for both genders[89]), there are still some differences. World Bank data from 2018 show women (96.9%) have higher primary education completion than men (91.2%). Similar situation happens regarding the completion rate for lower secondary education which for women is 91.5% and for men 81.4%. This trend is

88 UN Women data, https://data.unwomen.org/country/dominican-republic
89 World Bank data sets, (2019).


---
**Page 174**
---

consistent with the increment on the average schooling years, which changed from 8.4 years in 2008 to 9.1 years in 2015[9].

Although the rate of enrollment for primary and lower secondary education for women is 73% and for male 74.9%, according to UNICEF data (2016), the reasons for early school drop have differentials related to gender. The main reasons for quit school for men are preferences to work instead of study (45.7%), no liking school (17%) and lack of financial resources (16.5%). Meanwhile, for women the main reason is house chores (21.3%), lack of money (16.5%), work (15.7%), and marriage, pregnancy and taking care of own children (9.7%). This causes reflect persistent gender inequalities.

The gross attendance ratio for tertiary education is higher for women than men. The attendance of female population is 122%[10] and for male 71.5%. Students' distribution by gender reveals there are more women in careers like art and humanities (81% are women), health (80% are women), and education (79% are women). While from the total number of students in engineering, 23% are women[11]. Yet, the female share of graduates from STEM in this country is 40%.

### Economic Opportunities

According to the Ministry of Economy, Planning and Development, women poverty is higher than for men. The general poverty rate for women in 2020 was 24.61% which increased up to 25.78% in 2021. While for men it went from 22.39% in 2020 to 23.58% in 2021. On the other hand, the poverty femininity index was (25-59). In 2020, there were 138 women poor for every 100 men in the same situation. But for 2021, it increased to 150 poor women per every 100 men. The higher value since 2016[12]. On the other hand, the labor force participation rate of women (over 15 years old) was 48% in 2020 and decreased for women in 2021. This value has increased if it is compared with 2013, when 52.52% of women and 78.21% of men participated in the labor force. This date evidence economic dependency of women and reduce household incomes, that

Unemployment rate for women in 2020 was 8.94%, which means more than the dole of men with 4.21%. A data is compared to 2010, women unemployment rate with 9.25% and men with 3.65%, while unemployment since 201698.

Gender differences are also evident in the way people distribute their time for paid and unpaid activities. While women dedicate 31.2 hours per week to unpaid work (meaning 16.2% of their time dedicated to domestic chores and care work), men expend 5.6 weeks hourly (equivalent to the 9.6% of their time)99, which mean one third of the time a woman could for unpaid work. Situation is inverser regarding paid work, since women use 18 hours of their time and men 37.2 hours100.

Regarding women participation in the different economic sectors for 2018, there is a higher employment of this gender in services with 89% for women and 55% for men. While in the industry the figure is inverted: 26% of men working in this area and 10% of women. For agriculture situation is similar, since 15% of the men work in this sector, compared to only 3% of women, showing a masculinized activity101.

The ILO estimates that female labor force participation is significantly lower than male in the transport, storage and communication sectors (of the total of people employed in these sectors, there are men and only 7% are women), and in the mining and quarrying, electricity, gas and water supply (of the total of people employed in these sectors, 67% are men and only 13% are women)102.

Access to Financial Resources

Although there are differences in the access to bank account per gender, these are not significant since the 53% of women in Dominican Republic report have an account, compared to the 56% of men. A similar situation happened with people who borrow from a financial institution, since the percentage is the same for men and women [38%]. On the other hand, the

Gender Based Violence

According to the Experimental survey about women from 2018, 68.8% of women (aged +15) have experienced violence along their live. Values are higher for rural areas with 71.9% while in cities it is 61.9%. The rate of violence in the past 12 months remains high with 55.9% in general, but 68.3% in rurality and 54.8% in urban zones. More than half of women (51.7%) have experience violence along their live in the public sphere [labor, education and communitarian or social spaces], and 52.7% in the private dimension. Regarding violence in the public sphere, 84.3% of declared it happened in public places, while for the private sphere is been at 61.5%. About 90.5% experienced violence from a partner. About violence in the private sphere, the 41.8% of women experienced it from their current or last partner[104]. Regarding early marriage, the proportion of women aged 20-24 years who were married or in a union before age 18 is 35.9% and for women before age 15 is 12.3%. [105]

| Law on sexual crimes (2009) | Provides new provisions in case of rape and other sexual crimes. It also provides for the creation of a Registry of Sexual Offenders. |

### Educational gaps

In the education category Jamaica's net secondary school enrollment rate for men and women in 2017 was lower with 71% and 77% respectively compared to 75% and 78% in 2000. Similar trends were noted for a lower secondary completion rate with 97% for men and 98 for women in 2000 and 86% and 83% respectively in 2017.

### Economic Opportunities

The labor force participation rate gender gaps have had modest improvements between 2000 and 2017. Female labor force participation rate (of women over 15 years old) in Jamaica had a slight increase, while the rate for women ages 15-24 had a slight decrease. In 2017, labor force participation rates of women aged 15-24 was 68% for men and 60% for women, in comparison to 77% and 67%, respectively, for the same age group in 2000. Female, male and labor male force participation rate has decreased (2000: M: 52%; F: 34%. 2017: M: 45%; F: 36%). [108]

Furthermore, working conditions are similar between men and women: a slightly higher percentage of women are salaried workers (Men: 56%, W: 67%), the percentage of women and men in vulnerable employment[109] is similar (Men: 40%; W:33%). However, unemployment rate is higher for women than for men (7%; W: 12%). [108]

According to the Statistical Institute of Jamaica (SOI), estimated for October 2019, female participation in the transport, storage and communication sector was 21.10% of the total people employed in the sector; 21% for the mining and quarrying, electricity, gas and water supply

Access to Financial Resources

In 2014, 79% of men in the country had accounts in financial institutions, in comparison to 78% of women (% of population over 15 years old); for the same year, men than women obtained loans from financial institutions with the same rate (11% for both).112 Jamaica's gender gap regarding access to and use of financial services is similar to Latin America and upper-middle income countries (9% of women vs. 11% of men, for both).

Loan collaterals and guarantees tend to be stricter for women and regulatory requirements limit their access to financing.113 In Jamaica, these barriers impact the percentage of people who have access to emergency funds and women are disproportionately affected (only 54% of women vs. 64% of men accessing emergency funds).114 Additionally, 125% more women than men save savings to start, operate or expand a farm or business, significantly lower to the percentage of men [1998].115

# PANAMA

## Legal, regulatory, and institutional framework

The Political Constitution of Panama (1972) establishes that "women and men have equal rights and opportunities; women may not be subjected to any kind of discrimination", and in its article 13 that "the State will promote the conditions for that equality is real and effective and will adopt measures in favor of discriminated or marginalized groups".

## Law/Documents^118

| Content |
| --- |
| Article 2 establishes the principle of non-discrimination, equal pay under equal conditions without distinction and the protection of maternity. |
| Art. 10, 105, 106, 107, 114, 116: Establishes the equality of legal pay (Art. 10), maternity protection and the right to breastfeeding (Art. 105, 106, 107, 114, 116). |
| Law No. 3 of May 17, 1994^119: Approves the Family Code that protects the rights of married women or women in common law, in case of divorce in relation to property, that as a partner of marriage has the right to receive compensation for her work and sacrifices. |
| Law No. 12 of April 20, 1995: Approves the Inter-American Convention to prevent, punish, and eradicate violence against women. Concerning Do's To Women. |
| Law No. 50 of November 23, 1995: Protects and encourages breastfeeding. |
| Law No. 4 of January 29, 1999: Institutes Equal Opportunities for Women. |
| Law No. 17 of March 26, 2001: Approves the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). |
| Law No. 38 of July 10, 2001: Reforms and adds articles to the Criminal and Judicial Code on the prevention of violence and abuse of children and adolescents, repeals articles of law 27 of 1995 and dictates other provisions. |
| Law No. 16 of March 31, 2004: Establishes measures for the prevention of crimes against integrity and sexual freedom, modifies and adds articles to the Penal and Judicial Code. |
| Law No. 11 of April 22, 2005: Prohibits employment discrimination and adopts other measures. |

Educational gaps

Panama has made considerable progress in terms of education indices for both men and women. In the educational category there is a slight gap in favor of women. net primary school enrollment rate decreased between 2000 and 2017 (Women: 95%, Men: 93%; 2017: Women: 87%, Men: 87%), but the completion rate increased for women and men. In 2017, net secondary school enrollment rate was 72% for women and 67% for men, and gross tertiary school enrollment ratio (% of relevant age group) was 58% for women and 37% for men. Gross tertiary graduation ratio was 33% for women and 18% for men in 2017; in comparison to 30% and 13%, respectively, in


---
**Page 183**
---

2000.[1] Gross enrollment in tertiary education has been increasing, especially female gross enrollment, including the female shares of graduates from STEM increasing from 41% to 49% between 2000 and 2017.[2]

Access to Financial Resources

In 2017, 53% of men in Panama had accounts in financial institutions, in comparison to 42% of women (of population over 15 years old); for the same year, more men than women obtained loans (9% and 7% respectively).[3] Panama's gender gap regarding access to loans from the formal financial sector is similar than Latin America and upper-middle income countries (9% of women vs. 11% of men, for both). [4]

Loan collateral and guarantees tend to be stricter for women and regulatory requirements limit their access to borrowing.[5] In Panama, these barriers impact the percentage of people who have access to emergency funds; and women are disproportionately affected (only 36% of women vs. 51% of men have access to emergency funds).[6] Additionally, only 13% of Panamanian women have savings to start, operate or expand a farm or business, significantly lower to the percentage of men in Panama.[7]

Economic Opportunities

The labor force participation rate gender gaps have had modest improvements between 2000 and 2017. Female labor force participation rate (of women over 15 years old) in Panama had a slight increase, while the rate for women ages 15-24 had a slight decrease. In 2017, labor force participation rate (% of population over 15 years old) was 80% for men and 53% for women, in comparison to 81% and 45%, respectively, in 2000.[8] For young population, ages 15-24, female

Gender Based Violence

Panama is one of the countries with lower rates of GBV (Gender Based Violence) in the region with 128 cases per 100,000 people compared to the regional average with 256.111 On the National Survey of sexual and reproductive health in 2014-2015, 34% of the female respondents experienced physical, emotional, or sexual violence at least once in their lifetime. Between January and November of the year 2018, 18 females and 14 attempted femicides were registered throughout the country, a majority were registered in the city of Panama.112 Likewise, 14,365 cases of family violence and 2,348 cases of rape were reported during the same period113

# PARAGUAY

## Legal, regulatory, and institutional framework

Paraguay has taken various measures to ensure gender equality with the adoption of the new Constitution of 1992 and has developed an institutional structure in women's rights, such as the constitution of the Ministry of Women and the execution of the III National Plan of Equal Opportunities between Women and Men 2008-2017.

| Law/Document | Content |
| --- | --- |
| Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) | Ratified in 1987. Optional Protocol ratified in 2001. Mandatory reference on equality between men and women. With their ratification or accession, States are legally bound to eliminate discrimination in all areas, including laws and temporary extraordinary measures, so that women have the full enjoyment of all their human rights and fundamental freedoms. |
| Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995) | The signatory countries assume the responsibility of implementing the Platform for Action in which 12 critical areas of concern are identified on which the rights of women are defined. |
| Political Constitution (1992) | Art. 48: Of the civil, political, social, economic, and cultural rights. It establishes that the State will promote the conditions and create the appropriate environment for their full enjoyment and effective, smoothing out the obstacles that prevent or hinder its exercise and facilitating the participation of women in all areas of public life. Reform to the Political Constitution to explain legal equality between women and men, Law 19.611 (1999). |
| Labor Code (1993) | It ensures that women enjoy the same labor rights and have the same obligations as men. |
| Criminal Code (1997) | The crime of violence against sexual autonomy has been introduced in the current Penal Code. |
| Law 1,600 against domestic violence (2000) | A provisional measure to protect the life of the victim of violence, regardless of whether they live with the aggressor. It stipulates protection measures, dictated by the Justice of the Peace of the area, anticipating police protection. |


---
**Page 186**
---

Ministry of Women (2012)

Promotes and implements public policies with a gender perspective, for the full enjoyment of women’s rights.

Plan for Equality between Women and Men 2008-2017

Promotes the incorporation of the gender perspective in the preparation, coordination, execution, monitoring and evaluation of public policies through efficient regulatory instruments and actions aimed at eliminating all forms of gender discrimination, as well as equal opportunities and results, favoring the democratization of society.

National Development Plan 2014-2030

It includes gender equality policies to achieve equal opportunities and treatment between women and men.

Law 5.508 on the promotion, protection of maternity and support for breastfeeding (2013)

The law is intended to promote, protect, and support breastfeeding from birth until the child is two years old. It establishes that every worker shall have the right to fully access the Maternity Leave for a period of 18 (eighteen) uninterrupted days, in addition to granting, "in an inalienable way", in its article 13, to every worker father of a newborn, 2 (two) weeks after childbirth, with pay, at the employer's expense.

Educational gaps

In Paraguay, net primary school enrollment rate was 98% for women and 97% for men in 2000. However, the rate for secondary and tertiary enrollment is lower than the region’s average. In the country, net secondary school enrollment rate was 54% for women and 51% for men, and gross tertiary school enrollment ratio (% of relevant age group) was 18% for women and 14% for men (ibid).

Access to Financial Resources

In 2017, 33% of men in Paraguay had accounts in financial institutions, in comparison to 29% of women (% of the population 15 years old); for the same year, more men than women obtained loans (16% and 11% respectively).


---
**Page 187**
---

Loan collateral and guarantees tend to be stricter for women and regulatory requirements limit their access to financing.[137] In Paraguay, these barriers impact the percentage of people who have access to emergency funds and women are disproportionately affected (only 48% of women vs. 61% of men have access to emergency funds).[138] Additionally, only 5% of Paraguayan women have savings to start, operate or expand a farm or business, slightly lower than the percentage of men (12%).[139]

### Economic Opportunities

In Paraguay, the participation of women in the labor force remains significantly lower than that of men. In 2017, the participation of women in the labor force was 50%, while that of men was 84%.[137] The participation rate of women in the labor force (of all the total women over 15 years of age) increased 4 percentage points in the period 2000-2017, while that of men (of the total of men over 15 years of age) decreased 2 percentage points.[138] Additionally, the participation of young people (between 15–24 years old) has decreased, for women it decreased from 48% in 2000 to 37% in 2017 and for men it decreased from 69% to 55%. The employment conditions for women and men have improved; the percentage of salaried people increased[139] and the percentages of people in vulnerable employment[140] and unemployment decreased.[141] However, the percentage of women in vulnerable employment and unemployed conditions continues to be higher than that of men and the gaps in these indicators have increased between 2000 and 2017. According to the ILO, in Paraguay, only 35% of women are employed in the informal sector while the majority of women work in the service sector (78% compared to 51% of men).[141] In 2019, the ILO estimated that female labor force participation was significantly lower than male in the transport, storage and communication sectors (of the total people employed in these sectors, 87% are men and only 13% are women), and in the mining and quarrying, electricity, gas and water supply.

water supply (of the total of people employed in these sectors, 73% are men and only 27% are women) [146]

The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) carried out a gender study and analysis of the value chain of the transport sector in Paraguay. The study found that in rural areas, 42% of women lack their own income and in cases where they are part of the labor force, their income represents 62.3% less than that of their male peers. The study also noted the low participation of women in non-traditional sectors such as road construction and maintenance, even when there is a labor shortage. The low participation of women in these sectors is because job offers are not directed at women or to stereotypes and cultural and social norms [147]. As a result of the diagnosis, a gender action plan was developed with two main improvements: i) within the Neighborhood Roads Improvement Program. The pilot focused on: i) promoting a training, awareness and dissemination plan on gender aspects related to the participation of women in non-traditional trades of civil works and maintenance, with a view to their labor insertion; and ii) promoting the inclusion of women in the construction sector by providing them with tools for working in works to improve and maintain the program. Specifically, the training activities and interventions showed positive findings regarding the self-worth of women in non-traditional jobs, and an improvement in the work environment of the work camps, through coexistence manuals and awareness talks.

Gender Based Violence

In Paraguay between 60-80% of violence against women is perpetrated by the survivor's current or former partner (husband, concubine, ex-husband and ex-concubine, boyfriend) [148]. Additionally, of the total number of femicides in the country, around 90% are perpetrated by the victim's partner. The majority of victims of violence are women between the ages of 25 years to 35-40 years old, and psychological violence is the type of violence with higher incidence (55%) followed by physical (40%). [149] Regarding safety in public transportation, a survey in Asunción found that around 78% of transport users felt unsafe at least once using the transportation system in Asunción.

# URUGUAY

## Legal, regulatory, and institutional framework

Uruguay’s Political Constitution (1967) states all persons are equal before the law. The country is a signatory of several international women protection agreements like the Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) (1981), and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995). In 2006, the National Women’s Institute (Instituto MIES) was created to promote policies of equality in women’s rights. The country also has the National Coordinating Council for Public Policies on Gender Equality (2007) and a National Plan for Equal Opportunities and Rights (2007-2011) to mainstream the gender approach in the State.

## Law/Document | Content

| Labor Activity (Law 16,045, 1989) | Prohibits all discrimination in the areas of calls for provision of charges; Selection criteria; recruitment and hiring; performance evaluation criteria; right to promotion and advancement; access to training; stable social benefits; suspension and dismissal; possibilities for professional and technical transfer or reversion; training and updating; and remuneration criteria. |
| --- | --- |
| Domestic work (Law 18065, 2006) | Regulate domestic workers rights and the labor relationship. |
| Law on Equal Rights and Opportunities between Men and Women (2007). | The State must adopt measures to guarantee the design, follow-up and evaluation of public policies to integrate the gender perspective. |
| Contraception law (Law 18246, 2008) | Establishes rights and obligations for concubine union. |
| Sexual and reproductive health (Law 18375, 2008) | To promote responsible sexual and reproductive health, as well as responsible motherhood and fatherhood. |
| Quota law (Law 18476, 2009) | To integrate Uruguayan women in political life at the legislative chambers. |
| Sexual harassment (Law 18561, 2009) | To prevent sexual and sexual harassment in the workplace and in teacher-student relationships. |
| Gender identity (Law 18420, 2009) | Recognizes the right to gender identity and allows to modify name and sex recorded in the registers of the documents. |
| Voluntary interruption of pregnancy (Law 18587, 2012) | Decriminalization of abortion. |
| Parental leave (Law 19162, 2013) | Maternity and paternity right and subsidy, and subsidy for the newborn care. |

# Educational gaps

Uruguay has made considerable progress in closing gender gaps in education. According to Bathyany et al., (2016), Uruguayan women record higher educational achievements at all educational levels (primary, secondary and higher education), measured by the completion of education. The gross enrollment ratio for primary education was 98% for both female population and 96.9% for male. While the completion rate for lower secondary education was 93.8% for women and for men it is 4%. The net primary enrollment rate for 2017 was 98% for both genders, but in secondary enrollment, there were differences, since it is higher for women (96%) than for men (95%).

Women represent over 60% of the total number of students who complete the secondary cycle and enter the tertiary cycle. By 2018 the gross attendance ratio for tertiary education is higher for women (103.5%) than men (79.4%) attend. And the female share of graduates from STEM is 45%. Yet, there are less women than man in basic and technological careers at the tertiary level.

# Economic Opportunities

Labor force participation rate in 2018 for persons over 15+ are, 56% for women 56% and 74% for men 74%. This difference persists for youth people aged 15-24, with 42% for female population and 50% for male. The rate of unemployment rates since is higher for women aged 15+ than 18% and 6% for men. Employment distribution by sector also varies. There has also some gender differences: 87% of women participate mostly in service-related activities, while the 59% of men work in this area. Employment in industry have a higher participation of men. The informal economy is dominated by women (40%) and the agricultural sector concentrates 13% of the male work force and 9% of female. This sector has the lower employment occupation%. The proportion of time spent on unpaid domestic chores and care work is unevenly distributed. Women dedicate 25% of their time for these activities, and men 8-10%.


---
**Page 191**
---

Although women participation in the labor force participation grew 39% between 2009 and 2019160, important salary gaps persist. Women earn 25% less than men161. One of the factors that explains these gaps being the high level of occupational segregation: in construction, women are underrepresented in higher productivity jobs (they account for only 3% of employed persons)162, while they are overrepresented in lower productivity jobs163. Likewise, analyzes carried out by the UNDP [2014] show that the levels of occupational segregation are lower among the more qualified population.

Regrettably women participation in road infrastructure construction, only 6% of the positions are held by them. Usually, the kind of positions occupied by women are in administration, technical and cleaning areas. Few of them work in operational activities. In this country, the transport sector is perceived as masculine, causing women entrance to the sector limited by stereotypes and cultural barriers. Plus, a lack of infrastructure for women, like differentiated bathrooms, and the lack of flexibility of the sector with long working hours and locations away from home, discourage woman participation.

Access to Financial Resources

More men (68%) than women (61%) have a financial institutional account, which constitute an important asset for being part of the formal financing system and accessing to loans, having savings, or paying bills. Women are less likely to have access to credit facilities by borrowing to a financial institution, since more women (19%) than men (17%) accessed to it in the last 12 months of 2017. The panorama is similar regarding the ability to raise funds from a bank, employer, or a lender. The 18% of women aged 15+ might access to these funds, compared to the 34% of men.

Gender Based Violence

In Uruguay, 76.7% of 15-year-old women and more, report have experienced gender-based violence throughout life. The 54.8% of these women face violence in public spaces along their life. This kind of experiences limit the free of movement of women in places like streets.

transport, bars, parks, sport clubs, dancing clubs, church, health services and virtual spaces. It has impact on women and girls' possibilities to be part of public life, work, study, leisure activities and access to services.

Uruguay is one of the countries in Ibero-America with the highest incidence of women killed by their partners or ex-partners (12.2 / 100 thousand in 2019; 22 women). The number of femicides has increased from 18 to 30 murders between 2014 and 2018, followed by a slight decrease in 2019[16].

Regarding the proportion of ever-partnered women and girls subjected to physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner in the previous 12 months, aged 15-49, the 6.3% of women have faced this situation. On the other hand, the proportion of women aged 20-24 year who were married or in a union before age 18, is 26.6%.

### Programmatic Approach of the GAP

Taking into consideration the main gender gaps identified at the sector level and each country assessment, a Gender Action Plan (GAP) was developed to address these gaps. The GAP's objective is to promote gender equality in the electromobility sector and support countries in closing gender gaps.

To address the main gender gaps identified, the GAP has sectoral level activities that focus on capacity building, knowledge development and dissemination and network building. Moreover, the GAP also has country level activities that focus on context specific actions developed during the design phase of each project.


| Level | Activity |
| --- | --- |
| Sectoral | Develop a training program for women that allow them to acquire the necessary technical skills to work in the electromobility sector |
|  | Include gender specific procurement requirements in the project eligibility criteria |
|  | Develop and implement a gender module to include in any capacity building activity in the Regional Sectorial Council of Skills for Electromobility or any activity training in the program |

The GAP will be implemented by the 108 gender experts in the transportation, energy, and climate change divisions (one expert per division, in total 3 experts). The consultants will: (i) support the implementation of the Sectoral Actions by identifying firms or consultants to conduct them and supervising their work; and (ii) support the EE during the project design and implementation phases. Depending on the EE's needs, the consultant may have different roles during project design. Their role could be more supervisory (review documents and TORs) or it could be more hands on. However, no matter the EA competency, the IDB gender experts will be involved in the project to make sure that the project complies with the Bank's requirements for gender alignment.

# Gender Action Plan

## E-Mobility Program for Sustainable Cities in Latin America and the Caribbean

Data availability is a challenge shared by the nine countries. The Gender Assessments by country were built with available data, but position levels in the transport sector per country; mobility data; violence against women in the public transportation; sex-disaggregated data in electromobility. The opportunity identified is to fill the data gaps and produce new and gender-aware data as a new resource for policy and decision-makers.

All projects (concessional loans and grants for pilot projects) financed by the Program will conduct a detailed and context specific gender gap analysis. This gender analysis will be more detailed than the country assessments, it will include smaller groups (e.g., age groups) and quantitative data (census data, demographics, income, etc.). Additionally, these analyses will be conducted in the project interest areas. The gender gaps might vary depending on the type of project and will expand on the preliminary information from the country assessments. Based on this analysis, actions to define actions to close the gender gaps identified and project indicators to measure the progress.

The IDB has gender experts in the transportation, energy and climate change divisions (one expert per division, in total 3 experts) who will support the EE during the project design and implementation phases. Depending on the EE complexity the IDB gender expert will have different roles: in the project design phase it could be more hands-on (e.g., provide documents and TORs) or it could be more hands-off. However, no matter the EE complexity, the IDB gender expert will be involved in the project to make sure that the project complies with the Bank's requirements for gender alignment.
